Course map

Every module and what each section covers. Open a module to see its sections; open a section to read what it is about and jump straight to it.

W1Business domain & where VibeIQ sits

The foundation. What the platform is for, whose calendar it runs on, and the positioning you have to get right before anything technical makes sense.

Open the module →

🤝1. The domain: seasonal product creation

The seasonal GTM calendar from trend through production, and the merchant vocabulary customers use constantly — line, assortment, style, colourway, SKU, carryover, OTB, sell-in. Learn these cold; every later conversation assumes them.

Go to this section →

🤝2. The problem VibeIQ exists to solve

Why line planning is painful: decisions get made months ahead across disconnected spreadsheets, decks and mood boards, and the reasoning behind them is lost by the time products reach development — when mistakes are expensive.

Go to this section →

🤝3. Where VibeIQ sits

The keystone of the module. VibeIQ sits upstream of PLM and alongside PLM/ERP/PIM/DAM rather than replacing them. Both words do work: one sets data direction, the other defuses the overlap objection.

Go to this section →

🤝4. Who the users are

The five teams — merchandisers, designers, planners, product development, regional — and what each cares about. The operational point: adoption is never monolithic, so knowing which team lags tells you where account health is actually failing.

Go to this section →

🤝5. Contrail vs. the marketing brand

Contrail is the engineering sub-brand behind the docs and the @contrail/* packages; VibeIQ is the commercial brand. Also the caution on AI claims: heavily marketed, thinly documented — confirm GA vs. roadmap before repeating any of it.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

What to read this week and, just as important, what only to skim. Orient to the docs structure rather than studying it.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Two ~60-second elevator pitches, one for a VP of Merchandising and one for an integration engineer — without using "PLM" as a synonym for VibeIQ.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic's VP of Product, six weeks after a Centric PLM rollout: "Isn't this overlap?" Your response shape plus one discovery question that can honestly come back "no".

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Eight questions on positioning, the GTM calendar, vocabulary, the user teams and the AI caution. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Build the customer-landscape discovery template — 8–12 questions across existing systems, data ownership, seasonal cadence and downstream handoff. First artefact of the account-inheritance kit; Week 10 reuses it.

Go to this section →

W2Core data model

The keystone module. Almost every escalation you will ever handle resolves to something on this page — which entity, which property level, which federatedId.

Open the module →

⚙️1. The three item layers

Item, ProjectItem and AssortmentItem. Not three copies: an Item is the product, and the other two are relationship entities carrying data about that product in a season or an assortment. The most important structural idea in the platform.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. Property levels: Family, Option, All, Override

Family, Option, All and Override, and how each propagates through the Family → Option relationship. Maps directly onto Week 1's style → colourway. Family copies down and wins; Override copies down but can be beaten; All has no copy-down at all.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. federatedId, and the duplicate-records bug

The ID you supply that drives upsert — create if absent, update if present. Entities created by users in the UI have none by default, which is the root cause of the platform's most common integration bug: duplicates of everything on the first Loader run.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. Projects, Assortments and their owners

Projects as the season-scale container, assortments as curated selections, and which load type creates a Plan, Board or Showcase alongside. Includes the default that bites: assortment membership is replaced, not merged.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Where this is heading: the publish lifecycle

A deliberate preview of Week 3, because it is what the data model is for. Introduces AssortmentPublishChange and two facts worth memorising now: deletes do not accumulate across publishes, and S3 links expire in 24 hours.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

The four doc pages this module rests on — Property Type Handling being, for your role, the single most important page in the documentation.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Take one hoodie in 4 colourways and 6 sizes, in two assortments at different price points, and place 10–15 properties on the right layer at the right property level — with a reason for each.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic's copy-down clobber: 30 outlet colourway prices reverted overnight, nobody touched them, leadership presentation on Thursday. Hypothesis, the two checks that confirm or kill it, and what you say before you know.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Eleven questions on the three layers, property levels, federatedId semantics and assortment structure. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Draft the data-model health check — the 6–8 questions to ask on inheriting any account, aimed at this module's failure modes. Artefact two of the kit.

Go to this section →

W3End-user apps & the publish lifecycle

What your users actually touch, and what happens the moment one of them hits publish. The seam where "it works in VibeIQ but not in PLM" lives.

Open the module →

🤝1. The three apps

Board, Plan and Showcase — replacing Miro, Excel and PowerPoint respectively, and each backed by an Assortment. The technically important detail: in Plan, each row is an actual product record, not a spreadsheet cell.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. The platform underneath

The four platform layers beneath the apps — product data, the agent layer, the event and workflow engine, and the integration/app platform. Carries forward the Week 1 caution about which agent capabilities are actually verified.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. The publish lifecycle, end to end

The six steps from a user editing Plan rows to an external system processing the change. The step people skip is the internal commit — Plan rows persisting down to the Assortment — and skipping it causes bad diagnosis.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. Anatomy of an AssortmentPublishChange

The three parts: the baseline link (full state now), the delete link (this publish only), and the detail arrays. Most real consumers need both baseline and detail — one for day-to-day sync, one for recovery.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Two ways to consume it

Event-driven push versus change-history polling, compared on trigger, latency and complexity. The shape only; Week 5 goes deep. Notes that polling is temporal filtering, not cursors — which is where the bug lives.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Four pages, with Assortment Publish Integrations flagged to read properly rather than skim — it is the spine of the module.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Twenty lines of pseudocode for a well-behaved publish consumer that survives 48 hours of downtime and recovers without human help — plus the two lines a naive implementation gets wrong.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

Forty styles cut in VibeIQ still active in Centric, with sourcing already costing two of them. Four checkpoints in order, the leading hypothesis, and why "who's wrong?" is the wrong question.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Ten questions on the apps, the lifecycle, the change object's fields and missed-publish recovery. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Write the publish integration review checklist — delete handling, missed-publish recovery, link expiry, reconciliation cadence, and who gets alerted. Artefact three.

Go to this section →

W4Data in: the Loader framework

With Week 5, the technical heart. Where customer data becomes VibeIQ entities — and where a large share of your escalations will originate.

Open the module →

⚙️1. A load is an entity, not a command

A LoaderProcess is an inspectable entity, not a fire-and-forget command. Two fields make it a diagnostic instrument: failureArea, which localises a failure to one of four segments, and preprocessingStepResults, one artefact per step.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. The five phases

CSV parsing, validation, preprocessing, entity construction, API upsert — and which failureArea value each phase produces. Learn the mapping and a failed load stops being a mystery.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. Validation: fail-fast, and no partial loads

Validation is fail-fast and there are no partial loads — you never get half a file applied. Most people assume the opposite, and the assumption changes how a recovery is planned. Errors halt; warnings accumulate and continue.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. The six preprocessing steps

Boolean conversion, federated mappings, property removal, type transforms without warnings, conditional columns, then type transforms with warnings. Type transformations run twice on purpose — and a value altered in step 4 then overwritten in step 5 warns nowhere.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. The configuration, for real

A complete, real load configuration JSON read field by field, rather than a field list in the abstract.

Go to this section →

⚙️6. How Family and Option are expressed in a CSV

Week 2's Family/Option made concrete in a CSV: rows sharing an itemFamilyFederatedId become options of one family, and the loader creates the parent automatically. Simpler than most people expect.

Go to this section →

⚙️7. The four flags that change what a load is

The four fields that change the nature of the operation, three of them quietly. Includes shouldSkipAssortmentPublish, which makes a successful load completely invisible downstream, and partialAssortmentUpdate, which defaults to replace.

Go to this section →

⚙️8. federatedId, with the mechanics

The mechanics behind Week 2's concept: there is no "which column is my ID?" setting, the value has to arrive under the expected property, and IDs must not change between loads or the Loader creates duplicates.

Go to this section →

⚙️9. Transformers and images

The documented transformer set for heavier reshaping, and why transformer choice is a maintainability question you should have an opinion on — Morph can do anything, which is exactly the problem.

Go to this section →

⚙️10. Getting the file in, and CLI vs. API

The two-call upload, and the CLI/API split. The drift risk worth knowing: the CLI cannot use a stored configuration, so anyone loading from it is passing a local copy that diverges from production.

Go to this section →

⚙️11. What a load does to your workflows

A load is an event storm — 20,000 rows is 20,000 trigger evaluations. Except when it deliberately isn't: one flag silences the publish event while entity workflows still fire.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Six doc pages, with the Loader Configuration Reference flagged as the densest page for this module.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Write a complete load configuration for a real CSV, trace one row through all six preprocessing steps, and name the two steps where something can go wrong with no warning.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

Four symptoms after a load that reported success — blanks, duplicates, three days of silence at FlexPLM, and 1,200 Slack messages at 2am. They are not one root cause. Name the exact artefact that confirms each.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Twelve questions on phases, failure areas, the preprocessing steps, the dangerous flags and a load's workflow side-effects. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Write the pre-load checklist for any first production load or unusually large one — including whether each of the four flags is still deliberate. Artefact four.

Go to this section →

W5Data out: integration patterns

The other half of the technical heart, and the seam customers actually break. By the end you should be able to read an integration design and name what will fail in production.

Open the module →

⚙️1. Choosing the pattern

Polling versus events on trigger, latency, complexity, where state lives and — the row that decides most real architectures and is easiest to miss — scope. Publish events cover assortment publishes only.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. Authentication and the API surface

The base URL, the two required headers, what the app: key prefix signifies, and what each error code actually means. Includes why a 404 here is not proof a record is absent.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. The event-driven path, end to end

The thin two-ID payload, and the fact that matters most: the delivery mechanism is an Event Workflow. So "the event never arrived" is a workflow investigation, and Week 6 already taught you how to run it.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. The polling path, and the seam

Change-history polling, and why temporal filtering is not a cursor. Boundary exclusivity, clock skew and in-flight writes each drop or duplicate records at the seam between polls.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Designing a consumer that survives contact with reality

Weeks 3–5 pulled together into six hazards a production consumer must handle — expiring links, non-accumulating deletes, downtime, duplicates, partial failure and silent stalling — each with its design answer.

Go to this section →

⚙️6. Webhook security, in both directions

Inbound and outbound have genuinely different security postures, and conflating them is how you give a security team a wrong answer. Outbound supports HMAC; inbound documents only an optional shared-secret header.

Go to this section →

🤝7. Attribute ownership, the artefact that prevents the arguments

Two-way integration means every attribute needs an owner, or both systems write the same field and the last writer wins — intermittently. The map that prevents the argument, and what belongs in each row.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Four pages, with Assortment Publish Integrations flagged to read line by line.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Write the attribute ownership map for a brand running VibeIQ, Centric and SAP — fifteen attributes, each with authoritative system, direction, entity layer, property level and conflict rule.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario — an architecture review

A real pre-build architecture review: a nightly polling job with a subtly wrong window, stored S3 links, and no delete handling. Find every flaw, rank them, and write the reply.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Eleven questions on pattern choice, auth, the polling seam, consumer resilience and webhook security in both directions. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Write the integration design review template — the standard questions to put to any customer before they build against VibeIQ. Artefact five.

Go to this section →

W6Event Workflows

The deepest technical module, and the one closest to the day job. When a customer says "the automation didn't run," this is the entire surface you have to reason about.

Open the module →

⚙️1. Anatomy

Triggers, conditionals and actions, declared in an app's app.yml. A trigger says when, conditionals say which branch, actions say what happens.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. The six trigger categories

System events in {entityType}|{action} form, plus scheduled, webhook, manual, app-completion and bulk-change triggers. Note what is not there: assortment offers only publish.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. Paths, and the rule that bites

Paths are an ordered if/else-if chain and only the first matching path runs. Authors routinely write them as independent rules, which silently strands every later path.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. Conditional expressions

JavaScript evaluated against the event payload, with newData, oldData, propertyDiffs and changeObjects available. oldData is absent on creates — the source of the classic create-versus-update bug.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Actions and per-workflow config

Tasks run strictly serially and fail closed: one failed task means every later task in that process never runs — not failed, not retried, simply never executed.

Go to this section →

⚙️6. Concurrency and message grouping

Static serialization versus dynamic grouping, and the concurrency ceilings. This produces the hardest bugs you will see, because they are intermittent and load-dependent — raising throughput without grouping converts a slow workflow into a corrupting one.

Go to this section →

⚙️7. Webhooks

Creating a webhook, linking it to a workflow by ID, and firing it. Note the webhook ID in the URL functions as a bearer credential.

Go to this section →

⚙️8. From app to running workflow

Workflows are versioned artefacts of an app, not settings in a console — so "can you just tweak that automation?" is a release conversation, not a five-minute favour.

Go to this section →

⚙️9. Observability, and the "why didn't it fire?" ladder

The Process List, task outputs, the four statuses, and the ordered ladder for "why didn't it fire?". Also the 90-day retention that caps how far back any investigation can reach.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Four pages, with Creating Workflows flagged to read twice — most of this module comes from it.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Write a complete app.yml workflow block from a plain-English requirement, including the grouping that keeps one family serial while different families run in parallel.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

A copy-down workflow that ran fine for four months, then corrupted 60 styles during a 3,000-item bulk update. A concurrency race — and the symptom shape tells you so before any detail does.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Thirteen questions on anatomy, triggers, first-match-wins, conditionals, serial task execution, concurrency and observability. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Build the workflow review checklist — unreachable paths, create-vs-update behaviour, access grants, environment config, grouping, concurrency budget and failure alerting. Artefact six.

Go to this section →

W7Apps, Extensions & the config boundary

How far you can go without code, where the line is, and what crossing it costs. The judgement call you'll be asked for on nearly every account.

Open the module →

🤝1. The spectrum

"Configuration vs. customization" is a false binary — it is a five-rung ladder from types and properties up to external systems, each rung costing more to build, more to change, and involving more people.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. Formulas

Programmatic property values, what inputs each entity type gets, and the three silent failure modes — including the one that produces a value correct today and wrong next season.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. Rule Sets

Controlling whether a property is editable, required, range-limited or option-restricted, based on entity state. Includes OR/AND evaluation and what "locked wins" means. Note: documented as evaluating client-side.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. The rest of the configuration layer

Validation functions, blueprints, uniqueness and option set hierarchies. Includes the hierarchy trap: configs keyed on TypePropertyIds, where a slug-based config silently fails to load.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Configuration Copy, and how environments actually work

How configuration actually moves between orgs, and the answer to a question open since Week 1: an "environment" is just another org. Configuration promotes one way, apps another — a full promotion is two mechanisms.

Go to this section →

⚙️6. Apps: identity, ownership, versions

Three permanent decisions you should be in the room for: the app identifier is global and immutable, and app ownership cannot be transferred. An SI's app can never become the customer's.

Go to this section →

⚙️7. Extensions

The three extension types, how they are declared, and the hosting implication — extensions run in an iframe from someone's public HTTPS host, which is a security and ownership question, not just a technical one.

Go to this section →

🤝8. Holding the line

A practical routing table for "can it do X?" — request shape on the left, the rung to reach for on the right, including the two "wrong system" boundaries from Week 1.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Four areas of the configuration and app-lifecycle docs, with Formulas and Rule Sets to be read properly.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Route seven real customer requests to a rung of the spectrum, each with one sentence on cost of ownership.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

An integrator's design note containing two irreversible mistakes — the app created in their own org, and an extension hosted on their infrastructure. Eight weeks from go-live.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Twelve questions on the spectrum, formulas, rule sets, configuration copy, app identity and extensions. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Write the "can the platform do X?" routing guide — the artefact most likely to be read by someone other than you. Artefact seven.

Go to this section →

W8Platform, security & operations

The conversation with a customer's IT and security teams — and the one where being honest about what you don't know matters more than sounding complete.

Open the module →

⚙️1. Tenancy: the org is the boundary

The org is the unit of isolation, ownership, configuration and capacity. Hold that and most platform questions resolve — including why environments drift.

Go to this section →

⚙️2. The permission model, layer by layer

Five layers from org membership down to a single property. Corrects a common misconception: VibeIQ does have an end-user permission model. What is missing is documentation, not capability.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. Credentials: where they live and how they leak

Three credential surfaces with different exposures — API keys, CLI credentials cached to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on every laptop that ever ran it, and webhook IDs. The CLI cache is an offboarding risk nobody raises.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. Where the data actually goes

Uploads to S3, publish payloads on expiring links, and extension iframes on customer-hosted infrastructure — the honest answer to "where does our data travel?"

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Runtime and platform upgrades

Node runtime declarations and migration obligations — a recurring, schedulable duty customers reliably forget, because the app still works right up until it doesn't.

Go to this section →

⚙️6. What you can actually see

The real monitoring surface and its horizons — chiefly that workflow evidence lives 90 days. Check the horizon before promising root cause on an old problem.

Go to this section →

⚙️7. Capacity as a shared, invisible resource

The org's 60 concurrent processes are a budget shared by every integration, workflow and bulk load — with no per-team allocation and no documented way for a customer to see their own utilisation.

Go to this section →

🤝8. Answering what you don't know

The standing list of six things a security team will ask that public documentation does not settle, and how to take them away without bluffing or stalling.

Go to this section →

📚Recommended reading

Four pages covering key hygiene, the configurable/governable surface, monitoring, and the shape of a runtime obligation.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Write the platform section of an implementation runbook — org topology, credential custody and rotation, data paths, monitoring ownership, runtime obligations and the concurrency budget.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario

A pre-contract security questionnaire with the VP copied and a 48-hour deadline, containing questions you can answer, questions you must qualify, and questions you have to take away.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Twelve questions on tenancy, permission layers, credentials, data paths, monitoring horizons and capacity. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Build the security questionnaire response kit, split into answer confidently, answer with qualification, and must escalate. Artefact eight.

Go to this section →

W9Troubleshooting & escalation

Everything from Weeks 2–8 turned into one repeatable method. Almost no new facts — the value is entirely in sequence and discipline.

Open the module →

⚙️1. Localise before theorising

The single most common failure in technical support is proposing a cause before establishing where the problem is. The first question is which segment of the pipeline, not why.

Go to this section →

🤝2. The universal question set

Six questions that apply to almost any data-correctness report, each eliminating whole classes of cause before you touch anything — entity layer, property level, who wrote it, when, how distributed, what changed.

Go to this section →

⚙️3. The three ladders

The consolidated ladders for a bad load, a workflow that didn't fire, and a downstream sync that didn't arrive. Learn them as three routes, not as scattered notes from Weeks 3–6.

Go to this section →

⚙️4. Symptom shape tells you the class of cause

Before content, look at distribution. How a problem spreads across records and time narrows the cause faster than any detail about the records — intermittent and load-correlated means concurrency, consistent and silent means configuration.

Go to this section →

⚙️5. Read evidence, don't reason from descriptions

Anchor every claim to a named artefact with a known lifetime — LoaderProcess and its step files, the Process List, the workflows dashboard. Descriptions are what people remember; evidence is what happened.

Go to this section →

🤝6. Escalating well

Escalation is a handover, not an admission. What a good one contains — the segment, the identifiers, what you ruled out and how — and why that determines whether the next person starts where you stopped or starts again.

Go to this section →

🤝7. Holding the customer while it's open

The other half of the job: separating what you know from what you suspect, explicitly and every time, for a customer who is anxious and accountable to someone else.

Go to this section →

✅Practical exercise

Write the one-page triage card from memory, then test it against three earlier scenarios and confirm it would have routed each correctly.

Go to this section →

✅Realistic customer scenario — a live incident

A live incident: the VP calls you directly, three symptoms, and a line review at 14:00. Localise, sequence, and decide what to say at each check-in.

Go to this section →

✅Quiz

Eleven questions on the localisation rule, the checkpoints, the ladders, symptom shape, evidence horizons and escalation quality. Model answers behind a toggle.

Go to this section →

✅Hands-on challenge

Assemble the full account-inheritance kit — all eight artefacts in one document, with a note on when each gets used.

Go to this section →

Discovery, architecture review, incident and QBR — what you are really doing in each, and the distinct way each one fails.

The generalised review method: establish the requirement first, trace one record end to end out loud, test the unhappy path. Most flaws surface without any need for cleverness.

Reporting risk rather than usage statistics nobody acts on. The three questions a QBR must answer, and which signal answers each.

Meridian Outdoor, run end to end across four phases — discovery, architecture review, incident and QBR — where later phases are shaped by choices you made in earlier ones. Work them in order and don't read ahead.

TAMBuilding the TAM practice

Not a curriculum week — how the job is run. Written because the TAM role isn't defined at VibeIQ yet, which makes the definition itself the first deliverable.

Open the page →

🤝1 · Charter: what the role owns

An undefined role is defined by whoever asks first. A written statement of what the job owns, so that every later escalation isn't a negotiation about whose problem it is. The boundary table is a proposal pending internal confirmation.

Named, repeatable deliverables rather than availability — each already backed by an artefact from Weeks 1–10, so "that is a design review, here is what it involves" replaces absorbing an unbounded ask.

Daily, weekly, monthly and quarterly rhythms, and what each is for. A standing slot means bad news arrives early and small.

Five fixed intake fields, so informal requests become a visible queue you can defend rather than a private mental list the customer cannot see.

Expansion signals a TAM sees months before anyone with a CRM does, and how to pass them on as qualified signal with technical context attached. Noticing, not selling.

Where each number actually comes from, and its caveat. Where a signal has no source, that is a finding rather than a gap to paper over.

Four tiers — outcome, practice, leading, and anti-metrics. A newly defined role gets measured on whatever is easiest to count, so naming the wrong metrics is part of the proposal. Written to be sent upward.

Module 1 · Week 1

Business domain & where VibeIQ sits

The foundation week. Light on mechanics by design — you can't reason about integrations until you understand what the platform is for, whose calendar it runs on, and what problem it solves for whom.

On this page

  1. 🤝The domain: seasonal product creation
  2. 🤝The problem VibeIQ exists to solve
  3. 🤝Where VibeIQ sits
  4. 🤝Who the users are
  5. 🤝Contrail vs. the marketing brand
  6. 📚Recommended reading
  7. ✅Practical exercise
  8. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  9. ✅Quiz
  10. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) describe the seasonal go-to-market process at the level a merchandiser would recognize, using their vocabulary, (2) explain in one minute what VibeIQ does and why it is not a PLM, (3) draw a customer's system landscape and say which system owns which attribute, and (4) navigate the "Contrail vs. marketing brand" distinction.

Concept 1 — The domain: seasonal product creation

VibeIQ's customers are brands and retailers that create seasonal, visual, assortment-driven product — apparel, footwear, accessories, home, hardlines, toys, beauty. The defining trait is not "manufacturing" but seasonality plus visual merchandising: a new line, several times a year, decided largely by looking at pictures of products next to each other.

The seasonal calendar — the spine of everything

A brand runs a GTM (go-to-market) calendar per season. Every conversation you have with a customer is anchored somewhere on it, so learn its shape first:

StageWhat happensWho leadsWhose system
Trend / conceptResearch, inspiration, mood boards, colour directionDesign, trendVibeIQ
Line planningHow many styles, in which categories, at what price points, hitting what marginMerchandising / planningVibeIQ
DesignSketches, colourways, materialsDesignVibeIQ
Line review / milestone meetingsAssortment is presented, edited live, cut or keptMerch + leadershipVibeIQ
Development & sourcingTech packs, BOMs, costing, vendors, samplesProduct dev, sourcingPLM
Sell-in / marketAssortment shown to wholesale buyers or internal channelsSalesVibeIQ
Production & launchPOs, inventory, ecommerce/retail launchSupply chain, ecomERP

VibeIQ lives in the top half — concept through line review, and again at sell-in. The bottom half (tech packs, BOM, costing, POs) belongs to PLM and ERP.

Key vocabulary — learn these cold

Customers use these constantly and will not stop to define them. Not knowing them is the fastest way to sound external in a room of merchants.

TermWhat it means
LineThe full set of products a brand offers for a season.
AssortmentA curated selection of products for a purpose: a channel, region, customer, store cluster, or catalogue. One line → many assortments.
SeasonSS26, FW26, Holiday 26. The planning container.
Style / colourway / SKUStyle = the design (a hoodie); colourway = that style in a colour; SKU = that colourway in a size. Volume explodes at each level — and this maps directly onto VibeIQ's property-level model in Week 2.
Carryover vs. newA style repeated from last season vs. one newly created.
Line reviewThe recurring meeting where the assortment is reviewed and cut. The single most important moment VibeIQ has to make good.
OTB / open-to-buyThe budget available to buy inventory; constrains the assortment.
Margin / IMUInitial mark-up — target profitability. Drives keep/cut decisions.
Sell-in vs. sell-throughSelling to the buyer vs. the consumer actually buying it.
Drop / deliveryA phased release within a season.

The style → colourway → SKU hierarchy is worth over-learning now. It is the mental model behind VibeIQ's property levels (Week 2), and property levels are the single concept most likely to be at the root of a "the data is wrong" escalation.

Concept 2 — The problem VibeIQ exists to solve

A consumer brand decides months in advance what products it will make for a season. Deciding which products, in which colors, at which price points, hitting which margin targets, for which regions — that's line planning. It happens before the expensive work of development and sourcing.

The problem: brands run separate systems for design, financial planning, product development, and selling, and many decisions about which products move forward still happen across disconnected documents and workflows. Teams lose the context behind key decisions once products enter development — nobody remembers why a product was chosen, dropped, or modified, and mistakes surface late when they're expensive to fix.

VibeIQ gives merchandising, design, planning, and product-development teams one live view of the product line connecting creative intent with commercial context, so they decide what moves forward before unnecessary cost, complexity, and margin risk build downstream. The claimed payoff: fewer planned SKUs, decisions visible months earlier, and thousands of manual hours eliminated.

Why customers buy it, in the order they'll usually say it:

  1. Cycle time — collapse the mood-board → spreadsheet → deck relay into one live workspace; line reviews stop needing a week of deck-building.
  2. One version of the truth in the decision phase — no more "which spreadsheet is current?"
  3. Better decisions — margin, cost and sell-through data sitting next to the image at the moment of the cut.
  4. Less manual work — no re-keying between Excel, PowerPoint and PLM.
  5. AI leverage — the platform is built so agents can act on the product data, rather than being bolted on.

The value you protect is earlier, better-informed product decisions with the reasoning preserved. When usage slips, the real questions are: "Are they deciding in VibeIQ, or drifting back to spreadsheets?" and "Is the context surviving the handoff downstream?"

Concept 3 — Where VibeIQ sits

Keystone

VibeIQ sits upstream of PLM, in the creative-to-commercial gap where products are still being decided rather than developed. It sits alongside PLM / ERP / PIM / DAM rather than replacing them, and integrates bi-directionally with all of them.

The PLM distinction, concrete:

  • PLM (e.g. Centric, PTC FlexPLM, Bamboo Rose) manages a product through development and production: tech packs, BOMs, sourcing, supplier collaboration, costing. The system of record for "how do we make and produce this."
  • VibeIQ manages the decision phase before all that: what should the line even be? The system of record for "what are we preparing to sell, and why."

Typical flow: decisions made in VibeIQ → approved products published to the PLM → PLM drives development/sourcing → ERP handles finance and production execution.

The full system landscape — what else is in the room

PLM and ERP are only the two loudest neighbours. You will be expected to draw all of this on a whiteboard:

SystemOwnsExample vendors
PLMProduct development record: tech packs, BOM, materials, costing, samples, complianceCentric, PTC FlexPLM, Bamboo Rose, Backbone
ERPTransactional truth: POs, inventory, finance, ordersSAP, NetSuite, Oracle, Dynamics
PIMCommerce-ready published product attributes and copyAkeneo, Salsify, inRiver
DAMMaster digital assets: images, video, 3DBynder, Aprimo, Adobe AEM Assets
Merch / assortment planningFinancial plans, OTB, store clusteringo9, Blue Yonder, RELEX, Anaplan
Creative toolsDesign source filesAdobe CC, CLO / Browzwear (3D)

What VibeIQ actually displaces

Not any system in that table — the tools it replaces are Miro, Excel and PowerPoint. That is VibeIQ's own framing: Board replaces Miro, Plan replaces Excel, Showcase replaces PowerPoint. The real incumbent in line planning is a spreadsheet with images pasted into it, emailed around.

This is usually your best answer to "we already have PLM, why do we need this?" — because it names the thing the customer is actually doing today, rather than arguing abstractly about categories. PLM is a system of record for how a product is made; it was never designed for a merchant and a designer to sit in a room and cut 400 styles down to 250 while looking at images. That decision-making layer is what VibeIQ owns.

The three end-user apps (system of engagement)

  • Board — visual workspace: trend research, mood boards, creative direction.
  • Plan — structured workspace: line planning and product definition (the grid/spreadsheet replacement, but image-first and live).
  • Showcase — presentation workspace: line reviews and stakeholder alignment.

The platform underneath (system of record + automation)

  • Product Data Platform — products, assortments, assets. (Week 2: the core data model.)
  • Agent layer — native agents, custom agent authoring, prompt templates.
  • Event & workflow engine — triggers and automation. (Week 6.)
  • Integration & app platform — connectors, APIs, MCP server, App Marketplace. (Weeks 4–6.)

"Upstream" sets the data direction, and it cuts both ways. VibeIQ is often where a product record is born (a concept, a placeholder, "we need 4 more knits at $79") before it is real enough for PLM. Later, PLM and ERP become authoritative for costs, materials and inventory, which flow back into VibeIQ to inform decisions. So most customers run a two-way integration with a clear attribute-level ownership map — and getting that map right is core TAM work. Nearly every escalation you'll see ("the data is wrong in Plan", "publish didn't reach PLM") is really a question about that map.

"Alongside" also constrains scope. If a customer asks VibeIQ to do BOM costing rollups or PO management, that's a "wrong system" conversation — not a feature request. Your hardest technical conversations are almost never about VibeIQ alone; they're about the seam between VibeIQ and the PLM/ERP: what data flows across it, which direction, when, and what breaks. That's why Weeks 4 & 5 are the technical heart of the program.

Concept 4 — Who the users are

TeamOwnsCares about
Merchandisers / line plannersThe assortment — mix, price points, margin targetsShape of the line, hitting commercial goals. Primary daily users.
DesignersCreative intent — look, colorways, materialsCreative context not being lost as products move forward.
Planners (financial/merch)The numbers — volumes, margins, budgetsCommercial targets connected to actual products.
Product developmentThe bridge to PLMHandoff being complete and accurate.
Regional teamsRegional adoptionWhich products work for which markets.

"Adoption" is never monolithic. Merchandisers can be all-in while design still works elsewhere — meaning creative context enters VibeIQ late or not at all. That's a real, diagnosable health problem. Know which team is the weak link.

Concept 5 — Contrail vs. the marketing brand

The platform has two voices for the same thing:

  • Marketing / product voice (vibeiq.com): "AI-native product decision platform," role-based AI agents, concept generation, line-plan automation. Reflects a real 2026 repositioning; VibeIQ raised $22.5M (led by Volition Capital, Aug 2026) to push this direction.
  • Technical / engineering voice (docs.vibeiq.com): the platform is documented as Contrail — a configurable, extensible product workspace tool. SDK, CLI, and npm packages are all @contrail/*.

You'll code-switch constantly: AI-decision-platform language with a VP of Merchandising, Contrail/entity/workflow language with an integration engineer. When "Contrail" shows up in Slack, it just means "the platform internals."

Caution — don't overpromise AI

AI capabilities are heavily marketed but thinly documented publicly. Until you confirm internally what's GA vs. beta vs. roadmap, don't promise AI functionality you can't substantiate. Overpromising on AI is the fastest way for a new TAM to create a trust problem.

Recommended reading

Primary (official):

  • vibeiq.com/platform — the "products, assortments, assets" core framing and the AI-as-operating-model narrative.
  • docs.vibeiq.com — just orient to the structure (Data Loading, Apps, Extensions, Workflows, Configuration, Integration Patterns). Don't study yet; see the map.

Context (external, not official docs): any August 2026 funding announcement for current positioning and named customers (New Balance, L.L. Bean, Vera Bradley, Converse, Kizik, Malbon).

Background: if line planning is new, a general primer on "merchandise assortment planning" — industry context, not VibeIQ-specific.

Practical exercise

Write two ~60-second (~130-word) elevator pitches:

  1. For a VP of Merchandising (non-technical, business outcomes).
  2. For an integration engineer (technical, how it fits their stack).

Constraint: neither may use "PLM" as a synonym for VibeIQ, and each must correctly convey the "upstream, sits alongside" positioning.

The answer

1 · For a VP of Merchandising (128 words)

Every season your team decides what to make — how many styles, in which categories, at what price points, hitting what margin. That decision gets made across a spreadsheet with images pasted in, a deck rebuilt before every line review, and a mood board nobody can find in March. VibeIQ gives that work one live place. Your merchants, designers and planners look at the same line, with cost and margin sitting next to the picture, and they cut it in the room instead of a week later. Line reviews stop needing a week of deck-building. And when the line is set, the decisions — with the reasoning attached — carry forward into PLM instead of being re-keyed. It doesn't replace what you have downstream. It replaces the spreadsheet.

2 · For an integration engineer (131 words)

VibeIQ is where the product record gets born, before it's real enough for PLM — concept, placeholder, "four more knits at $79." So it sits upstream of your PLM and alongside it, not on top of it. Practically: you'll feed it from your systems, and it publishes approved assortments back out. Inbound is a CSV loader keyed on a federatedId you control, which drives upsert. Outbound is event-driven — an assortment publish fires an event you can consume by webhook, or you poll change history if you'd rather own the schedule. The platform underneath is called Contrail; docs and SDKs are all @contrail/*. The design question isn't protocol, it's ownership: which system is authoritative for each attribute. Cost and materials usually stay yours and flow back in.
How to think about it

The two pitches answer different questions. The VP is asking "what changes for my team?" — so lead with the line review, the moment VibeIQ has to make good, and with cycle time and re-keying. The engineer is asking "what will this do to my stack?" — so lead with the data direction, the integration seam and who owns which attribute. Same positioning, different entry point. Neither pitch is a feature list.

Why "upstream of, and alongside" has to survive both. Those two words do separate jobs. Upstream sets the data direction and tells the engineer where records originate. Alongside defuses the objection the VP is already forming — "we just bought PLM." Drop either and the pitch breaks: upstream alone sounds like a replacement, alongside alone sounds like a redundant extra system.

The trap the constraint exists to block. The easy way to explain VibeIQ is by category — "it's like a PLM for the fun part," "a lightweight PLM front end." It lands, which is why it's tempting, and it's wrong in a way that costs you later: it invites a scope conversation about BOMs, costing rollups and POs that VibeIQ will lose, and it makes the customer's existing PLM investment sound threatened. Say what it does, not what category it resembles.

Name the incumbent. Both pitches land on something concrete being replaced — the spreadsheet, the deck, the mood board. That's VibeIQ's own framing (Board→Miro, Plan→Excel, Showcase→PowerPoint) and it's the strongest move available, because the customer recognises the pain immediately and it reframes the purchase as removing a process rather than adding a system.

Self-check: read each pitch aloud and time it. If it runs past ~60 seconds you've added a feature. If a listener could reasonably reply "so it's a PLM?", the positioning failed regardless of length.

Sources
  • vibeiq.com/platform — the official positioning both pitches are built on: Board/Plan/Showcase replacing Miro/Excel/PowerPoint, the four platform layers, and "connect to PLM, ERP, and any AI tool."
  • docs.vibeiq.com — the Contrail developer surface the engineer pitch points at. Note what it is not: it carries only Data Loading, Apps, Extensions, Workflows, Configuration, Integration Patterns and Release Notes. There is no business-positioning page, which is precisely why this pitch has to be constructed rather than quoted.
  • /integration_patterns — backs the engineer pitch's event-driven vs. polling claim. You'll work it properly in Week 5.
  • External — not official documentation: for general grounding on assortment planning as a discipline, any industry primer on "merchandise assortment planning." Useful context; not a VibeIQ claim, and not something to quote to a customer as fact.

Realistic customer scenario

You're on an intro call with "Summit Athletic," a mid-size footwear brand. Their VP of Product says:

"We already have Centric PLM and just finished rolling it out. I don't understand why we'd need another product system on top of it. Isn't this overlap?"

Sketch your response: the core distinction you'd draw, plus one discovery question to test whether VibeIQ actually fits. Don't write a speech — just the shape of the response and the question.

The answer

The shape of the response — four moves, in order:

  1. Validate, and mean it. "If you've just finished a Centric rollout, you should be sceptical of another product system. I would be." You are six weeks after a painful, expensive project. Anything that sounds like "actually you also need…" makes you the next line item on a budget they're defending.
  2. Draw the boundary once, in their language. Centric is the system of record for how a product gets made — tech packs, BOMs, materials, costing, vendors, samples. It starts being the answer at development and sourcing. The question before that one is different: what should we make at all? How many styles, in which categories, at what price points, hitting what margin. That's the line-planning decision, and it happens months earlier.
  3. Then stop talking about PLM and name the incumbent. "Where does that decision live for you today?" You already know the answer: an Excel file with images pasted in, a PowerPoint rebuilt before every line review, and a Miro board from the concept phase. That is what VibeIQ replaces — not Centric.
  4. Close on the relationship, not the product. "Centric stays exactly where it is. If anything, the cleaner the decision is when it reaches Centric, the better your rollout looks." You are making their PLM investment succeed, not competing with it.

The one discovery question:

"Walk me through your last line review. Who was in the room, what were they looking at, and how long did it take someone to build it?"

Then stay quiet. If the answer is "a merchant spent four days on a deck, we cut 380 styles to 240 in the room, and the reasoning lived in someone's notes" — VibeIQ fits and they've just described the fit themselves, which is worth more than you describing it. If the answer is "we don't really do that, product just goes into Centric when design finishes" — you have a genuine qualification problem, and you need to know that on the intro call rather than in month four.

How to think about it

Don't argue PLM categories. This is the whole move. The instinct is to explain why VibeIQ is not a PLM — and that's a debate about definitions, on the customer's home turf, that you can only draw at best. They just spent a year with a PLM vendor and their definition is fresher than yours. Every minute in that argument reinforces the frame that these two things are comparable.

Naming the incumbent moves the frame. "You're replacing your line-planning spreadsheet" is concrete, immediately recognised, and unarguable — they know that file exists and they know it's painful. It also changes what the purchase is: not a second product system stacked on the one they just rolled out, but the removal of a process everybody in the room already resents. Same product, entirely different objection.

Why the objection is reasonable, not an obstacle. "Isn't this overlap?" is the correct question from someone who has just been through an implementation. Treating it as resistance to be handled is how you lose a technical buyer. Treating it as a fair question you have a real answer to is how you become the person they call later.

The discovery question has to be able to come back "no." A question that can only confirm your pitch isn't discovery, it's a setup, and experienced buyers hear the difference. This one is built to be genuinely diagnostic: it asks about a specific recent event rather than a general process, so you get what actually happened instead of the org chart's version. And a "no" is a good outcome on an intro call — the expensive failure is a customer who buys and then discovers their line review isn't the meeting VibeIQ makes good.

What a weak answer looks like: a feature list ("we do boards, planning grids and presentations"), which invites feature-by-feature comparison against Centric modules; or a capability claim about AI, which you cannot yet substantiate — see the quiz question on that below, and note it is the single fastest way for a new TAM to create an unrecoverable trust problem.

The deeper point. Nearly every escalation you will handle later — "the data is wrong in Plan," "the publish never reached PLM" — is this same boundary question wearing different clothes. Drawing the line cleanly on an intro call is the same skill as drawing it during an incident in Week 9.

Sources
  • vibeiq.com/platform — the "connect to PLM, ERP, and any AI tool" framing, and the Board/Plan/Showcase → Miro/Excel/PowerPoint displacement that move 3 depends on.
  • Concept 3 — Where VibeIQ sits — the two sentences worth memorising, and the reason "alongside" is what defuses this exact objection.
  • /assortment_publish_integration — the mechanism behind "decisions carry forward into PLM." Worth knowing it exists now; you build it in Week 3.
  • External — not official documentation: Centric Software's own product scope (tech packs, BOM, costing, sourcing) is the fairest source for what their PLM claims to own. Read the vendor's positioning rather than asserting a boundary from memory in front of a customer who just bought it.

Quiz

  1. In one sentence, what business problem does VibeIQ primarily solve, and when in the product timeline does it operate?

    Brands decide months ahead which products to make for a season, and those decisions happen across disconnected spreadsheets, decks and mood boards — so the reasoning behind them is lost by the time products reach development. VibeIQ gives merchandising, design and planning one live view of the product line so the line is decided before cost and complexity build downstream. When: the decision phase — concept through line review and sell-in — before development and sourcing.

  2. Walk the seasonal GTM calendar from trend to production. Where does VibeIQ stop and PLM begin, and why is the boundary there?

    Trend/concept → line planning → design → line review → development & sourcing → sell-in → production & launch. VibeIQ owns the top half (concept through line review, and again at sell-in); PLM begins at development & sourcing; ERP handles production. The boundary sits there because that is where the question changes from "what should we sell?" to "how do we make it?" — tech packs, BOMs, costing and vendors are a different system of record, and the expensive work starts there. Everything upstream is reversible; everything downstream costs money to change.

  3. Define style, colourway and SKU, and explain what "one line → many assortments" means.

    Style = the design (a hoodie). Colourway = that style in a colour. SKU = that colourway in a size. Volume explodes at each level, and this maps onto VibeIQ's Family → Option model (Week 2). "One line → many assortments": the line is everything the brand offers for a season; an assortment is a curated selection of it for a purpose — a channel, region, customer or store cluster. The same style appears in several assortments, potentially on different terms.

  4. A customer says "VibeIQ is basically our PLM, right?" — what's wrong with that, and what's the correct relationship?

    It's wrong because PLM is the system of record for how a product is made — tech packs, BOMs, sourcing, costing — and was never designed for a merchant and a designer to cut 400 styles to 250 while looking at images. The correct relationship: VibeIQ sits upstream of, and alongside, PLM. It does not replace it; it carries approved decisions downstream while preserving the reasoning. Decisions in VibeIQ → publish to PLM → PLM drives development → ERP handles production. Integration is typically two-way, with PLM/ERP authoritative for cost and materials flowing back.

  5. Which tools does VibeIQ actually displace, and why is that a stronger answer to the overlap objection than arguing about PLM?

    It displaces Miro, Excel and PowerPoint — Board replaces Miro, Plan replaces Excel, Showcase replaces PowerPoint. It's the stronger answer because it names what the customer is actually doing today: a spreadsheet with images pasted in, emailed around. Arguing PLM categories is abstract and invites debate about definitions; naming the incumbent is concrete and the customer recognises it immediately. It also reframes the purchase as replacing a painful process rather than adding a system on top of one they just rolled out.

  6. Name three user teams VibeIQ is built for, and one thing each cares about.

    Any three of — Merchandisers / line planners: the shape of the line, price points and margin targets (primary daily users). Designers: that creative context isn't lost as products move forward. Planners: volumes, margins and budgets connected to actual products. Product development: a complete, accurate handoff to PLM. Regional teams: which products work in their markets. TAM angle: adoption is never monolithic — knowing which team lags tells you where health is actually failing.

  7. What does "Contrail" refer to, and why might you hear it internally instead of "VibeIQ"?

    Contrail is the engineering/technical sub-brand for the platform — the name on the developer docs (docs.vibeiq.com), with SDK, CLI and npm packages all under @contrail/*. VibeIQ is the commercial brand. You'll hear it internally and from engineers because it refers to the platform internals; you code-switch by audience — AI-decision-platform language with a VP of Merchandising, Contrail/entity/workflow language with an integration engineer.

  8. Why should a new TAM be cautious when a customer asks about VibeIQ's AI agent capabilities?

    Because AI is heavily marketed but thinly documented. Named native agents are a public claim, not a verified capability set, and nothing public tells you what is GA versus beta versus roadmap. Promising AI functionality you can't substantiate is the fastest way for a new TAM to create a trust problem — and it's unrecoverable, because once a customer catches one overstatement they verify everything else you say. Confirm internally first, then speak precisely.

Hands-on challenge

Produce a one-page "customer landscape" discovery template — 8–12 questions to map where VibeIQ sits in any customer's ecosystem, grouped into: existing systems, data ownership, seasonal cadence, downstream handoff. We'll reuse and sharpen it in Week 10.

The answer

Twelve questions, four groups. Each carries the thing that matters most — what the answer changes. A question whose answer changes nothing is a question you drop.

A · Existing systems — what's in the room

  1. "Which systems does a product record touch between first idea and store shelf? Name the vendor for each."
    Changes: your integration surface, and who else is in the room at every future architecture conversation. Insist on vendor names — "our PLM" hides whether it's Centric, FlexPLM or a homegrown database, and those are three different projects.
  2. "Where does line planning physically happen today — what file, opened by whom?"
    Changes: whether there is a real incumbent to displace. The honest answer is usually a spreadsheet and a deck. If it's a PLM module they already paid for, expect a harder internal fight and a different sponsor.
  3. "Which of these went live in the last eighteen months, and how did it go?"
    Changes: your political map and your pace. A brand six weeks out of a painful PLM rollout has no appetite and no budget goodwill, and you will hear "isn't this overlap?" in every meeting.

B · Data ownership — the map that prevents the arguments

  1. "For cost, margin, materials, images and seasonal status — which system is authoritative for each, and who edits it there?"
    Changes: essentially the whole integration design. This is the attribute-ownership map, and getting it right is core TAM work; nearly every later escalation is really a dispute about one row of it.
  2. "What uniquely identifies a style across all these systems? Is that identifier stable if the style is renamed or recategorised?"
    Changes: your federatedId strategy, and whether you are heading for the duplicate-records bug. "Stable" is the word that matters, and the answer is often no — people volunteer an identifier that is really a formatted label.
  3. "Where does a product record get created first — and does anything exist in your systems before it's real enough for PLM?"
    Changes: whether VibeIQ is a source or a sink. If placeholders ("four more knits at $79") have nowhere to live today, that's the strongest fit signal in this template.
  4. "Which attributes must flow back into the decision — cost, sell-through, inventory?"
    Changes: whether this is a one-way publish or a two-way integration. Most customers need two-way, and it is routinely scoped as one-way because nobody asked.

C · Seasonal cadence — the clock everything runs on

  1. "Walk me through your GTM calendar for one season. What are the fixed dates?"
    Changes: your implementation timeline and every go-live conversation. Seasonal deadlines don't move, so the calendar — not the project plan — decides what "late" means.
  2. "How many styles per season, and how many colourways per style? How many assortments does one line become?"
    Changes: your sense of scale, and it's the first place the Family/Option model will bite. "One line → many assortments" is the concept to listen for.
  3. "Tell me about your last line review — who was in the room, what were they looking at, how long did someone spend building it?"
    Changes: whether VibeIQ makes good on the moment that matters. This is the single highest-value question in the template, and it must be able to come back "we don't really do that."

D · Downstream handoff — where decisions go to die

  1. "Once the line is approved, how does it get into PLM today? Who does that, and how long does it take?"
    Changes: your integration priority and your quantified business case. The answer is often a person re-keying for a week, which is both the value story and the first thing to automate.
  2. "When a decision gets reversed downstream, how does anyone find out why it was made in the first place?"
    Changes: whether they feel the pain VibeIQ actually solves. Lost reasoning is the problem the product exists for, and customers rarely name it unprompted — they've stopped noticing it.

How to run it: not as a questionnaire. Twelve questions is roughly a 60-minute conversation if you let people talk, and the ones that pay are 4, 5 and 10 — protect time for those and cut from A if you're running long. Write the answers down verbatim where you can; the phrasing a customer uses for their own identifier scheme is evidence you will want later.

How to think about it

The design rule: every question earns its place by changing a decision. That's why each line above carries a "changes" note. It's the discipline that separates discovery from an interrogation — and it's what lets you cut the list in half when the call runs short, because you can see what you're giving up.

Ask about specific past events, not general process. "Walk me through your last line review" beats "how does line review work?" People describe processes as they are documented and events as they happened, and the gap between those two is where your project risk lives.

Questions 4 and 5 are load-bearing. Attribute ownership and identifier stability are the two answers that shape the most downstream work, and they're the two most likely to be answered confidently and wrongly. Expect to re-ask both later, against an actual data sample, rather than trusting the whiteboard version.

Some answers expire. Anything that changes the integration design is worth far more before the integrator writes their design document than after. That timing pressure is exactly what the Week 10 capstone tests, when it asks which three of your twelve questions lose their value if not asked before implementation starts.

This is artefact one of eight. It's the first of the account-inheritance kit, and it's meant to be used and sharpened, not admired. Take this version, cut what doesn't sound like you, and add what your own accounts teach you — a template you haven't edited is one you won't reach for on a live call.

Sources
  • Concept 3 — Where VibeIQ sits — the system-landscape map (PLM / ERP / PIM / DAM / planning / creative) that group A is built to fill in.
  • /data_loading/getting_started — why question 5 is phrased around stability: federatedId is the value that drives upsert, so a computed or reformatted identifier creates duplicates on the next load. Worked in Week 4.
  • /integration_patterns — the push/poll choice that question 7's answer determines. Week 5.
  • External — not official documentation: named VibeIQ customers and funding coverage are useful for reading a prospect's peer set, but treat them as press, not fact, and don't repeat specifics you haven't confirmed internally.

I know this when I can…

  • Walk the seasonal GTM calendar and use merchant vocabulary without hesitating.
  • Give the 60-second pitch in both registers without conflating VibeIQ with a PLM.
  • Draw the customer's system landscape and say which system owns which attribute.
  • Explain the upstream/alongside positioning to a skeptical VP — and name what VibeIQ actually displaces.
  • Name the user teams and what each values.
  • Decode "Contrail" and hold a grounded (not overpromised) AI conversation.
