Module 2 · Week 2

Core data model

The keystone module. Almost every escalation you will ever handle resolves to something on this page: which entity is the value on, at which property level, and under which federatedId. Slow down here — Weeks 4, 5 and 9 assume it cold.

On this page

  1. ⚙️The three item layers
  2. ⚙️Property levels: Family, Option, All, Override
  3. ⚙️federatedId, and the duplicate-records bug
  4. ⚙️Projects, Assortments and their owners
  5. ⚙️Where this is heading: the publish lifecycle
  6. 📚Recommended reading
  7. ✅Practical exercise
  8. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  9. ✅Quiz
  10. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) explain the Item / ProjectItem / AssortmentItem split and say what lives at each level, (2) choose the right property level for a given attribute and predict what happens on the next load, (3) explain what a federatedId is and diagnose the duplicate-records bug it causes, and (4) describe how Projects, Assortments and the Board/Plan/Showcase apps fit together.

Concept 1 — The three item layers

VibeIQ does not store "a product" once. There is one Item — and then two relationship entities that attach data to it in a particular context. That distinction is sharper than "three copies," and it is how the docs describe it:

  • "A ProjectItem ties an Item to a Project. While Item data is global, ProjectItem data is specific to a project (e.g., a season)."
  • "An Item's membership in an Assortment is represented by an AssortmentItem entity."

So an Item is the product; a ProjectItem is "this product, in this season"; an AssortmentItem is "this product, in this assortment." Each relationship carries its own data. This is the most important structural idea in the platform.

EntityScopeAnswersTypical data
ItemOrg-wide, season-independent"What is this product, ever?"Style number, name, category, base attributes, colour, size range
ProjectItemOne Project (≈ one season)"What is this product for SS26?"Season-specific attributes, that season's target price, lifecycle state
AssortmentItemOne Assortment"Is this product in this assortment, and on what terms?"Placement, assortment-specific overrides, ordering, channel/region terms

The nesting is strict and one-way, and the Loader enforces it — you cannot load the narrow thing without the broad thing:

  • ITEM alone loads Items only.
  • PROJECT_ITEM must be paired with ITEM — loads Item and ProjectItem in the same load.
  • ASSORTMENT must be paired with ITEM, and can also be paired with PROJECT_ITEM — loading all three instances together.

Why the design exists: a style lives across many seasons; its commercial framing differs each season; and the same season's product appears in many assortments on different terms. Flattening these into one record would either lose the history or duplicate the product.

How the levels interact on a write

When a property exists on more than one of these entities, the loader resolves it "based on the property's level designation (Family, Option, All, Override)." So the two ideas in this module — entity layer and property level — are not independent axes you consider one at a time. They compose on every write, which is why a value can land somewhere neither the customer nor the integration expected.

"The data is wrong in Plan" is nearly always a level question, not a corruption question. Ask: which entity is the user looking at, and which entity did the integration write to? A value written to the Item won't show up where a user expects if the app is displaying an AssortmentItem override — and vice versa. Train yourself to ask "at which level?" before you ask anything else.

Concept 2 — Property levels: Family, Option, All, Override

Property levels apply to entity types with hierarchical structure — Item and ProjectItem. They control how a value propagates through the Family → Option relationship. And they map straight onto Week 1's vocabulary:

The bridge from Week 1

Family ≈ style (the hoodie) · Option ≈ colourway (the hoodie in black). If you understood style → colourway → SKU last week, you already have the shape of this.

LevelWhat the docs say it doesUse it for
FamilyUpdates the single Family-level entity, and "those values will be copied down and persisted onto each of that Family's Options."True style-level facts: category, silhouette, description
Option"Will update the single Option-level entity and those values will be persisted only onto that Option entity."Genuinely per-colourway facts: colour code, option image
AllValues are "managed individually for every entity in a Family, regardless if it's a Family or Option level entity." Critically — "No copy down behavior exists for these properties."Values set explicitly at every node, with no inheritance at all
OverrideLike Family, the value "will copy down to all the Options' properties" — but "you can override the value on the Option level by specifying a different value."Defaults that usually hold but sometimes don't: price, margin target

In a CSV, column suffixes target the level: somePropertyFamily, somePropertyOption.

The three failure modes to recognise on sight

  1. Wrong level chosen at config time. Price modelled as Family instead of Override, so a single premium colourway can't carry its own price. Presents as "the system won't let us do this."
  2. Copy-down clobber. A Family property is reloaded and overwrites option-level edits users made by hand. Presents as "our data reverted overnight." This is the classic one.
  3. All vs Override confusion. The team expects inheritance, gets none, and every option comes up blank. Presents as "the load didn't work."

Property level is a configuration decision made early that is expensive to reverse, because changing it rewrites how existing values propagate. When you inherit an account, find out which properties are Family, which are Override, and which are All. That map predicts their future tickets better than any other single thing you could ask for.

Concept 3 — federatedId, and the duplicate-records bug

Definition

A federatedId is "a value you provide which uniquely identifies the entity within an org." It drives upsert: "If an entity with that federatedId does not exist in VibeIQ, it will be created. If it does exist, it will be updated."

For Custom Entities, "if no property is specified as the federatedId, the out of the box name property will be used."

The trap, stated plainly in the docs

Entities created through the core applications — i.e. by users clicking around in the UI — do not have a federatedId by default. If you later want the Loader to update those records, someone has to assign one first.

This is the root cause of the most common integration bug in the platform: duplicates. A customer pilots by hand-creating products in the UI, then wires up a Loader feed keyed on style number. Every record loads as new, because the hand-made ones had no federatedId to match against. Nothing errors. They now have two of everything, and they discover it in a line review.

Ask about federatedId strategy before the first production load. And ask a second question: what is the federatedId derived from? If it's a mutable business value — a style number merchants renumber mid-season — the upsert key changes underneath you and you get orphans. Stable meaningless keys beat meaningful ones.

Concept 4 — Projects, Assortments and their owners

Project — the season-scale container. The Project Configurator creates "Projects with nested folders, plans, assortments, showcases, and boards," with assortments possible at both project root and folder level.

Assortment — a curated selection of products for a purpose: a channel, region, customer, or store cluster. One line → many assortments, exactly as in Week 1.

An assortment "can be created on its own or it can have an associated plan, board, or showcase":

Load typeCreates
ASSORTMENTAn assortment on its own
PLANA Plan and its assortment
BOARDA Board and its assortment
SHOWCASEA Showcase and its assortment

Assortment membership is replaced, not merged, by default

A full assortment load has explicit semantics: CSV items not in the assortment are added; items in both are updated if changed; matching items with no changes are unchanged; and "assortment items not in CSV → deleted." That last clause is the whole risk — membership is defined by the file, so an incomplete file is a deletion instruction. Week 4 covers the flag that changes this (partialAssortmentUpdate); carry the semantics from here.

The link back to Week 1

Board, Plan and Showcase are each backed by an assortment. They are three views onto the same kind of underlying object — which is exactly why a product can travel from mood board to line plan to line review without being re-created. The apps are the surface; the assortment is the substance.

Concept 5 — Where this is heading: the publish lifecycle

A preview of Week 3, included here because it is what the data model is for. When a user publishes a Plan, VibeIQ persists the Plan rows down to the underlying Assortment and creates an AssortmentPublishChange — "a snapshot of those changes" for downstream systems. It carries:

  • assortmentBaselineDownloadLink — an S3 URL for "the Assortment's complete state at publish time"
  • deleteDataDownloadLink — an S3 URL with "only Items that were dropped or deleted in this specific publish"
  • detail — arrays of adds, updates, deletes, familyItemsRemoved

Two facts to memorise now — both cause production incidents

  • Deletes do not accumulate. "Delete data does NOT accumulate across publishes. Each AssortmentPublishChange only contains deletes from that individual publish." Miss one publish and those deletes are permanently missed — unless you re-baseline.
  • S3 links expire after 24 hours. Re-fetching the change object refreshes them. A consumer that stores the URL breaks; one that stores the change ID can always recover.

Recommended reading

  • Data Loading — Getting Started — entity types, load type pairing, federatedId. Read properly, not skimmed.
  • Property Type Handling — the four property levels. The most important page in the docs for your role.
  • Loading Projects — project structure, folders, assortment owners.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — skim now for the shape; we return to it in Weeks 3 and 5.

Practical exercise

Take a single real-ish product — a hoodie in 4 colourways, 6 sizes, planned for SS26, appearing in both a Wholesale assortment and a DTC assortment at different price points.

Write out, as a list: which values live on the Item, which on the ProjectItem, which on the AssortmentItem, and for each Item-level property, which property level (Family / Option / All / Override) you would configure it as — and one sentence on why. Ten to fifteen properties is plenty.

The answer

The hoodie: 4 colourways, 6 sizes, SS26, in a Wholesale and a DTC assortment at different prices.

Item — org-wide, season-independent. The product itself.

  • styleNumber — Family. The identity of the style; cannot vary by colourway.
  • styleName ("Tech Fleece Hoodie") — Family. Same reason.
  • category / silhouette — Family. A hoodie stays a hoodie in every colour.
  • gender / division — Family.
  • colourName ("Black") — Option. This is the axis the options exist along.
  • colourCode — Option.
  • sizeRange — Family if all colourways share it; Override the moment one colourway ships a short size run. Ask, don't assume.
  • materialComposition — Family, unless one colourway uses a different fabric — then Override.
  • primaryImage — Option. Every colourway needs its own; a family-level image is the classic "why do all four look black?" complaint.
  • lifecycleStatus — Override. Usually moves for the whole style, but one colourway does sometimes get cut alone.

ProjectItem — this product, in SS26. Anything that could legitimately differ next season.

  • seasonalStatus (carryover / new / drop) — the definitional ProjectItem value.
  • targetRetailPrice — the season's intended price, before any channel decides otherwise.
  • targetMargin / plannedVolume — season-scoped commercial targets.
  • deliveryDrop ("Drop 2") — phasing is a property of the season, not of the product.

AssortmentItem — this product, in this assortment. The two assortments disagree here, and that is the whole point.

  • channelRetailPrice — $89 in DTC, $85 in Wholesale. This is the value the exercise is built around: it cannot live on the Item or the ProjectItem, because there is no single correct answer for it.
  • placement / doorCount / rank — position within that assortment.
  • assortmentStatus — kept or cut from this assortment, independent of the style's overall lifecycle.

And the sizes? Six sizes do not become six entities here. Size is expressed through the size-range template on the style, not as a third hierarchy level — the Family → Option hierarchy in this model is style → colourway. If you wrote out 24 option rows, that is the thing to correct.

How to think about it

Ask one question per property: "could this legitimately differ, and along which axis?" If it cannot differ at all, it is Family. If it differs by colourway, it is Option. If it usually follows the style but an exception is legitimate, it is Override. If it differs by season, it is not an Item property at all — it is a ProjectItem property. If it differs by channel, it is an AssortmentItem property. That single question resolves almost every row.

The Family/Override distinction is where money is lost. Both copy down. The difference is only whether an option-level value survives the next load. Model a price as Family and every colourway edit a merchandiser makes is silently reverted the next time the style-level value reloads — which is exactly the Week 2 scenario below, and one of the most common escalations in the platform. The test is simple and worth saying out loud: is an exception ever legitimate? If yes, Override.

Watch for the value that has no single right answer. Channel price is the giveaway in this exercise. If a property's correct value depends on which assortment you are looking from, it cannot live above the AssortmentItem — and putting it there is not a modelling preference, it is a correctness requirement. The same logic pushes seasonal status down to ProjectItem.

"All" is the level people forget and then misuse. It manages values individually for every entity in a family with no copy-down at all. That is right when each node genuinely owns its value and inheritance would be misleading. It is wrong as a "safe default" — a team expecting inheritance gets none, every option comes up blank, and the customer reports it as "the load didn't work."

What a weak answer looks like: assigning levels without reasons, or putting everything on the Item because that is where a product "is." The reasons are the deliverable — this exercise is rehearsal for the conversation where a customer asks why their data reverted, and the answer is a property level.

Sources
  • Property Type Handling — the four levels and their copy-down behaviour. For your role this is the single most important page in the documentation — the "All" definition and the "no copy down behavior exists" wording come from here.
  • Data Loading — Getting Started — entity types and the load-type pairing that shows ProjectItem and AssortmentItem are relationships to an Item, not copies of it.
  • Concept 1 — The three item layers — the scope test behind the Item / ProjectItem / AssortmentItem split.
  • Week 1 — style, colourway, SKU — the vocabulary this maps onto: Family ≈ style, Option ≈ colourway.

Realistic customer scenario

Back at Summit Athletic, six weeks post go-live. A merchandiser messages you:

"Something's broken. On Monday we set custom retail prices on about 30 individual colourways for the outlet channel. This morning they're all back to the standard style price. Nobody touched them. We're presenting to leadership Thursday."

You have their nightly Loader feed, their property configuration, and the publish history available. Give your first hypothesis, the two things you'd check first to confirm or kill it, and the configuration change you'd expect to end up recommending. Then: one sentence on what you'd say to the merchandiser today, before you know the answer.

The answer

First hypothesis: retailPrice is configured at Family level, not Override. The merchandiser's 30 colourway edits were real and were saved. Then the nightly Loader sent the style-level price again, Family-level copy-down ran, and every option was overwritten with the style value. Nobody touched them — a scheduled job did, exactly as configured.

The two things I'd check first — chosen because either one alone can kill the hypothesis:

  1. The property configuration for retailPrice. One lookup, and it is decisive: if the level is FAMILY, the mechanism is confirmed and the rest is detail. If it is OVERRIDE, my hypothesis is dead and I stop pursuing it rather than trying to rescue it.
  2. The overnight LoaderProcess — did it run, when, and did its file carry a retailPrice column? Timing has to line up with "fine Monday, wrong Tuesday morning," and the column has to be present. A load that did not include the field cannot have clobbered it, however the property is configured.

Together these separate the two candidate causes cleanly: configuration (wrong level) versus payload (the feed newly started sending a column it never sent before). Both produce identical symptoms and have different fixes.

The configuration change I'd expect to recommend: move retailPrice from Family to Override. It still copies down — so setting the style price still populates every colourway, which is the behaviour they want — but a deliberate option-level value now wins and survives the next load. That is precisely the case Override exists for.

Two things to raise alongside it, because the fix alone is incomplete:

  • Recovery of the 30 values. They are gone from the current state; publish history or the pre-load data tells you what they were. Establish that before promising a restore.
  • Which other properties have the same shape? Anything a merchandiser can edit per colourway that is modelled at Family will do this too. Price is simply the one they noticed. Audit before Thursday, not after the next incident.

What I'd say to the merchandiser today, before knowing:

"Nothing you did caused this and the edits weren't lost by accident — this looks like the system doing what it was configured to do, which means it is fixable and preventable. I'm confirming the mechanism now and I'll come back this afternoon with what happened and what it takes to stop it. Thursday is the priority: tell me whether you need those 30 prices restored, or correct in the deck."
How to think about it

"Nobody touched them" is evidence, not an excuse. Taken literally it is the most useful sentence in the message: no human edited these records, and they still changed. That rules out user error and points straight at an automated writer — a load, a workflow, or a formula. Treat customer statements as data rather than noise and the search space collapses immediately.

The symptom shape names the class of cause before any detail does. This is consistent (all 30), silent (no error anywhere) and it affects every record of a kind (option-level prices). That shape is configuration, not a race and not a bad row. Had it been "some styles, different ones each night," you would be looking at concurrency instead — a Week 6 problem with an entirely different investigation.

Pick checks that can disprove you, and cheap ones first. The property-level lookup is seconds of work and kills the hypothesis outright if it comes back Override. That is what makes it the right first move — not that it confirms what you already believe. A check that can only agree with you is not a check.

Family vs. Override is the whole lesson. Both copy down, so both look identical the day you configure them. They differ only under the next load, which is days or weeks later — by which time nobody connects the two events. This is why property level is the second of the six universal questions in Week 9, and why it belongs on the data-model health check.

The communication is not padding. A merchandiser presenting to leadership on Thursday is judging you on whether you sound in control, and the temptation is to say either "I don't know yet" or a confident guess. The move is to separate what you know (the edits weren't lost by accident) from what you suspect (the mechanism), commit to a time, and ask the one question that changes what you do next — restore, or work around it for the deck. That habit is Week 9, Concept 7, arriving early.

What a weak answer looks like: going straight to the Loader logs. The load succeeded — it will tell you nothing is wrong, because from its point of view nothing is. The configuration is what makes a successful load destructive, so read the configuration first.

Sources
  • Property Type Handling — the Family vs. Override copy-down semantics this scenario turns on.
  • Data Loading — Getting Started — the LoaderProcess record you pull to confirm timing and payload.
  • Week 9 — Symptom shape → class of cause — why "consistent and silent" means configuration rather than concurrency.
  • Key findings — Symptom → likely cause — "Our data reverted overnight" is the first row of that table, and this is why.

Quiz

  1. Name the three item layers and give one value that belongs at each. Why can't the same value live at all three?

    Item (org-wide, season-independent) — e.g. style number or category. ProjectItem (one project ≈ one season) — e.g. that season's target price or seasonal status. AssortmentItem (one assortment) — e.g. placement or channel-specific terms. They aren't three copies: an Item is the product, while ProjectItem and AssortmentItem are relationship entities that carry data about the product in a context. The same value can't sensibly live at all three because each answers a different question — a style number doesn't vary by season, and a channel-specific price is meaningless org-wide.

  2. Why must a PROJECT_ITEM load be paired with ITEM? What does that tell you about the relationship?

    Because an AssortmentItem or ProjectItem is a relationship to an Item — it cannot exist without one. The Loader enforces the dependency: PROJECT_ITEM must be paired with ITEM, and ASSORTMENT must be paired with ITEM too. The pairing rule is evidence of the nesting: you cannot load the narrow, contextual thing without the broad, durable thing it attaches to.

  3. Explain Family vs. Override in one sentence each. Give an attribute you'd model as each, and say why.

    Family: updates the family-level entity and copies that value down onto every option — use it for true style-level facts like category or silhouette, which cannot vary by colourway. Override: also copies down, but an option can be given a different value that wins — use it for price or margin target, which usually follow the style but occasionally differ for one colourway (a limited edition priced higher). The test is simple: if an exception is ever legitimate, it's Override, not Family.

  4. What is distinctive about the All level, and when would you actually want it?

    All means values are "managed individually for every entity in a Family, regardless if it's a Family or Option level entity," and critically "no copy down behavior exists". So there is no inheritance at all — every node must be set explicitly. Want it when each entity genuinely carries its own independent value and inheritance would be actively wrong or misleading. The risk is that a team expecting inheritance gets none and every option comes up blank, which presents to the customer as "the load didn't work".

  5. What is a federatedId, and what exactly happens on load when one already exists?

    A federatedId is "a value you provide which uniquely identifies the entity within an org." It drives upsert: if no entity with that ID exists it is created; if one does, it is updated. So the ID is the matching key — which is why stability matters more than readability. Note there is no setting to nominate an ID column (Custom Entity excepted); the value must arrive under the expected property, usually via federatedMappings.

  6. A customer hand-created 200 products in the UI during their pilot, then turned on a nightly Loader feed. Predict what happens on the first run, and explain why.

    All 200 load as new, producing duplicates. Entities created through the core apps have no federatedId by default, so there is nothing for the incoming rows to match against — every row upserts as a create. The load reports success and nothing errors. The customer discovers it later, usually in a line review, with two of everything. Prevention: assign federatedIds to the UI-created records via the API before the first production load.

  7. What is the relationship between an Assortment and the Board / Plan / Showcase apps?

    Board, Plan and Showcase are each backed by an Assortment. An assortment can exist on its own, or with an associated plan, board or showcase — the load types are ASSORTMENT, PLAN, BOARD, SHOWCASE. They are three views onto the same kind of underlying object, which is exactly why a product can travel from mood board to line plan to line review without being re-created. The apps are the surface; the assortment is the substance.

  8. An integration consumed publish #14 but its worker was down for publish #15. Publish #16 succeeds. What is now wrong downstream, and what's the fix?

    Publish #15's deletes are permanently lost — "delete data does NOT accumulate across publishes," so #16 carries only its own deletes and will never replay #15's. The downstream system now holds products the brand has already cut, silently: nothing errors, and processing #16 successfully makes it look recovered. The fix is to re-baseline — pull the current assortmentBaselineDownloadLink, which is the assortment's complete state at publish time, and reconcile downstream against it rather than resuming from incremental detail.

Hands-on challenge

Draft a "data model health check" — the 6–8 questions you'd ask on inheriting any existing account, aimed squarely at the failure modes in this module: property-level mapping, federatedId source and stability, UI-created records, assortment structure, and publish consumption. This becomes a real artefact you'll use in your first weeks on the job, and we'll fold it into the Week 10 capstone.

The answer

The data-model health check. Eight questions, each aimed at a failure mode from this module. Run it on any account you inherit, before forming an opinion about anything.

  1. "Show me the property configuration export. Which properties are Family, which Override, which All?"
    Finds: the copy-down clobber before it happens. Ask specifically for anything a user can edit per colourway that is modelled at Family — those are scheduled incidents. Take the export (contrail types getAll), not anyone's description of it.
  2. "What generates your federatedId, and what could ever change it?"
    Finds: duplicate records. "Could ever change" is the operative half — a stable-looking ID that is really a formatted label (or is computed in a conditionalColumn) will change, and the next load duplicates everything.
  3. "Was anything created by users in the UI before the integration was switched on?"
    Finds: the classic pilot-data bug. UI-created entities have no federatedId by default, so the first Loader run creates a second copy of every one of them rather than updating it.
  4. "For each of your top ten attributes, which layer is it on — Item, ProjectItem or AssortmentItem?"
    Finds: values modelled too high. Anything that varies by season sitting on the Item, or anything that varies by channel sitting above the AssortmentItem, is a correctness bug waiting for someone to notice.
  5. "How is your assortment load configured — partialAssortmentUpdate true or false?"
    Finds: the delete-everything default. It defaults to false, meaning replace: a file containing only this week's changes removes everything not in it. Ask what a typical file contains, then ask what the flag is set to, and see whether the two answers are compatible.
  6. "Is shouldSkipAssortmentPublish set anywhere, and does anyone remember why?"
    Finds: the silent integration. It has a legitimate use for high-volume backfills; the danger is that it gets set "just for the migration" and stays in a reusable config forever, making every subsequent load invisible downstream.
  7. "Who consumes publishes, and what do they do with the delete file?"
    Finds: ghost products in PLM. The delete link is the most-ignored field in the change object, and deletes do not accumulate across publishes — miss one and those removals never reappear.
  8. "Where do Projects and Assortments come from — are they created by hand, by load, or by the Project Configurator?"
    Finds: structural drift. It also surfaces workspaceIdentifier problems early, which matter because that property is not editable in the Hub UI and takes an API call to correct.

How to run it: questions 1 and 2 are the ones that pay; if you get thirty minutes, spend twenty there. Ask for artefacts rather than answers wherever an artefact exists — a config export, a sample file, a LoaderProcess ID. And write down what you were told and what you verified, separately. The gap between those two lists is your risk register for the account.

How to think about it

A health check is not a questionnaire, it is a set of traps you already know about. Every question above corresponds to a specific documented failure mode in this module. That is what makes it fast — you are not exploring, you are checking eight known places where accounts break. Questions that do not map to a failure mode are conversation, not diagnostics.

Ask for ground truth, not recollection. Half the value here is refusing to accept a verbal answer where an export exists. People describe the configuration they intended, and the configuration that is running is often two years and three staff changes away from that. contrail types getAll settles it in one command, which is also why Week 10 puts the config export first in the inheritance sequence.

Two questions look like history and are actually the highest-yield ones. "Was anything created in the UI first?" and "does anyone remember why this flag is set?" both dig at decisions made during implementation by people who have since left. Those decisions are invisible in the current data and are the cause of a disproportionate share of inherited problems.

Order matters more than completeness. Property levels and identifier stability come first because their answers change how you read everything else. If federatedId is unstable, every duplicate you find later is explained; if it is stable, duplicates mean something different entirely.

This artefact compounds. It pairs with the Week 3 publish review and the Week 4 pre-load checklist; by Week 9 the eight artefacts become one account-inheritance kit. Write it so a colleague could run it without you — that is the bar for something worth calling a practice artefact rather than notes.

Sources
  • Property Type Handling — questions 1 and 4 — the property levels and the layer each value belongs on.
  • Data Loading — Getting Started — questions 2 and 3 — federatedId as the upsert key, and the fact that UI-created entities have none by default.
  • Loader Configuration Reference — questions 5 and 6 — partialAssortmentUpdate and shouldSkipAssortmentPublish, with their defaults.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — question 7 — the delete file, and the rule that deletes do not accumulate.
  • Loading Projects — question 8 — project and assortment structure, and workspaceIdentifier.

I know this when I can…

  • Given a symptom, name the layer it's likely on before doing any investigation.
  • Choose a property level for a new attribute and correctly predict the next load's behaviour.
  • Explain the duplicate-records bug end to end, and the two questions that prevent it.
  • Draw Project → Assortment → Board/Plan/Showcase from memory.
  • Say why a missed publish is not self-healing, and what re-baselining is for.

Module 3 · Week 3

End-user apps & the publish lifecycle

What your users actually touch, and what happens the moment one of them hits publish. This is the module that connects Week 2's data model to Weeks 4 and 5's integrations — the publish seam is where "it works in VibeIQ but not in PLM" lives.

On this page

  1. 🤝The three apps
  2. ⚙️The platform underneath
  3. ⚙️The publish lifecycle, end to end
  4. ⚙️Anatomy of an AssortmentPublishChange
  5. ⚙️Two ways to consume it
  6. 📚Recommended reading
  7. ✅Practical exercise
  8. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  9. ✅Quiz
  10. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) explain what each of Board, Plan and Showcase is for and what it replaced, (2) walk the publish lifecycle end to end and name what happens at each step, (3) describe an AssortmentPublishChange field by field and say what a consumer should do with each, and (4) localise a "published but didn't arrive" failure to one of four checkpoints before theorising about causes.

Concept 1 — The three apps

Each app replaces a specific incumbent tool — this is VibeIQ's own framing, from Week 1 — and, from Week 2, each is backed by an Assortment.

AppVibeIQ's descriptionReplaces
Board"The visual workspace for trend research, mood boards, and creative direction"Miro
Plan"The structured workspace for line planning and product definition"Excel
Showcase"The presentation workspace for line reviews and stakeholder alignment"PowerPoint

The technically important detail in each:

  • Board connects unstructured creative work directly to product data, so agents can interpret and act on creative intent rather than on a picture of it.
  • Plan — "each row represents an actual product record." This is the whole difference from a spreadsheet: the grid is a view onto entities, not a document. Editing a row edits a ProjectItem or AssortmentItem.
  • Showcase "remains live and current, with feedback flowing back into the product record." A deck that is a rendering of live data, not an export of it.

When a customer mentions they "exported to PowerPoint for the line review," treat it as an adoption failure with a technical smell underneath — usually Showcase not being trusted, or the product data being too incomplete to present without cleanup. Chase the cause, not the behaviour. The same logic applies to any export-to-spreadsheet habit that survives go-live.

Concept 2 — The platform underneath

  • Product Data Platform — products, assortments, assets (Week 2's territory).
  • Agent layer — native agents for "assortment comparison, tech pack generation, trend research, carryover analysis," plus custom agents and a prompt-template library.
  • Integration & App Platform — an App Store of prebuilt connectors to PLM, ERP, DAM and supply-chain systems; an MCP server exposing product data to external orchestration layers; and APIs, SDKs and a CLI.

Still the Week 1 caution

The agent layer is described in marketing detail and documented thinly. Named native agents are a public claim, not a verified capability set. Confirm GA vs. beta vs. roadmap internally before you repeat any of it to a customer.

Concept 3 — The publish lifecycle, end to end

  1. Users modify Items — products, materials — inside a Plan.
  2. Users adopt images, colours and attributes onto those Items.
  3. A user triggers publish.
  4. The system persists the Plan rows down to the underlying Assortment.
  5. An AssortmentPublishChange is created — "a snapshot of those changes."
  6. External systems retrieve and process it.

The lifecycle can be bypassed entirely

A Loader configuration can set shouldSkipAssortmentPublish: true, which writes assortment item updates straight to the database — "bypassing the publish workflow… skips change history, summary recalculation, composite assortment updates, and the assortment|publish event." Everything on this page then does not happen: no AssortmentPublishChange, no event, nothing for a consumer to receive. Week 4 covers it in full; carry the fact from here, because "the data is in VibeIQ but PLM never heard" is a Module 3 symptom with a Module 4 cause.

The step everyone skips

Step 4. Publish is not "send to PLM." Publish is first an internal commit from the Plan down to the Assortment. The downstream integration is a consumer of the record that commit produces. So a publish can succeed while the downstream sync fails — and those are two different investigations, with different owners.

Concept 4 — Anatomy of an AssortmentPublishChange

FieldContentsWhat a consumer does with it
assortmentBaselineDownloadLinkS3 URL — "the Assortment's complete state at publish time"Recovery and reconciliation. The answer to "what is true now?"
deleteDataDownloadLinkS3 URL — "only Items that were dropped or deleted in this specific publish"Apply removals. The field most often ignored, and the one that causes ghost products downstream.
detailadds, updates, deletes, familyItemsRemovedEfficient incremental sync. The answer to "what changed this time?"

Most real consumers need both: detail for day-to-day efficiency, baseline for recovery when detail has been missed.

The two rules that cause production incidents

  • Deletes do not accumulate. "Delete data does NOT accumulate across publishes. Each AssortmentPublishChange only contains deletes from that individual publish." A consumer that misses publish #15 has permanently missed those deletes. Nothing later will replay them. The only cure is re-baselining.
  • S3 links expire after 24 hours. Re-fetching the change object refreshes them. Store the change ID, never the URL — a consumer that persists the URL breaks after a day; one that persists the ID can always recover.

Non-accumulating deletes make missed publishes silently unrecoverable. Nothing errors; the downstream system simply keeps products the brand has already cut. It surfaces weeks later as "PLM has styles we dropped" — usually in front of someone senior. On any account with a publish integration, ask early: what happens when your consumer is down for a day, and who notices?

Concept 5 — Two ways to consume it

Week 5 goes deep on this; here is the shape, so the lifecycle is complete.

AspectChange History PollingAssortment Publish Events
Trigger"Time-based (scheduled)""Event-based (on publish)"
Latency"Minutes to hours""Near real-time"
Complexity"Simple to implement""Requires webhook endpoint"

Polling hits /api/change-history filtered by a time range — createdOn supports a BETWEEN syntax. The consumer stores a last-sync timestamp and advances it after each successful poll. Note this is temporal filtering, not cursors: clock skew and overlap windows are the consumer's problem, and a naive implementation drops or duplicates records at the seam between polls.

Best for: batch sync on a schedule, tracking multiple entity types, historical retrieval, and systems with no webhook support.

Event-driven pushes on publish; the consumer calls back for the full change detail. Best for: real-time needs, assortment-specific workflows, webhook-capable systems, and minimising API calls.

Recommended reading

  • Integration Patterns — overview — the comparison table above, in its original form.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — read this one properly; it is the spine of the module.
  • Change History Polling — skim; Week 5 returns to it.
  • vibeiq.com/platform — the app descriptions quoted above.

Practical exercise

Write the pseudocode for a well-behaved publish consumer — twenty lines is plenty. It must: record what it has processed, survive being down for 48 hours, apply deletes correctly, and recover without human help after a missed publish. Then annotate the two lines that would be wrong in a naive implementation, and say what each would cost the customer.

The answer
on_publish_event(assortmentId, changeId):        # 1
    if store.already_processed(changeId): return       # 2  idempotency
    enqueue(changeId)                                  # 3  ack fast, work later

process(changeId):
    last = store.last_processed_change(assortmentId)   # 4
    change = api.get_change(changeId)                  # 5  fetch fresh — links are 24h
    if not is_consecutive(last, change):               # 6  a gap = missed publishes
        reconcile_from_baseline(change); return        # 7  re-baseline, don't resume
    adds    = fetch(change.detail.adds)
    updates = fetch(change.detail.updates)
    deletes = fetch(change.deleteDataDownloadLink)     # 8  the ignored one
    with transaction():                                # 9
        apply(adds); apply(updates); remove(deletes)
        store.mark_processed(changeId)                 # 10 same commit as the work

reconcile_from_baseline(change):
    baseline = fetch(change.assortmentBaselineDownloadLink)
    replace_assortment_state(baseline)                 # 11 full truth at publish time
    store.mark_processed(change.id)

nightly:                                               # 12 catches silent stalling
    if hours_since_last_event(assortmentId) > 26: alert()

The two lines a naive implementation gets wrong:

Line 5 — fetching the change object fresh, rather than storing the S3 URL. A naive consumer persists assortmentBaselineDownloadLink when the event arrives and re-uses it later. Those links expire after 24 hours. What it costs the customer: recovery fails at exactly the moment it is needed. The stored URL works in every test, works for a day, and is dead the morning someone actually needs to reconcile. Persist the change ID; re-fetch to get fresh links.

Line 6–7 — detecting the gap and re-baselining instead of resuming. A naive consumer comes back from downtime and processes the newest change, or walks forward applying each missed detail in turn. Both are wrong, because deletes do not accumulate across publishes — each change carries only the items dropped in that publish. What it costs: products cut weeks ago stay active in PLM forever, and sourcing starts costing styles nobody is making. Nothing self-heals, because no future publish will ever mention those deletions again. That is the Week 3 scenario in one line of code.

How to think about it

The whole design follows from one asymmetry: adds and updates are self-correcting, deletes are not. Miss an add and the next publish that touches the item fixes it. Miss a delete and it is gone forever from the event stream. So every recovery decision in a publish consumer is really a decision about deletions, and "just resume where we left off" is the instinct that has to be trained out.

Store the ID, never the URL. This is the single most transferable rule from the module, and it generalises: persist the identifier of the thing, fetch the access to it on demand. Any time an API hands you a pre-signed URL, assume it is perishable.

Idempotency is not optional, because at-least-once delivery is the norm. Line 2 costs almost nothing and removes an entire class of duplicate-application bug. Note it pairs with line 10: marking a change processed must happen in the same transaction as the work, or a crash between the two leaves you having done the work and not recorded it — or worse, recorded it and not done it.

Ack fast, work later (line 3). Doing the whole sync inside the webhook handler means a slow PLM write turns into a delivery timeout, which turns into a retry, which arrives while the first attempt is still running. Now you have concurrency you never designed for. Accept the event, queue it, return.

Line 12 is the one people leave out and regret. Every failure mode above is loud. The dangerous one is silence — a consumer that stopped receiving events and therefore stops doing anything, reporting nothing, because "no errors" and "no work" look identical from the outside. A staleness alarm is the only thing that distinguishes them, and it is three lines.

What a weak answer looks like: pseudocode that processes the event payload directly. The payload is deliberately thin — two IDs — and carries no product data at all. If your twenty lines never call back to the API, you have misread the mechanism.

Sources
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — the change object's three parts, the 24-hour link expiry, and the rule that delete data does not accumulate across publishes. The spine of this exercise.
  • Integration Patterns — overview — the push/poll comparison that frames why an event consumer needs its own recovery path.
  • Week 5 — Designing a consumer that survives — the same six hazards worked in full, with the design answer for each.
  • Key findings — Numbers worth memorising — 24 hours and 90 days: the two horizons that constrain every recovery conversation.

Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic, three months in. Their PLM lead emails:

"Centric has 40 styles that merchandising says were cut from the SS27 line weeks ago. They're showing as active and our sourcing team has started costing two of them. Merch swears they dropped them in VibeIQ. Who's wrong?"

Give your four checkpoints in order and what you'd look for at each. Then: your leading hypothesis, the fix for the immediate problem, and the change that stops it recurring. Note that "who's wrong" is the wrong question — say what you'd reply instead.

The answer

The four checkpoints, in order. Each one splits the problem in half, so you never investigate a segment you have already cleared.

  1. Were the 40 styles actually dropped in VibeIQ? Look at the assortment's current state. If they are still members, merchandising did the work somewhere that did not reach the assortment — a Plan edit never published, or a cut made in a different assortment. Nothing downstream is at fault and the investigation ends here.
  2. Did a publish happen after the cut, and did it record the removals? Find the AssortmentPublishChange covering that window and read its detail.deletes and familyItemsRemoved. This is the seam between "the user did it" and "the system carried it" — the internal commit from Plan down to Assortment. If the change object contains the 40, VibeIQ has done its job completely.
  3. Was the change delivered? The delivery mechanism is an Event Workflow, so this is a Process List question: did the workflow fire for that change, what was the task output, did it complete or fail? A failed or missing process localises the problem to delivery rather than to data or consumption.
  4. Did the consumer fetch and apply the delete file? If the event was delivered successfully, the remaining possibility is that their integration read detail.adds and detail.updates and ignored deleteDataDownloadLink. Ask what their code does with that field.

Leading hypothesis: checkpoints 1–3 pass and 4 fails — the consumer never processes deletes. This is the most common publish integration defect, because removals are the easiest field to overlook while building: everything looks correct in testing, since additions and edits flow perfectly and nobody tests a deletion end to end.

The likely variant, if their consumer does handle deletes: it missed a publish during an outage. Because deletes do not accumulate, those 40 removals appeared in exactly one change object and will never be mentioned again. That version does not self-heal and no amount of waiting fixes it.

Fix for the immediate problem: reconcile from the baseline. Fetch assortmentBaselineDownloadLink from the current change — freshly, because the link expires in 24 hours — and treat it as the full truth for that assortment: anything in Centric and not in the baseline gets deactivated. That corrects all 40 at once and any others nobody has noticed yet. Do this before hunting the root cause; sourcing is spending money right now.

The change that stops it recurring: the consumer must (a) process the delete file on every publish, (b) track processed change IDs so a gap is detectable, (c) re-baseline rather than resume when it detects one, and (d) run a periodic baseline reconciliation regardless — weekly is usually enough — so a missed deletion has a bounded lifetime instead of an unbounded one.

On "who's wrong?" — what I'd reply instead:

"Nobody is wrong, and I don't think this is a disagreement about facts — merch dropped them and Centric never heard about it, and both of those can be true at once. The publish and the sync are two different steps with two different owners, so let me find out which one didn't complete. Meanwhile, can we get those two styles out of costing today? I'll have the mechanism this afternoon and a permanent fix in the next few days."
How to think about it

The framing is the trap. "Who's wrong?" invites you to arbitrate between two teams, and any answer you give makes one of them the loser in front of the other. It is also technically wrong: merch did drop them and Centric did not receive them, both facts, no contradiction. Refusing the framing is not diplomacy — it is the accurate reading, and it is what lets you keep both teams cooperating during the investigation.

Publish is not "send to PLM." It is first an internal commit — Plan rows persisting down to the Assortment — and only then a downstream sync. Two steps, two owners, two ways to fail. People skip the internal commit when walking the lifecycle and it produces bad diagnosis: they conclude "publish is broken" when publish worked perfectly and delivery or consumption failed. Separating those is the whole value of checkpoint 2.

Order the checkpoints by what they eliminate, not by the pipeline. Each one clears a segment permanently. This is the Week 9 localisation rule arriving early: establish where before theorising about why. Someone who leads with "their consumer is broken" is usually right here and still working badly — they will spend a day defending the theory if they are wrong.

Stabilise before diagnosing. Sourcing is costing styles that do not exist, and that clock runs while you investigate. Re-baselining fixes the customer's actual problem in minutes and is safe regardless of root cause, because the baseline is authoritative by definition. Root cause still matters — it just does not have to come first. New TAMs routinely invert this and deliver a correct diagnosis to someone who has already spent the money.

Note what the delivery checkpoint really is. The publish event is dispatched by an Event Workflow, which means integration debugging and workflow debugging are the same skill and use the same Process List. That equivalence, more than any single fact, is why Week 6 earns its own module.

What a weak answer looks like: "their consumer has a bug" with no checkpoints. It is probably correct and it is unusable — you cannot say it to the PLM lead without evidence, and if it turns out to be the missed-publish variant instead, the fix is different and you have already committed publicly to the wrong story.

Sources
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — the delete file, the baseline link, the 24-hour expiry, and the non-accumulation rule that makes a missed publish permanent.
  • Observing Workflows — the Process List used at checkpoint 3 — statuses, task outputs, and the 90-day retention that limits how far back you can look.
  • Concept 3 — The publish lifecycle — the six steps, and the internal commit people skip.
  • Week 9 — Localise before theorising — the discipline the four checkpoints are an instance of.

Quiz

  1. Name the three apps, what each replaced, and what each is backed by in the data model.

    Board — "the visual workspace for trend research, mood boards, and creative direction," replaced Miro. Plan — "the structured workspace for line planning and product definition," replaced Excel. Showcase — "the presentation workspace for line reviews and stakeholder alignment," replaced PowerPoint. All three are backed by an Assortment in the data model.

  2. Why is "each row in Plan represents an actual product record" a technically important statement, not marketing?

    Because it means the Plan grid is a view onto entities, not a document. Editing a row edits a ProjectItem or AssortmentItem directly — there is no export, no separate copy, no reconciliation step. That's the entire difference from a spreadsheet: a spreadsheet is a snapshot that immediately starts diverging from reality, whereas Plan is the reality. It also explains why publishing can work at all, and why a Plan edit can trigger workflows.

  3. Walk the six steps of the publish lifecycle. Which step do people skip, and why does skipping it cause bad diagnosis?

    (1) Users modify Items in a Plan. (2) They adopt images, colours and attributes. (3) A user triggers publish. (4) The system persists the Plan rows down to the underlying Assortment. (5) An AssortmentPublishChange is created. (6) External systems retrieve and process it. Step 4 is the skipped one. Publish is not "send to PLM" — it is first an internal commit from Plan to Assortment. Skipping it causes bad diagnosis because a publish can succeed while the downstream sync fails; those are two different investigations with different owners, and conflating them sends you looking in the wrong system.

  4. What are the three parts of an AssortmentPublishChange, and what would a consumer use each for?

    assortmentBaselineDownloadLink — S3 URL for "the Assortment's complete state at publish time"; used for recovery and reconciliation ("what is true now?"). deleteDataDownloadLink — S3 URL with only items dropped or deleted in this publish; used to apply removals, and the field most often ignored, causing ghost products downstream. detail — arrays of adds, updates, deletes, familyItemsRemoved; used for efficient incremental sync ("what changed this time?").

  5. Why do most real consumers need both the baseline and the detail?

    Because they answer different questions and each fails where the other works. Detail is efficient for day-to-day sync but is per-publish only — miss one and that information never returns. Baseline is complete but expensive to process every time. So the working pattern is detail for normal operation and baseline for recovery: when a gap in processed changes is detected, or on a scheduled reconciliation, fall back to the baseline. A consumer with only detail cannot recover; one with only baseline is wasteful and slow.

  6. A consumer's worker is down for 36 hours and misses two publishes. What is now wrong, what will not fix itself, and what is the recovery?

    Both publishes' deletes are permanently lost and will never be replayed. Updates and adds are recoverable from a later baseline, but the deletes are not self-healing — the downstream system keeps products that were cut, with nothing reporting a problem. Recovery: fetch the current baseline (re-fetching the change object refreshes the expired S3 links), reconcile downstream state against it, and remove anything present downstream but absent from the baseline. Then fix the consumer so a gap in processed change IDs triggers this automatically.

  7. Why should a consumer store the change ID rather than the S3 URL?

    Because the S3 links expire after 24 hours, while the change object itself is durable and re-fetching it refreshes the links. A consumer that persists the URL has a dead reference tomorrow and no way to recover the payload; one that persists the change ID can always re-fetch and get working links. It's a one-line design decision that determines whether the consumer can recover from any outage longer than a day.

  8. Polling uses time ranges rather than cursors. Name one failure that design invites, and how you'd guard against it.

    Temporal filtering is not a cursor — it's a guess at a position. One failure: boundary exclusivity. Polling (lastSync, now] loses any record written in the same instant as the cutoff; polling [lastSync, now] processes records twice. (Also valid: in-flight writes committing after the query but timestamped before the cutoff, or clock skew between consumer and server.) Guard: overlap the window deliberately — poll from lastSync − safetyMargin — and make processing idempotent so re-fetching is harmless. Also advance the timestamp from the maximum createdOn actually returned, not the local clock, and never advance on partial failure.

Hands-on challenge

Write a one-page publish integration review checklist — the things you'd verify on any account running a publish integration, covering: delete handling, missed-publish recovery, link expiry, baseline reconciliation cadence, and who is alerted when the consumer fails. Pair it with the Week 2 data-model health check; together they become your standard account-inheritance kit.

The answer

Publish integration review checklist. Run it on any account with a publish integration — at handover, after an incident, and once a quarter.

1 · Delete handling

  • Does the consumer fetch deleteDataDownloadLink on every publish, or only when detail.deletes is non-empty? (Both fields matter; familyItemsRemoved is a separate case people miss.)
  • What does "delete" mean in the target system — hard delete, deactivate, or flag? Get this in writing; it is a business decision that engineers make silently.
  • Has an end-to-end deletion ever been tested in a non-production environment? Almost always the answer is no.

2 · Missed-publish recovery

  • Does the consumer record which change IDs it has processed — enough to detect a gap, not just to remember the last one?
  • On detecting a gap, does it re-baseline or resume? Resuming is wrong: the deletes in skipped publishes are gone permanently.
  • What happens after 48 hours of downtime? Ask them to describe it, then ask what they have actually observed.

3 · Link expiry

  • Are S3 URLs ever persisted anywhere — a database column, a queue message, a retry payload, a log used for replay? All four are common and all four break after 24 hours.
  • Is the change object re-fetched by ID at processing time?
  • If a retry queue holds items for longer than a day, does the retry re-fetch or replay the stale payload?

4 · Baseline reconciliation cadence

  • Is there a scheduled full reconciliation, independent of events? What period, and what does it do with discrepancies — report or correct?
  • When did it last run, and did anyone read the output? An unread reconciliation report is not a control.
  • Does the cadence bound the damage acceptably? Weekly means a missed deletion lives at most a week; none means forever.

5 · Alerting and ownership

  • Who is paged when the consumer fails — a named person or team, not "the integration team"?
  • Is there a staleness alarm — an alert on no publishes processed for longer than normal? This is the one almost nobody has, and silence is the failure mode that survives longest.
  • Is the delivering Event Workflow monitored for FAILED on the VibeIQ side, and does anyone look at that dashboard?
  • Does the customer know that workflow evidence is retained 90 days, so an unexamined failure becomes unexplainable?

6 · Idempotency and ordering

  • Is processing idempotent under redelivery?
  • Is the processed-marker committed in the same transaction as the work?
  • Can two publishes for one assortment be processed concurrently, and does that matter?

Scoring it: failures in sections 1–3 are correctness bugs — data is wrong now and nobody knows. Failures in 4–5 are detection bugs — you will find out late. Section 6 is a resilience bug — it bites under load. Report them in that order; a customer who fixes only section 1 has still bought a lot.

How to think about it

A review checklist should be answerable by observation, not by opinion. Every item above resolves to a thing you can look at: a line of code, a schedule, a person's name, a config value. "Is the integration robust?" is not a checklist item. If a question can be answered "yes, probably," rewrite it until it cannot.

Sections 1–3 are the same underlying fact seen three times. Deletes do not accumulate, links expire, and gaps do not self-heal all trace back to one property: the event stream is not replayable. Once a consumer team internalises that, most of these items become obvious to them. Leading with the principle and then the checks lands better than a flat list.

Section 5 exists because the worst failures are quiet. Everything in sections 1–4 announces itself eventually. A consumer that has stopped receiving events looks identical to a consumer with nothing to do — same logs, same zero error count. The staleness alarm is the only control that distinguishes them, and it is the cheapest item on the list.

Ask what has been observed, not what should happen. "What happens after 48 hours down?" gets you a design; "what happened the last time it was down?" gets you the truth. Half of integration review is finding the gap between the two, politely.

Pair it with the Week 2 health check. Together they cover the two halves of an inherited account — how the data is modelled, and how it leaves. That pairing is the beginning of the account-inheritance kit, and it is deliberate that neither is much use without the other: a perfect data model published to a consumer that ignores deletes is still a customer with wrong data in PLM.

Sources
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — sections 1–4: the change object's fields, the expiry, and the non-accumulation rule.
  • Change History Polling — the alternative consumption path, for accounts running both — and the reconciliation source when events are the primary channel.
  • Observing Workflows — section 5: the workflow failure surface and the 90-day evidence horizon.
  • Week 5 — Designing a consumer that survives — the six hazards this checklist audits for, each with its design answer.

I know this when I can…

  • Explain each app, what it replaced, and what it sits on in the data model.
  • Walk the publish lifecycle and correctly separate the internal commit from the downstream sync.
  • Describe an AssortmentPublishChange field by field, without notes.
  • Explain why a missed publish is not self-healing, and what re-baselining is for.
  • Take a "published but didn't arrive" report and localise it to one of four checkpoints before proposing a cause.
