Module 4 · Week 4

Data in: the Loader framework

With Week 5, the technical heart of the program. The Loader is where customer data becomes VibeIQ entities — and where a large share of your escalations will originate. The goal this week is to read a failed load the way an engineer reads a stack trace: know the phase, know the artefact, know the field.

On this page

  1. ⚙️A load is an entity, not a command
  2. ⚙️The five phases
  3. ⚙️Validation: fail-fast, and no partial loads
  4. ⚙️The six preprocessing steps
  5. ⚙️The configuration, for real
  6. ⚙️How Family and Option are expressed in a CSV
  7. ⚙️The four flags that change what a load is
  8. ⚙️federatedId, with the mechanics
  9. ⚙️Transformers and images
  10. ⚙️Getting the file in, and CLI vs. API
  11. ⚙️What a load does to your workflows
  12. 📚Recommended reading
  13. ✅Practical exercise
  14. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  15. ✅Quiz
  16. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) name the five phases and six preprocessing steps and say what each produces, (2) read and write a loader configuration and predict its effect on a given CSV, (3) interpret a failed load from failureArea, issueType and the per-step artefacts, (4) explain the four configuration flags that change what a load fundamentally does — including one that silently bypasses the publish lifecycle — and (5) predict which workflows a load will set off, and when it won't.

Concept 1 — A load is an entity, not a command

A LoaderProcess is an asynchronous entity holding the metadata of an import: execution status, the configuration parameters used, downloadable process logs, and operational metadata. Creating one via API, SDK or CLI enqueues it.

Two fields make it a diagnostic instrument rather than a receipt:

  • failureArea — where it broke: NONE, PARSING_CSV, VALIDATING_DATA, or TRANSFORMING_DATA.
  • preprocessingStepResults — an array of file IDs, one per step, each holding the data as it stood after that step.

Ask for the process ID the way you would ask for a request ID, then read failureArea before anything else. It localises the failure to one of four segments in a single field — the cheapest diagnostic move available anywhere in the platform. When a customer says a load behaved differently than last week, compare the LoaderProcess records rather than taking anyone's word for it; the configuration used is recorded on each one.

Concept 2 — The five phases

  1. CSV parsing — "The CSV file is downloaded and parsed... Column headers from the first row are used as property keys." Failures here set PARSING_CSV.
  2. Validation — data checked against entity-type rules, before transformations. Failures set VALIDATING_DATA.
  3. Preprocessing — the six-step transformation sequence.
  4. Entity construction — rows become entity objects matching the schema, correctly typed.
  5. API upsert — create or update, matched by federatedId. Failures in 3–5 set TRANSFORMING_DATA.

Concept 3 — Validation: fail-fast, and no partial loads

The rule that shapes everything downstream

"If validation produces any errors, the load stops immediately with a VALIDATING_DATA failure area." Errors halt the job; warnings let it continue. No partial loads occur — you never get half a file applied. Most people assume the opposite, and the assumption changes how they plan a recovery.

Validation checks four categories — configuration completeness, required field availability, type path validity, and data integrity. Every load, regardless of entity type, requires a non-empty config, a loadType with at least one value, and both federatedId and name reachable "from csv headers, federatedMappings, or conditionalColumns."

The error taxonomy

issueTypeMeans
CONFIGConfiguration problem — missing mappings, invalid types
HEADER_MISMATCHCSV headers don't match entity properties
INVALID_DATAMalformed or invalid data
VALIDATION_ERRORData fails a validation check
DUPLICATE_FEDERATED_IDMultiple rows share a federated ID
SYSTEM_ERRORInternal processing error

A row-level issue looks like this:

{
  "rowNumber": 5,
  "columnIssues": [
    {
      "columnName": "itemFamilyFederatedId",
      "message": "itemFamilyFederatedId cannot be empty",
      "issueType": "CONFIG",
      "data": "{...row data...}"
    }
  ]
}

A file- or config-level issue carries no row number:

{
  "message": "typePath must be set to 'color' via csv data, conditionalColumn, or federatedMappings.",
  "issueType": "CONFIG"
}

Error messages worth recognising on sight: "itemFamilyFederatedId cannot be empty" (per-row) and "The following families have conflicting federated IDs: …" (job-level — the same family arriving with two different IDs).

Warnings that do not stop a load come from steps 4 and 6 (unrecognised columns, archived properties), option sets (values not matching defined options), size ranges (missing templates, invalid sizes), and object references (federated IDs with no matching entity). These accumulate in the step result files.

Fail-fast is good news you should say out loud. "Nothing was partially applied — the file was rejected as a whole" is reassuring, and it means the fix is fix-and-rerun rather than a reconciliation exercise. The corollary matters too: a load that reports completed genuinely applied everything it accepted, so if data looks wrong afterwards, the problem is in transformation or configuration — not in a half-finished job.

Concept 4 — The six preprocessing steps

#StepWhat it does
1Boolean conversionConverts "true"/"false" strings to real booleans — CSV parsers only ever produce strings.
2Federated mappingsFor each { targetProperty: sourceColumn }: if the source column exists, its value is copied to the target; if it doesn't, the literal value is set on all rows. Original columns are preserved.
3Remove unwanted propertiesDeletes the propertiesToRemove columns from every row.
4Type transformations (no warnings)Label-to-slug for headers, and display-to-value for Single/Multi Select backed by option sets.
5Conditional columnsApplies conditionalColumns to create or overwrite values, with {columnName} interpolation and JavaScript evaluation.
6Type transformations (with warnings)Repeats step 4, this time recording warnings for unmatched columns, disabled properties and invalid option values.

Why steps 4 and 6 are the same thing twice

Step 4 runs the type transformations silently, so conditional columns in step 5 can operate on already-normalised values. Step 6 re-runs them and reports. The consequence: a value can be silently altered in step 4 and never generate a warning if step 5 subsequently overwrites the column. When a value doesn't match the CSV and no warning exists, suspect the 4/5/6 interaction and read the artefacts rather than reasoning about it.

The artefacts are the whole game

Each step produces rows, errors and warnings, saved as loader-process-step-<Step Name>.json and listed in the preprocessingStepResults array on the LoaderProcess. You can see the data after every single step — diff consecutive files and the step that broke a value is unambiguous. Reach them via the Admin Console (attached files on the LoaderProcess), or by iterating preprocessingStepResults from the API or SDK. Most people argue about loads; you can just look.

Concept 5 — The configuration, for real

A load configuration is a JSON object. Here is a complete one that loads items with options into a project and splits them across two assortments — read it before the field table:

{
  "loadType": ["ITEM", "PROJECT_ITEM", "ASSORTMENT"],
  "fileLocation": "file-entity-id",
  "federatedMappings": {
    "name":                    "Style Name",
    "itemFamilyFederatedId":   "Style Number",
    "itemOptionFederatedId":   "SKU",
    "optionName":              "Color Name"
  },
  "conditionalColumns": [
    { "toProperty": "typePath",    "default": "item:product:apparel" },
    { "toProperty": "optionGroup", "default": "color" }
  ],
  "propertiesToRemove": ["internalNotes", "legacyCode"],
  "workspaceIdentifier": "fall:2025",
  "assortmentSplit": {
    "fieldToSplitOn": "gender",
    "values": [
      { "value": "mens",   "assortmentId": "assortment-id-1" },
      { "value": "womens", "assortmentId": "assortment-id-2" }
    ]
  }
}
FieldReq.What it does
loadTypeyesArray of entity types: ITEM, PROJECT_ITEM, ASSORTMENT, COLOR, CUSTOM_ENTITY, SIZE_RANGE_TEMPLATE. Only ITEM can be combined with others.
fileLocationAPI/SDKThe uploaded File entity ID. Set automatically by the CLI; must be provided manually otherwise.
federatedMappingsnoTarget property → source column, or a literal applied to every row.
conditionalColumnsnoCreates/overwrites columns from conditions or expressions.
propertiesToRemovenoColumns to strip — useful for killing columns that cause warnings or wrong mappings.
workspaceIdentifierwith PROJECT_ITEMThe project's identifier. See the warning below.
assortmentSplitwith ASSORTMENTRoutes rows to assortments by column value.
partialAssortmentUpdatenoDefault false. See Concept 7 — this one is dangerous.
assortmentItemDropFieldnoColumn marking items to drop during partial updates.
shouldSkipAssortmentPublishnoDefault false. See Concept 7 — this one is more dangerous.
loaderConfigurationIdnoID of a pre-uploaded YAML/JSON config, for reuse across loads.

conditionalColumns in detail

interface ColumnDefinition {
  toProperty: string;          // target column (overwrites if it exists)
  fromProperty?: string;       // copy from this column (ignored if conditions given)
  conditions?: ConditionalValues[];   // first match wins
  default?: any;               // fallback value, or a template expression
}

Three shapes you'll meet. A static value:

{ "toProperty": "typePath", "default": "item:product:apparel" }

Conditional logic — note first match wins, exactly like workflow paths in Week 6:

{
  "toProperty": "region",
  "conditions": [
    { "conditional": "{countryCode} === 'US' || {countryCode} === 'CA'", "value": "North America" },
    { "conditional": "{countryCode} === 'UK' || {countryCode} === 'FR'", "value": "Europe" }
  ],
  "default": "Other"
}

And a JavaScript expression in default — "strings containing { are evaluated as JavaScript template expressions":

{ "toProperty": "itemFamilyFederatedId", "default": "{familyNumber}.split('-')[0]" }

That last pattern — deriving the family ID by splitting a string — is common and quietly load-bearing. It means the federatedId is computed, so its stability now depends on the format of a source field nobody thinks of as an identifier. If someone changes familyNumber from ABC-123 to ABC_123, the upsert key changes for every row. Ask to see the conditionalColumns whenever you ask "what generates this ID?" — the answer is often in there rather than in the source system.

Concept 6 — How Family and Option are expressed in a CSV

Week 2 gave you Family and Option abstractly. Here is the concrete form, and it's simpler than expected: each row typically represents one option.

  • Rows sharing an itemFamilyFederatedId become options of the same family — the loader creates the parent family automatically if needed.
  • A row with itemOptionFederatedId maps to an option. A row without it represents a family only.
  • name is the family-level name, shared across options; optionName is that option's own name.
  • typePath is required on every row (e.g. item:product:apparel) — usually supplied by a conditional column rather than a CSV column.
  • optionGroup categorises the option axis, e.g. color.
style,styleName,colorName          →  {
SH-4471,Tech Fleece Hoodie,Black       "federatedMappings": {
SH-4471,Tech Fleece Hoodie,Navy          "itemFamilyFederatedId": "style",
SH-4471,Tech Fleece Hoodie,Gold          "itemOptionFederatedId": "colorName",
SH-9902,Trail Short,Black                "name":                  "styleName",
                                         "optionName":            "colorName"
Three rows → one family (SH-4471)     },
with three options; plus a second     "conditionalColumns": [
family (SH-9902) with one option.       { "default": "item:product:apparel", "toProperty": "typePath" },
                                        { "default": "color", "toProperty": "optionGroup" }
                                      ]}

Now Week 2's property levels become concrete. styleName is the same on all three Black/Navy/Gold rows — that is a Family property, and reloading it copies down. A colour code differs per row — Option. A price that is usually the style's but occasionally differs for one colourway — Override. You can read a customer's property-level model straight off their CSV by asking which columns repeat within a family and which vary. That is a five-minute exercise that predicts years of their tickets.

Concept 7 — The four flags that change what a load is

Most configuration fields shape how data is mapped. These four change the nature of the operation, and three of them do damage quietly.

1 · shouldSkipAssortmentPublish — the one that breaks integrations invisibly

When true, "assortment item updates are written directly to the database, bypassing the publish workflow. This skips change history, summary recalculation, composite assortment updates, and the assortment|publish event."

Read that list again. No AssortmentPublishChange. No change history. No assortment|publish event — so every downstream consumer, whether event-driven or polling change history, never learns the data moved. The load succeeds. VibeIQ is correct. PLM is stale, indefinitely, with nothing anywhere reporting a problem.

The flag has a legitimate purpose: the docs recommend it for a "high volume of changes" where "full history is not required" — a migration or backfill where you genuinely don't want a publish per row. The failure is that it gets set once "just for the migration" and stays in a reusable configuration forever. Put it on your pre-load checklist as a question with two parts: is this flag set, and is that still deliberate? Then: if it is set, who re-syncs the downstream system, and when?

2 · partialAssortmentUpdate — a default that deletes

Defaults to false. When false, an assortment load replaces the assortment's contents; when true, items are added and dropped rather than replaced.

So sending a partial file — say, only the 40 styles that changed — against the default configuration does not update 40 items. It leaves the assortment containing only those 40. The load reports success.

The full-load semantics, precisely: CSV items not in the assortment are added; items in both are updated if changed; unchanged matches are left unchanged; and "assortment items not in CSV → deleted."

Partial updates use two boolean columns: addField marks rows to add or update, dropField (or top-level assortmentItemDropField) marks rows to remove. One rule worth knowing: "items marked for add that already exist in the assortment are treated as updates instead," so an add on an existing item is safe rather than duplicating.

Style Name,addItem,dropItem,gender
Classic Tee,false,true,womens     → removed from the womens assortment
V-Neck Tee,true,false,womens      → added, or updated if already present
                                  → everything else in the assortment is untouched

3 · assortmentSplit — one file, many assortments

Routes rows to different assortments based on a column value — fieldToSplitOn plus a values array mapping each value to an assortmentId or assortmentIdentifier. "The Loader groups rows by the value in the fieldToSplitOn column. Each group is matched to an assortment from the values array." One load can therefore affect several assortments, and so trigger several downstream publishes.

The catch: "rows with fieldToSplitOn values not matching any entry are skipped." Not errored — skipped. A new region code, a typo, a trailing space, and those rows quietly never load while the job reports success. Any change to the vocabulary of that column is a silent data-loss event, so the values array needs to be reviewed whenever the source system gains a new category.

4 · workspaceIdentifier — and a genuine UI gap

Required whenever loadType includes PROJECT_ITEM, "even when also loading assortments." It matches a project's identifier exactly.

Two things to know. First, vocabulary: a "project" in the UI is a workspace with workspaceType: 'PROJECT' in the API — worth recognising when reading API responses or error messages. Second, the trap: the identifier "is not editable in the Hub UI; it must be set via direct PUT request to the workspace entity." So if a project's identifier is wrong or missing, an admin cannot fix it by clicking — someone has to make an API call.

Concept 8 — federatedId, with the mechanics

There is no "which column is my ID?" setting

"There is no configuration to say 'this column is my Federated ID'." The only exception is Custom Entity, and if none is specified there, "the out of the box name property will be used." For everything else the value must arrive under the expected property name — which in practice means federatedMappings or a conditionalColumn puts it there.

The stability rule, verbatim: "It is critical to ensure that federatedId values in your load files do not change between loads. Doing so will risk the Loader creating duplicate data."

And the same bug from another angle: entities created in the core apps have no federatedId. To update that data by Loader, values must be assigned manually through the API — the platform "doesn't synchronize property values with federatedId internally."

Three facts, one predictable story: the source system renumbers a style, the federatedId changes, the next load creates a second entity, and neither the load nor the app reports anything wrong. Note that validation does catch duplicates within a single file (DUPLICATE_FEDERATED_ID, and "conflicting federated IDs" for families) — but nothing catches a value that differs from last week's file. Your prevention question stays one sentence: "What generates this value, and what could ever cause it to change?" — and now you know to check conditionalColumns for the answer as well as the source system.

Concept 9 — Transformers and images

Beyond preprocessing, transformers do heavier reshaping. The documented set:

TransformerFor
Type Mapping · Mapping SectionStructural mapping of source shape to target
Map File Utility ClassLookups from a companion map file
RekeyingMove a value to a different key
RemoveDrop values
ValueAlter values
ConditionalApply changes on a condition
MorphThe general case — "can make any number of changes to the object that is passed in," for "situations where multiple changes will need to be made at a time or the key for the data value is variable." Configured with processor: 'MORPH' and functionTransformersKey.

Transformer choice is a maintainability question you should weigh in on. Morph can express anything, which means it can also hide everything: a customer who does all their reshaping in one Morph has a load nobody can review or hand over. Prefer the specific transformers where they fit, and treat a growing Morph as technical debt with a support cost attached.

Images have three documented approaches, worth knowing exist because image loading is where volume and latency problems show up: programmatic loading, federated load from file, and the zip extractor. A customer sending thousands of images per season should not be discovering these options after go-live.

Concept 10 — Getting the file in, and CLI vs. API

Upload is two calls: create a File entity, which returns an uploadPost containing an S3 URL and form parameters; then POST the binary to that S3 URL. The SDK's createAndUploadFileFromBuffer() wraps both and returns a File entity with an id — which becomes fileLocation.

CLIAPI / SDK
Commandcontrail load upload-and-load <File> <Config>Upload file, then create the LoaderProcess
fileLocationSet automaticallyYou must provide it
Stored configsNot available — loaderConfigurationId unsupportedSupported
Good forLocal files, one-offs, testingProduction and automation

A real drift risk

Because the CLI cannot use a stored configuration, anyone loading from the CLI passes a local copy that drifts from the stored one. The symptom is common and maddening: "the same file loads differently depending on who runs it." When you hear it, compare the configuration recorded on each LoaderProcess before investigating anything else — and pay particular attention to whether the flags in Concept 7 differ.

Concept 11 — What a load does to your workflows

A load is an event storm — usually

Phase 5 upserts entities, so a load emits ordinary system events: item|create for new rows, item|update for existing ones, and likewise for project-item and assortment-item. Every event workflow bound to those triggers fires, once per row. A 20,000-row load is 20,000 trigger evaluations.

…except when it deliberately isn't

With shouldSkipAssortmentPublish: true, the assortment|publish event is not emitted. So the storm is selective: entity-level workflows still fire, but anything hanging off publish — including the outbound integration workflow from Week 5 — stays silent. This is why "we loaded it and PLM never got it" can be true with no failure anywhere. Check the flag before you check the consumer.

Three consequences to hold together:

  1. Create vs. update changes behaviour. From Week 6, {oldData} is absent on creates, so a conditional like {newData.status} !== {oldData.status} evaluates differently for new rows than existing ones in the very same load. A load can therefore notify for part of a file and not the rest — which looks like a bug and is not.
  2. Concurrency ceilings are shared. A bulk load can saturate the org's 60-process budget, starving unrelated automation. "Our other integration got slow on Tuesday" is often "someone ran a big load on Tuesday."
  3. Races become likely precisely when load is heaviest. A workflow with a high parallelWorkerCount and no message grouping may be fine daily and corrupt data during a bulk load, because that is when colliding events actually overlap.

Before any large or first-time load, ask: which workflows are bound to these entity types, what do they do on create, is anything grouped, and is publish being skipped? If the answer involves notifications or outbound sync, plan for it — deactivate, narrow the conditional, or schedule deliberately. "The load worked but it emailed 3,000 people" and "the load worked but nothing reached PLM" are both avoidable, and avoiding them is exactly what a TAM is valued for.

  • Getting Started — LoaderProcess, upload, CLI vs API.

  • Preprocessing Pipeline — the six steps and the per-step artefacts.
  • Load Items — the family/option CSV mechanics in Concept 6.

Practical exercise

You're handed a CSV with headers: style, Style Name, colorName, Active ("true"/"false"), legacy_code, Retail Price, gender.

Requirements: load items with options into the fall:2025 project; drop legacy_code; copy Retail Price to msrp; set typePath to item:product:apparel; derive a region column as "EMEA" when gender is womens and "Global" otherwise; and split rows into a mens and a womens assortment.

  1. Write the complete configuration JSON.
  2. Trace one row through all six preprocessing steps, writing what it looks like after each.
  3. Name the two steps where something could go wrong without a warning.
  4. Your file contains only the 120 styles that changed this week. What happens with your configuration as written — and what one field changes the answer?
The answer

1 · The configuration

{
  "loadType": ["ITEM", "PROJECT_ITEM", "ASSORTMENT"],
  "fileLocation": "<file-entity-id>",
  "workspaceIdentifier": "fall:2025",
  "federatedMappings": {
    "name":                   "Style Name",
    "itemFamilyFederatedId":  "style",
    "itemOptionFederatedId":  "colorName",
    "optionName":             "colorName",
    "msrp":                   "Retail Price"
  },
  "propertiesToRemove": ["legacy_code"],
  "conditionalColumns": [
    { "toProperty": "typePath",    "default": "item:product:apparel" },
    { "toProperty": "optionGroup", "default": "color" },
    { "toProperty": "region",
      "value": "{gender} === 'womens' ? 'EMEA' : 'Global'" }
  ],
  "assortmentSplit": {
    "fieldToSplitOn": "gender",
    "splits": [
      { "value": "mens",   "assortmentName": "FA25 Mens"   },
      { "value": "womens", "assortmentName": "FA25 Womens" }
    ]
  }
}

Two things to notice. workspaceIdentifier is mandatory because loadType includes PROJECT_ITEM — required "even when also loading assortments." And Active needs no configuration at all: step 1 converts "true"/"false" strings to real booleans automatically.

2 · One row through the six steps. Starting row:

style=SH-4471 · Style Name=Tech Fleece Hoodie · colorName=Black
Active="false" · legacy_code=OLD-99 · Retail Price=89 · gender=womens
  • 1 · Boolean conversion — Active becomes the boolean false, not the string "false". Everything else unchanged.
  • 2 · Federated mappings — target properties are added; source columns are preserved. Now also carries name=Tech Fleece Hoodie, itemFamilyFederatedId=SH-4471, itemOptionFederatedId=Black, optionName=Black, msrp=89.
  • 3 · Remove unwanted properties — legacy_code is deleted from the row.
  • 4 · Type transformations (no warnings) — header labels resolve to slugs, and any Single/Multi Select display values resolve to their option-set values. If gender is option-set backed, womens is normalised here. Silently — this pass reports nothing.
  • 5 · Conditional columns — typePath=item:product:apparel and optionGroup=color are set from defaults; region is evaluated: gender is womens, so region=EMEA.
  • 6 · Type transformations (with warnings) — the same pass re-runs over the now-complete row, including the values step 5 just created, and this time emits warnings. Final row: style, Style Name, colorName, Active=false, Retail Price, gender, name, itemFamilyFederatedId, itemOptionFederatedId, optionName, msrp, typePath, optionGroup, region=EMEA.

3 · The two steps where something goes wrong without a warning

  • Step 4 — type transformations without warnings. A display value that does not match its option set is altered or dropped here and reports nothing, by design. This pass exists to normalise silently so step 5 operates on clean data.
  • Step 5 — conditional columns. These create or overwrite values. A value quietly changed in step 4 and then overwritten in step 5 never surfaces in step 6's warnings either, because step 6 only sees the final state. The 4→5→6 interaction is the blind spot: when a value does not match the CSV and there is no warning anywhere, this is where to look. Diff the step artefacts rather than theorising.

Also worth naming, though it is not a step: a CSV column whose header matches nothing on the entity type is ignored entirely, with no warning.

4 · The 120-changed-styles file

As written, the load deletes every product not in the file from both assortments. partialAssortmentUpdate is absent and defaults to false, which means replace: items in the CSV are added or updated, and "assortment items not in CSV → deleted." Membership is defined by the file. The load reports success.

The one field that changes the answer is partialAssortmentUpdate: true, which makes it a merge — paired with boolean addField/dropField columns when you need explicit removals.

How to think about it

Read a configuration as an ordered pipeline, not a settings object. Almost every Loader surprise comes from step order: mappings add before removal runs, conditional columns overwrite whatever preceded them, and the two type-transformation passes bracket step 5 for a reason. If you can say what a row looks like after each step, you can predict the outcome of any config — which is the actual skill this exercise builds.

Why type transformations run twice. Step 4 normalises quietly so that step 5's JavaScript operates on clean, resolved values rather than raw display labels. Step 6 then re-runs the same pass over the finished row and reports. The design is sound; the consequence is a gap where a silent change can be overwritten before anything gets a chance to warn about it.

Question 4 is the point of the whole exercise. Everything above it is mechanics; this is the one that costs a customer their assortment. A partial file plus a default flag is a destructive operation that reports success — no error, no warning, correct-looking status. Ask what a typical file contains before you ever look at what the config says, because the two are only dangerous in combination.

The derived region column is safe; a derived identifier would not be. Deriving a descriptive value in a conditional column is ordinary. Deriving itemFamilyFederatedId the same way makes your upsert key depend on the formatting of a field nobody treats as an identifier — change ABC-123 to ABC_123 upstream and the next load duplicates everything. Same mechanism, completely different blast radius.

Prefer evidence over reconstruction. When a value is wrong, you do not have to reason about which step did it — every step writes loader-process-step-<name>.json with rows, errors and warnings, and the file IDs sit in preprocessingStepResults on the LoaderProcess. Diff two consecutive steps and the culprit is unambiguous. This converts an argument about what the loader did into an observation.

What a weak answer looks like: a config that works plus "it would only load the 120 changed styles." That is the intuitive answer and it is backwards, and the gap between intuition and the default is exactly why this is on the checklist.

Sources
  • Loader Configuration Reference — every field used above, with defaults — including partialAssortmentUpdate, assortmentSplit and the workspaceIdentifier requirement. The densest page for this module.
  • Preprocessing Pipeline — the six steps in order, why the type pass runs twice, and the per-step artefacts.
  • Load Items — the family/option CSV mechanics — which columns make a row an option, and automatic family creation.
  • Validation Rules & Error Handling — the error/warning split and the failure areas, for reading the result afterwards.

Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic, mid-season. Their integration engineer writes:

"Last night's load reported completed with no errors. But roughly 400 items now have a blank merchCategory, and about 90 look like duplicates of styles that already existed. Our FlexPLM team says they've had nothing from VibeIQ for three days even though we've loaded every night. And our Slack channel got 1,200 'item approved' messages at 2am, which has never happened before. Nothing changed in our export — we send the same file format we've sent for six months."

Four symptoms, and they are not one root cause. For each: a hypothesis, and the exact artefact, field or record you'd pull to confirm it — name them precisely (failureArea, a step file, a config field, a Process List filter). Then: the one question to their engineer that could resolve two symptoms at once; which symptom you'd chase first and why; and what you'd say about "nothing changed in our export" being simultaneously true and beside the point.

The answer

Symptom 1 — ~400 items with blank merchCategory

Hypothesis: the column stopped matching. Either the header was renamed upstream, or an option-set value no longer resolves, so the value is dropped in step 4 or overwritten in step 5. Unmatched columns are ignored with no warning at all.

Evidence: the preprocessingStepResults array on the LoaderProcess. Pull the step artefacts and diff step 3 against step 4, and step 4 against step 6 for one affected item. The step at which merchCategory went empty is then a fact, not a theory. Also compare the CSV header row against the previous night's file.

Symptom 2 — ~90 duplicates of existing styles

Hypothesis: federatedId instability. Either the upstream value changed format for those rows, or it is derived in a conditionalColumn from a field that changed. The 90 are the rows whose key moved; the rest were unaffected because their key did not.

Evidence: the conditionalColumns block of the configuration recorded on the LoaderProcess, then the itemFamilyFederatedId values in the step 5 artefact for the duplicated styles, compared against the federatedId on the pre-existing records. If those differ by formatting — a dash for an underscore, a case change, a trimmed prefix — that is the whole answer.

Symptom 3 — FlexPLM has had nothing for three days

Hypothesis: shouldSkipAssortmentPublish: true on the loader configuration. Assortment updates are written straight to the database, bypassing the publish workflow — no change history, no AssortmentPublishChange, and no assortment|publish event, so no downstream consumer of any kind learns anything moved.

Evidence: the flag itself, read from the configuration stored on the LoaderProcess (the config used, not the one someone shows you). Confirm by filtering the Process List for the outbound integration workflow over those three days — expected result is no processes at all, which is different from failed ones and is the signature of this flag.

Symptom 4 — 1,200 "item approved" Slack messages at 2am

Hypothesis: an event storm. Phase 5 upserts emit item|create/item|update per row, and a workflow bound to those triggers fires once per row. Either a workflow was newly bound, or a previously-narrow conditional now matches broadly — plausibly because the same field that broke symptom 1 now evaluates differently.

Evidence: the Process List filtered to that workflow and time window — count the processes and read triggerEvent on a few. Then the workflow's conditional against the step 6 artefact, to see why it matched.

The one question that resolves two symptoms at once:

"Can you send me last night's CSV and the one from the last night everything was fine — the files themselves, not a description?"

A header diff settles symptom 1 (renamed or removed column) and symptom 2 (changed identifier format) in a single comparison, because both are upstream payload changes and both are invisible from inside VibeIQ.

Which symptom first: number 3. The other three are wrong data inside VibeIQ, visible to the customer, bounded and repairable. Symptom 3 means three days of correct decisions have not reached the system that makes product, and every day it continues the gap widens and the reconciliation grows. It is also the cheapest to confirm — one field on a config. Highest consequence, lowest cost, and it is silent, which means nobody else is going to find it.

On "nothing changed in our export":

"I believe you — and I think it is true about the format and not about the content. The file still has the same shape, so nothing on your side flagged a change. What I need to check is whether a header or a value changed inside that shape, because the loader matches on exact header names and drops what it doesn't recognise without complaining. That is why I'm asking for the two files rather than the spec."
How to think about it

Resist the single-root-cause instinct. Four symptoms arriving together feel like one cause, and the pull to unify them is strong. Here there are at least three: a payload change (1 and 2), a configuration flag (3), and a workflow binding (4). Forcing them into one story means three of the four go unfixed while you defend it. Enumerate first, connect only where evidence connects them.

"Completed with no errors" is information, not reassurance. Because validation is fail-fast and there are no partial loads, a completed load genuinely applied everything it accepted. So the fault is not a half-finished job — it is in what the load accepted and transformed. That single inference eliminates an entire branch of investigation and is why the rule is worth memorising.

Name artefacts, not areas. "Check the logs" is not a plan. preprocessingStepResults, the config recorded on the LoaderProcess, a Process List filter, failureArea — each is a specific thing you can ask for and read. The discipline matters most when you escalate: a ticket saying "diffed steps 4 and 6, value lost at 4" starts the next person where you stopped.

Prioritise by consequence and silence, not by volume. The 1,200 Slack messages are the loudest symptom and among the least harmful. The stale PLM is invisible and is the one costing money. Loud problems get reported by the customer; quiet ones are your job precisely because nobody else will find them.

"Nothing changed" is almost always sincere and almost always incomplete. It means the export job did not change. It says nothing about the upstream system that feeds it — a renamed field in PLM, a new option value, an ID reformatted by an admin. Arguing with the statement is a mistake; reframing it from "did anything change?" to "let us compare the two files" gets you the answer without making anyone wrong.

The connective tissue worth spotting: symptoms 1 and 4 may share a cause. If a field the workflow conditional depends on stopped resolving, the conditional can start matching rows it used to skip. Two symptoms, one upstream change — but assert it only after the artefacts show it.

Sources
  • Validation Rules & Error Handling — failureArea, the error/warning split, and the fail-fast rule that makes "completed" meaningful.
  • Preprocessing Pipeline — the per-step artefacts and preprocessingStepResults — the diffing technique behind symptoms 1 and 2.
  • Loader Configuration Reference — shouldSkipAssortmentPublish and exactly what it bypasses.
  • Observing Workflows — the Process List filters used for symptoms 3 and 4, and the 90-day retention that bounds how long this remains investigable.
  • Key findings — Failures that produce no error — three of these four symptoms are on that list.

Quiz

  1. What is a LoaderProcess, and which field do you read before any other when a load fails?

    A LoaderProcess is an asynchronous entity holding a load's status, the configuration parameters used, downloadable logs and operational metadata — creating one enqueues the work. Because it's an entity, a load is inspectable and comparable rather than a fire-and-forget command. Read failureArea first: it localises the failure to one of four segments in a single field, which is the cheapest diagnostic move available anywhere in the platform.

  2. Name the five phases. What are the four failureArea values and which phases map to each?

    Phases: (1) CSV parsing, (2) validation, (3) preprocessing, (4) entity construction, (5) API upsert by federatedId. failureArea values: NONE (success), PARSING_CSV (phase 1 — malformed or empty CSV), VALIDATING_DATA (phase 2 — config or data validation), TRANSFORMING_DATA (phases 3–5 — preprocessing or entity construction).

  3. Does a failed load apply partially? Quote the rule, and say why the answer changes how you plan a recovery.

    No. "If validation produces any errors, the load stops immediately with a VALIDATING_DATA failure area," and "no partial loads occur" — errors halt, warnings accumulate and let the load continue. This changes recovery planning completely: a rejected file needs fixing and rerunning, not a reconciliation exercise to work out which rows landed. The corollary is equally useful — a load that reports completed genuinely applied everything it accepted, so if data looks wrong afterwards the cause is transformation or configuration, not a half-finished job.

  4. Distinguish errors from warnings. Give two examples of each.

    Errors stop the entire job; warnings let it continue. Error examples: "itemFamilyFederatedId cannot be empty" (row-level), and "The following families have conflicting federated IDs" (job-level) — also missing federatedId/name, or an invalid typePath. Warning examples: unrecognised or archived columns flagged in steps 4 and 6, option-set values not matching defined options, missing size-range templates, and object references whose federated IDs match no entity. Warnings accumulate in the step result files — they are the ones you must go and look for.

  5. List the six preprocessing steps. Why do type transformations run twice, and what can hide in the gap?

    (1) Boolean conversion, (2) federated mappings, (3) remove unwanted properties, (4) type transformations without warnings, (5) conditional columns, (6) type transformations with warnings. They run twice so that step 4 normalises values silently — letting conditional columns in step 5 operate on already-clean data — and step 6 then re-runs and reports. What hides in the gap: a value silently altered in step 4 and then overwritten by step 5 never generates a warning at all. So when a value doesn't match the CSV and there is no warning anywhere, suspect the 4/5/6 interaction.

  6. How do you get to the per-step artefacts from the API, and what would you do with two of them?

    Each step writes loader-process-step-<Step Name>.json containing rows, errors and warnings, and the file IDs are listed in the preprocessingStepResults array on the LoaderProcess — fetch it and iterate, or view the attached files on the LoaderProcess in the Admin Console. With two of them: diff consecutive steps. The step at which a value changed is then unambiguous, which converts an argument about what the loader did into an observation.

  7. In a CSV of items with options: which columns make a row an option rather than a family, and what happens to the family?

    A row becomes an option when it carries itemOptionFederatedId (usually with optionName and optionGroup); without it, the row represents a family only. Rows sharing the same itemFamilyFederatedId become options of the same family — and the loader creates the parent family automatically if it doesn't exist. name is the family-level name shared across options; typePath is required on every row, usually supplied by a conditional column.

  8. Explain shouldSkipAssortmentPublish. Why is it the most dangerous flag in the reference?

    When true, assortment item updates are written directly to the database, bypassing the publish workflow — skipping change history, summary recalculation, composite assortment updates and the assortment|publish event. It's the most dangerous flag because the failure is completely silent and asymmetric: the load succeeds, VibeIQ's data is correct, and every downstream consumer — event-driven or polling change history — never learns anything moved. PLM goes stale indefinitely with nothing reporting a problem. It has a legitimate use (high-volume backfills where history isn't required); the danger is that it gets set "just for the migration" and stays in a reusable config forever.

  9. A customer sends a file containing only the 40 styles that changed. What does the default partialAssortmentUpdate do?

    It replaces the assortment with those 40 styles. partialAssortmentUpdate defaults to false, and a full load's semantics are: CSV items not in the assortment are added, items in both are updated, unchanged matches stay unchanged, and "assortment items not in CSV → deleted." So membership is defined by the file. The load reports success. Setting partialAssortmentUpdate: true with boolean addField/dropField columns is what makes it a merge instead.

  10. What is workspaceIdentifier, when is it required, and why might an admin be unable to fix a wrong one?

    workspaceIdentifier is the identifier of the target project, and it's required whenever loadType includes PROJECT_ITEM — "even when also loading assortments." The loader resolves it by finding a workspace with workspaceType: 'PROJECT' whose identifier matches exactly (a "project" in the UI is a workspace in the API). An admin may be unable to fix a wrong one because the property "is not editable in the Hub UI; it must be set via direct PUT request to the workspace entity" — so it takes an API call, not a click.

  11. A conditionalColumn derives itemFamilyFederatedId by splitting another field. What risk has that introduced?

    It has made the upsert key depend on the formatting of a field nobody treats as an identifier. With something like "{familyNumber}.split('-')[0]", the federatedId is computed — so if the source system changes ABC-123 to ABC_123, the derived key changes for every row and the next load creates duplicates of everything. It also means "what generates this ID?" can't be answered by looking at the source system alone; you have to read the conditionalColumns too.

  12. A 20,000-row load runs. Which triggers fire and how many times? Name the flag that makes part of that answer wrong.

    Phase 5 upserts entities, so the load emits ordinary system events — item|create for new rows and item|update for existing ones, plus the equivalents for project-item and assortment-item. Every workflow bound to those triggers fires once per row: 20,000 rows is 20,000 trigger evaluations. The flag is shouldSkipAssortmentPublish — when set, assortment|publish is not emitted, so entity-level workflows still fire while anything hanging off publish (including the outbound integration) stays completely silent.

Hands-on challenge

Write a pre-load checklist for any customer's first production load or any unusually large one. Cover: federatedId source and stability (including anything computed in conditionalColumns), column-name matching, option-set label alignment, the four flags in Concept 7 and whether each is still deliberate, which workflows are bound to the affected entity types and what they do on create, org concurrency headroom, and how you'd verify success afterwards using failureArea and the per-step artefacts rather than trusting a "completed" status. This joins your Week 2 data-model health check, Week 3 publish checklist and Week 6 workflow review.

The answer

Pre-load checklist. For any first production load, any unusually large one, and any load run with a configuration nobody has read this quarter.

A · Identity — get this wrong and nothing else matters

  • What system generates federatedId, and what could ever change it? Reformatting, re-casing, a trimmed prefix, a recategorisation.
  • Is any identifier computed in a conditionalColumn? If so, the upsert key now depends on the formatting of a field nobody treats as an identifier. Read the expression and ask what breaks it.
  • Do records already exist that were created in the UI? They have no federatedId by default, so this load will duplicate rather than update them. Assign IDs first.
  • Have IDs changed since the last successful load? Diff the key column between the two files.

B · The file against the configuration

  • Diff this file's header row against the last known-good file. Unmatched columns are ignored with no warning.
  • Do Single/Multi Select values match their option sets exactly? Mismatches are altered or dropped in step 4, silently.
  • Is typePath present on every row — usually via a conditional column?
  • Is workspaceIdentifier set and correct, if loadType includes PROJECT_ITEM? Remember it is not editable in the Hub UI; fixing it takes a PUT.
  • Does every assortmentSplit value in the data match a configured split? Rows whose split value matches nothing are skipped silently.

C · The four flags — is each one still deliberate?

  • partialAssortmentUpdate — defaults to false (replace). Cross-check against what this file actually contains: a delta file plus the default deletes everything not in it. This pairing is the single most destructive combination in the reference.
  • shouldSkipAssortmentPublish — if true, this load will be invisible downstream: no change history, no AssortmentPublishChange, no assortment|publish event. Ask who set it and when. "For the migration" plus a reusable config is how it becomes permanent.
  • The remaining two flags from Concept 7 — same question for each: who decided this, when, and is the reason still true?
  • If any flag is set for this run only, is there a plan to unset it, and is that plan written down anywhere other than someone's memory?

D · Blast radius — what this load will set off

  • Which workflows are bound to item, project-item and assortment-item triggers? Every one fires once per row.
  • What do they do on create versus update? New rows and existing rows take different paths — a notification workflow that is fine on update can page a channel 20,000 times on create.
  • Do any of them call an external system? Row count is now a load test on someone else's API, and on their rate limits.
  • Concurrency headroom: the org has 60 concurrent processes shared across everything. What else runs at this hour? A large load at the same time as a nightly integration is how both get slow and one starts timing out.
  • Is anything grouped by messageGroupId in a way this volume will serialise into a queue that takes hours?

E · Verification afterwards — do not trust "completed"

  • Read failureArea first. NONE means the job finished, not that the data is right.
  • Pull the step artefacts from preprocessingStepResults and read the warnings — they accumulate without stopping the job, and nobody goes looking for them by default.
  • Spot-check three rows end to end: one new, one updated, one that exercises a conditional column. Compare the final artefact against the source row.
  • Confirm the count. Rows in the file versus entities created/updated — a silent skip shows up here and nowhere else.
  • If assortments were touched: did an AssortmentPublishChange get created, and did the downstream consumer process it? A successful load with a silent publish is the failure this whole checklist exists to prevent.
  • Check the Process List for the workflows in section D — count and failures.

F · Before you start

  • Has this configuration run successfully before, on this org? If it came from another environment, what differs — option sets, type paths, workspace identifiers?
  • Is anyone loading from the CLI? The CLI cannot use a stored configuration, so that person is passing a local copy that drifts from production.
  • Is there a rollback story? For a replace-mode assortment load there effectively is not, which is itself the argument for section C.
How to think about it

The checklist is ordered by cost of being wrong, not by pipeline order. Identity first, because a bad federatedId produces duplicates that are expensive and awkward to clean up. Flags next, because they are the destructive ones. Verification last, because it only matters if you got here.

Section C is the heart of it, and the question is deliberately not "what is this flag set to?" It is "is it still deliberate?" These flags are almost always set correctly at the time and then outlive their reason. A migration ends; the config gets reused; nobody re-reads it. Asking who and when surfaces that in a way that asking what never does.

Section D is the part most pre-load checks omit entirely. People check the file and the config and forget the load is an event storm. Twenty thousand rows is twenty thousand trigger evaluations, against a shared org-wide concurrency budget, potentially hammering a customer's external endpoint. The load can be flawless and still take down the integration for a day.

Section E exists because "completed" is the most misleading word in the platform. Fail-fast validation means a completed load applied everything it accepted — which is genuinely reassuring about partial application and says nothing about transformation. Warnings accumulate quietly in the step artefacts and nobody reads them unless a checklist says to. Make verification a step with a name, or it does not happen.

Cross-checks beat individual checks. The valuable items above pair two facts: the flag and what the file contains; the row count and the entity count; the workflow binding and its create-path behaviour. Individually each is fine. Neither of the two most damaging Loader outcomes is visible from one fact alone.

Write it for someone else to run. The point of an artefact is that it works without you in the room — a customer's integration engineer should be able to walk it before a Friday load. That constraint is what forces the items to be observable rather than judgement calls, and it is what makes this join the inheritance kit as a real deliverable.

Sources
  • Loader Configuration Reference — sections B and C — every field and default, including the four flags.
  • Preprocessing Pipeline — section E — the per-step artefacts and where warnings accumulate.
  • Validation Rules & Error Handling — failureArea, and the fail-fast/no-partial-loads rule behind section E.
  • Data Loading — Getting Started — section A — federatedId as the upsert key, and CLI vs. API for section F.
  • Creating Workflows — section D — trigger bindings, create-vs-update paths, message grouping and the concurrency settings.

I know this when I can…

  • Recite the five phases and six steps, and name the artefact each step leaves behind.
  • Read a loader configuration and predict its effect on a specific CSV row.
  • Read failureArea and an issueType and know where to look next.
  • Find the exact step that altered a value by diffing artefacts rather than theorising.
  • Explain all three federatedId failure modes, including a computed one.
  • Name the flag that makes a successful load invisible downstream, and the default that deletes.
  • Predict a load's workflow side-effects before running it — and say which will differ between new and existing rows.
