Module 5 · Week 5

Data out: integration patterns

The other half of the technical heart. Week 4 was data arriving; this is data leaving, and it is the seam customers actually break. By the end you should be able to sit in an architecture review, read someone's integration design, and name what will fail in production before it does.

On this page

  1. ⚙️Choosing the pattern
  2. ⚙️Authentication and the API surface
  3. ⚙️The event-driven path, end to end
  4. ⚙️The polling path, and the seam
  5. ⚙️Designing a consumer that survives contact with reality
  6. ⚙️Webhook security, in both directions
  7. 🤝Attribute ownership, the artefact that prevents the arguments
  8. 📚Recommended reading
  9. ✅Practical exercise
  10. ✅Realistic customer scenario — an architecture review
  11. ✅Quiz
  12. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) choose between event-driven and polling for a stated requirement and defend it, (2) trace the full event-driven path from assortment|publish to rows in the customer's system, naming every call, (3) explain why temporal polling drops or duplicates records at the seam and how to guard it, (4) review a consumer design and find the failure modes, and (5) hold a credible security conversation about API keys and webhooks in both directions.

Concept 1 — Choosing the pattern

AspectChange History PollingAssortment Publish Events
Trigger"Time-based (scheduled)""Event-based (on publish)"
Latency"Minutes to hours""Near real-time"
Complexity"Simple to implement""Requires webhook endpoint"
State livesIn the consumer (a timestamp)In the delivery (an event)
ScopeAny entity typeAssortment publishes only

Polling is right for batch sync on a schedule, tracking multiple entity types, historical retrieval, and systems with no webhook support. Events are right for real-time needs, assortment-specific workflows, webhook-capable systems, and minimising API calls.

The scope row is the one that decides most real architectures, and it is easy to miss. Publish events cover assortment publishes. If the customer also needs item-level changes that never go through a publish — a colour corrected, a description fixed — events alone will not carry them. Many customers end up running both: events for publish latency, polling as the safety net and for everything else. When someone insists on one mechanism only, ask what happens to changes that never publish.

Concept 2 — Authentication and the API surface

Base URL: https://api.vibeiq.com/prod/api. Every REST request carries two headers:

curl --location 'https://api.vibeiq.com/prod/api/items/item-id-123' \
  --header 'X-Api-Key: app:uEL7GCBZ7Xhd4w2z' \
  --header 'X-Api-Org: your-org-slug'

Keys come from contrail app getApiKey and look like app:uEL7GCBZ7Xhd4w2z — the app: prefix marks them as application-scoped rather than user credentials. "VibeIQ can provide an access token directly if using the CLI is not preferred."

CodeMeaningWhat to actually check
401UnauthorizedKey validity, expiry, and — the one people miss — whether X-Api-Org matches the org the key belongs to
404Not foundGenuinely absent, or access denied, or wrong entity type. A 404 here is not proof the record doesn't exist
429Rate limitedBack off exponentially, reduce frequency, batch

Rate limits are real but undisclosed

"VibeIQ APIs are rate-limited to ensure system stability," with no published numbers; customers exceeding them should "contact VibeIQ support to discuss higher limits." So you cannot promise a throughput figure, and you must insist consumers implement exponential backoff rather than assuming headroom. And from Week 1 — the "15 requests/hour" figure circulating online belongs to vibe.co, a different company. Never cite it.

Key hygiene, per the docs: never commit keys to source control; use environment variables or secrets management; keep separate keys per environment; prefer app keys over user keys; and rotate regularly, generating new keys and revoking old ones.

Separate keys per environment is worth pushing hard on, because it composes with a Week 6 hazard: a workflow action's config can point at any endpoint. One shared key across environments plus a promoted workflow config is how staging writes to production. Two controls, one failure — and the customer will experience it as data corruption, not as a credentials problem.

Concept 3 — The event-driven path, end to end

Publishing emits an assortment|publish event whose payload is deliberately thin:

{
  "assortmentId": "25uDvGNpYhZHw2nt",
  "assortmentPublishChangeId": "lTuARQLrhgWHNr3k"
}

The mechanism is a workflow

"Configure an Event Workflow in VibeIQ to forward events to your endpoint." The outbound integration is a Week 6 workflow — triggerKey: assortment|publish, with an action that POSTs to the customer's URL. Everything you learned about paths, conditionals, access grants, retries and the Process List applies directly to debugging an integration. "The event never arrived" is a workflow investigation, and you already know how to run it.

You receive the two IDs, then fetch the full change object:

curl --location \
  'https://api.vibeiq.com/prod/api/assortments/25uDvGNpYhZHw2nt/history/lTuARQLrhgWHNr3k' \
  --header 'X-Api-Key: your-api-key' \
  --header 'X-Api-Org: your-org-slug'

Or via the SDK:

const changeHistory = await new Entities().get({
  entityName: 'assortment',
  id: '25uDvGNpYhZHw2nt',
  suffix: 'history/lTuARQLrhgWHNr3k'
});

Then download the baseline — note it takes the same auth headers:

const baselineUrl = changeHistory.assortmentBaselineDownloadLink;
const baselineResponse = await fetch(baselineUrl, {
  headers: { 'X-Api-Key': '…', 'X-Api-Org': '…' }
});
const baselineData = await baselineResponse.json();

for (const assortmentItem of baselineData.assortmentItems) {
  const item        = assortmentItem.item;
  const itemFamily  = assortmentItem.item.itemFamily;
  const projectItem = assortmentItem.projectItem;
  // map to your system's data model
}

Week 2 is sitting right there in the payload

Look at the loop. Each assortmentItem exposes .item, .item.itemFamily and .projectItem — the AssortmentItem / Item / Family / ProjectItem layers, in one object. Every mapping decision the customer makes here is a decision about which layer is authoritative for which attribute. A team that never learned the layer model will map from whichever field is populated, and produce a downstream system that is subtly wrong in ways nobody can explain. This is the single highest-leverage moment for you to be in the room.

The documented consumer sequence: receive the webhook → fetch the change object → download and parse the baseline → extract assortmentItems → transform to your schema → persist → store the createdOn timestamp for future change tracking.

Concept 4 — The polling path, and the seam

Polling hits /api/change-history "for changes to entities within a specified time range." The consumer stores a last-sync timestamp, builds a date-range criterion from it, polls, processes, and stores the new timestamp. Filtering is temporal — createdOn accepts a BETWEEN 2024-01-01 and 2024-12-31 form — and "this approach gives you full control over when and how frequently you retrieve changes."

Temporal filtering is not a cursor, and the difference is the bug

A cursor is a position in an ordered sequence: resume from it and you cannot miss or repeat. A timestamp is a guess about a position, and three things break it:

  • Boundary exclusivity. Poll [lastSync, now] and records at exactly lastSync are processed twice; poll (lastSync, now] and any record written in the same instant as your cutoff is lost forever.
  • In-flight writes. A record whose createdOn falls just before now but which commits just after your query runs is never returned by this poll, and is before the cutoff on the next one. It vanishes silently.
  • Clock skew. The consumer's clock and the server's clock are not the same clock. A consumer that computes now locally can skip a window.

The guards, in order of importance:

  1. Overlap the window. Poll from lastSync − safetyMargin (a minute or two), not from lastSync. Deliberately re-fetch a little.
  2. Make processing idempotent. Overlap only helps if reprocessing the same record is harmless. Key on entity ID plus a change identifier and no-op on repeats.
  3. Advance the timestamp from the data, not the clock. Use the maximum createdOn actually returned, not the local time at which you polled.
  4. Never advance on partial failure. If processing threw halfway, the timestamp must not move.

The symptom this produces is the most frustrating you will meet: occasional missing records, no errors anywhere, and no reproducibility. Both systems look healthy. When a customer reports "we're missing maybe one in a few hundred, we can't work out which," stop looking at the data and go read how their polling window is computed. Almost every time, it is exclusive boundaries or a locally-computed now.

Concept 5 — Designing a consumer that survives contact with reality

Pulling Weeks 3–5 together. A production consumer must handle all six of these:

HazardWhy it bitesThe design answer
S3 links expire in 24hA stored URL is dead tomorrowPersist the change ID; re-fetch the change object to get fresh links
Deletes don't accumulateMiss one publish and those deletes never reappearTrack processed change IDs; on a gap, re-baseline rather than resume
DowntimeEvents delivered while you're down are goneReconcile against the baseline on restart; don't trust "we caught up"
Rate limits (429)Undisclosed and variableExponential backoff with jitter; batch; cache
Duplicate deliveryRetries and overlap windows both cause itIdempotent writes keyed on entity + change
Partial failureHalf a publish applied is worse than noneTransactional apply, or a resumable per-item ledger
Publish bypassed at sourceA load with shouldSkipAssortmentPublish emits no event and writes no change history — the consumer is correct and still staleNothing the consumer can do. Detect it with periodic baseline reconciliation, and audit loader configs

The reconciliation habit

Every incremental integration drifts eventually — a missed event, a partial apply, a bug shipped on a Friday. The healthy pattern is a periodic baseline reconciliation: on a schedule, pull the latest baseline and compare it against downstream state, reporting differences rather than silently correcting them. Customers who do this find problems in days; customers who don't find them in line reviews. Recommending it is cheap advice that makes you look prescient about a year later.

Concept 6 — Webhook security, in both directions

The two directions have genuinely different postures, and conflating them is how you give a security team a wrong answer.

Inbound: external → VibeIQOutbound: VibeIQ → external
PurposeTrigger a workflow from outsideNotify the customer's endpoint of an event
EndpointPOST /api/trigger-webhooks/{ID}The customer's own URL
VerificationX-Verify-Signature, documented as optional and shown as a shared secret in a headerThree options: API keys in custom headers, HMAC signatures using shared secrets, or IP allowlisting
SetupCLI or API; the webhook ID is in the URL"Configuration requires contacting VibeIQ support"

What to actually say to a security team

Outbound is defensible: HMAC signing is available, and you should recommend it over header keys or IP allowlists. Inbound is weaker — the trigger URL contains the webhook ID and functions as a bearer credential, and the documented verification is an optional shared-secret header rather than a signature over the request body. Treat trigger URLs as secrets, don't put them anywhere loggable, and confirm internally what is actually enforced server-side before you make a claim. Note also that outbound configuration goes through VibeIQ support — so it is a lead-time item in any implementation plan, not a self-serve toggle.

Concept 7 — Attribute ownership, the artefact that prevents the arguments

Two-way integration means every attribute needs an owner. Without an explicit map, both systems write the same field and the last writer wins — intermittently, and differently each time.

The map you should insist exists, per attribute: which system is authoritative · which direction it flows · at which entity layer it lives (Item / ProjectItem / AssortmentItem) · at which property level (Family / Option / All / Override) · what happens on conflict.

This single artefact resolves more disputes than any diagnostic skill. "PLM overwrote our margin target" is not a bug report — it is the absence of this map. Build it during implementation, keep it current, and bring it to every architecture review. Weeks 2, 4 and 5 exist so that you can fill it in credibly, which is why the columns name the layer and the property level rather than just the field.

Recommended reading

  • Integration Patterns — overview
  • Authentication & API Access — keys, headers, error codes, key hygiene.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — the code path in Concept 3. Read every line.
  • Change History Polling

Practical exercise

Write the attribute ownership map for a mid-size brand running VibeIQ, Centric PLM and SAP. Fifteen attributes is enough — include at least: style name, category, colourway, target retail price, actual landed cost, margin target, size range, lifecycle status, image, inventory position, supplier. For each: authoritative system, direction, VibeIQ entity layer, property level, and conflict rule. Then mark the three rows you'd expect to be argued about and say why.

The answer

The attribute ownership map. Columns: attribute · authoritative system · direction · VibeIQ layer · property level · conflict rule.

AttributeOwnerDirectionLayerLevelConflict rule
Style nameVibeIQ→ PLM, ERPItemFamilyVibeIQ wins; PLM edits are reported, not merged
Style numberPLM← PLMItemFamilyImmutable once set. This is the federatedId
Category / divisionVibeIQ→ PLMItemFamilyVibeIQ wins
Colourway name / codeVibeIQ→ PLMItemOptionVibeIQ wins
Size rangeVibeIQ→ PLMItemOverrideVibeIQ wins; exceptions per colourway are legitimate
Seasonal statusVibeIQ→ PLMProjectItem—VibeIQ wins. Season-scoped by definition
Target retail priceVibeIQ→ PLMProjectItem—VibeIQ wins
Channel retail priceVibeIQ→ ERPAssortmentItem—Per assortment. No single correct value exists
Margin targetVibeIQinternalProjectItem—VibeIQ wins
Actual landed costERP (SAP)← SAPProjectItem—SAP always wins. Read-only in VibeIQ
Standard / target costPLM← PLMProjectItem—PLM wins; VibeIQ displays for margin maths
Materials / compositionPLM← PLMItemOverridePLM wins. Developed downstream, not decided upstream
Supplier / vendorPLM← PLMItemOverridePLM wins
Lifecycle statusSplit — see belowbothItemOverrideDistinct value sets per system; never the same field
Primary imageDAM← DAMItemOptionDAM holds the master; VibeIQ holds the reference
Inventory positionERP (SAP)← SAPProjectItem—SAP wins. Read-only, and stale by design

The three rows that will cause an argument, and how to settle each before build:

1 · Lifecycle status. Both systems have one, both call it status, and they mean different things — VibeIQ's is a decision state (proposed, approved, cut), PLM's is a development state (in development, sampling, released). Mapping them onto one field guarantees a two-way overwrite loop where each system keeps correcting the other. Settle it: refuse the shared field. Two separate attributes, each owned by one system, each read-only in the other, with an explicit table for any transition that should trigger the other. This is the row that most often gets discovered in production.

2 · Cost. Merchandising needs a cost to compute margin during planning, when no real cost exists yet — so VibeIQ carries an estimate. PLM later produces a standard cost and SAP an actual landed cost. Three numbers, one word. Settle it: three named attributes with different names — plannedCost, standardCost, landedCost — and be explicit about which one margin is computed from at each stage. Never let them collapse into "cost."

3 · Channel retail price. The argument is not about ownership but about shape: ERP models one price per SKU, VibeIQ models a price per assortment, and the same product legitimately carries different prices in Wholesale and DTC. A naive integration flattens this and the last assortment written wins. Settle it: confirm the target system can accept price qualified by channel. If it cannot, the integration must select which assortment is authoritative for the outbound feed — a business decision, made once, in writing.

How to think about it

The artefact exists to make a decision explicit that is otherwise made by accident. Without the map, both systems write the same field and the last writer wins — intermittently, and differently each time, depending on which nightly job ran last. That produces the worst class of bug: not reproducible, not attributable, and it looks like flakiness rather than design.

Direction and ownership are two questions, not one. A field can flow into VibeIQ and still be owned by VibeIQ (a default that users then override), or flow both ways with one system authoritative. Collapsing them is why maps drawn as arrows on a whiteboard fail — the arrow says nothing about who wins.

The layer and level columns are what make this a VibeIQ artefact rather than a generic integration diagram. "VibeIQ owns retail price" is not actionable until you say which retail price: target price on the ProjectItem is a season-level decision, channel price on the AssortmentItem cannot have a single value at all. Half the integration bugs in Week 2 are really an ownership map that stopped one column short.

Look for one word covering several concepts — that is where the arguments live. Status and cost are the reliable offenders in this industry, and both appear above. The move is always the same: refuse the shared field, name the concepts separately, and let the mapping between them be explicit and one-directional. It feels pedantic in a design meeting and it prevents the loop.

Note which rows are read-only, and say so out loud. Landed cost and inventory flow in from SAP and are stale the moment they arrive. That is fine — they inform a decision, they do not govern one. Problems start when someone builds a workflow that writes back to a field VibeIQ does not own.

What a weak answer looks like: a map with owners but no conflict rules. "PLM owns cost" does not tell an engineer what to do when VibeIQ has a value and PLM sends a different one on the same night. The conflict rule is the column that turns a diagram into a specification.

Sources
  • Integration Patterns — overview — the two-way integration shape this map governs.
  • Property Type Handling — the property-level column — why size range is Override and colourway is Option.
  • Week 2 — The three item layers — the layer column, and why channel price cannot live above the AssortmentItem.
  • Concept 7 — Attribute ownership — the per-attribute fields this table instantiates.
  • External — not official documentation: Vendor documentation for the specific PLM and ERP in play — Centric and SAP model status and cost differently from each other, and the three contested rows can only be settled against what those systems actually store. Read their field definitions rather than assuming.

Realistic customer scenario — an architecture review

Summit Athletic's integration engineer sends you their design before build. This is the review you'll be doing for real:

"Nightly job at 02:00. We call /api/change-history with createdOn BETWEEN <last_run_time> AND <now>, where last_run_time is what our server recorded when the previous run started. We process each change and update Centric. We store the assortmentBaselineDownloadLink against each assortment so we can re-read it if someone asks questions later. One API key in our config file, same one for dev and prod so testing is realistic. If a call fails we log it and the next night's run picks things up. We're skipping the delete file for now since merch says they rarely drop styles mid-season."

Find at least six distinct problems. For each: the failure it causes, the symptom the customer would actually report, and the fix. Then rank them — which one do you insist is fixed before go-live, and which would you accept as a follow-up? Finally, write the two-sentence opening of your reply, given this person is proud of the design and you need them to stay collaborative.

The answer

Seven problems. Each with the failure, what the customer would actually say, and the fix.

1 · Skipping the delete file — the one that must not ship.

  • Failure: removals never reach Centric. And because deletes do not accumulate across publishes, they are never mentioned again — nothing self-heals.
  • Symptom reported: "Centric has styles we cut weeks ago. Sourcing has started costing two of them." (Exactly the Week 3 incident.)
  • Fix: process deleteDataDownloadLink on every change. "Merch rarely drops styles mid-season" is also false in the way that matters — line reviews are mass-deletion events, and they are the highest-stakes moment of the season.

2 · The polling window starts at last_run_time = when the previous run started

  • Failure: silent data loss. Changes written while the previous run was executing fall between the windows and are never queried. A 20-minute run drops a 20-minute hole every night. Worse, if a run fails midway, the next window still starts from the failed run's start time and the missed records are gone.
  • Symptom reported: "Some styles just never make it across. It's different ones each time and we can't reproduce it."
  • Fix: advance the watermark only on successful completion, and set it from a value derived from the records processed rather than a wall-clock read. Overlap the window deliberately — re-query a few minutes back — and rely on idempotency to absorb the duplicates.

3 · Temporal filtering treated as a cursor (the deeper form of #2)

  • Failure: boundary exclusivity and clock skew. Records landing exactly on a boundary are dropped or double-processed depending on inclusivity, and the customer's server clock is not VibeIQ's clock.
  • Symptom reported: the same as #2 — intermittent, unreproducible gaps.
  • Fix: accept that this is not a cursor. Overlap windows, make processing idempotent, and reconcile against a baseline periodically so drift is bounded rather than cumulative.

4 · Storing assortmentBaselineDownloadLink

  • Failure: the links expire after 24 hours. The stored URL is dead the next day — and it is stored precisely for "if someone asks questions later," which is by definition later than 24 hours.
  • Symptom reported: "We tried to check what happened last week and every link is broken."
  • Fix: persist the change ID and re-fetch the change object on demand to get fresh links. If they want a durable archive, download and store the content at processing time, not the URL.

5 · One API key shared across dev and prod

  • Failure: no blast-radius containment and no revocation path. A leaked or rotated key takes down both environments; worse, dev code can write to production data. Docs are explicit about keeping separate keys per environment.
  • Symptom reported: "Someone's test run overwrote production prices." Or, at contract time: "your security team is asking how keys are scoped and we don't have a good answer."
  • Fix: a key per environment, held in secrets management rather than a config file, with a documented rotation and offboarding procedure. Note this is a credential in a config file — ask whether that file is in version control.

6 · "If a call fails we log it and the next night picks things up"

  • Failure: it does not pick things up — see #2, the window has already moved past the failure. There is also no retry, no backoff, and no alert: a log line nobody reads is not error handling.
  • Symptom reported: "It's been broken for a week and nobody noticed." The silent stall — the failure mode that survives longest.
  • Fix: retry with exponential backoff; do not advance the watermark on failure; alert a named owner on repeated failure; and add a staleness alarm for "no changes processed in longer than normal," because zero errors and zero work look identical.

7 · No idempotency, and polling chosen without stating what it misses

  • Failure: once you overlap windows (the fix for #2) duplicate processing becomes routine, and nothing here is idempotent. Separately, nobody has written down why polling was chosen over events — a defensible choice, but its scope and latency consequences should be deliberate.
  • Symptom reported: "Centric has duplicate updates / the same change applied twice."
  • Fix: track processed change IDs; make application idempotent; record the pattern decision and what it trades away.

Ranking

  • Block go-live: #1 (deletes), #2 (the window), #6 (failure handling and alerting). These cause wrong data with no notification — the customer finds out from sourcing spend or a line review, which is the most expensive way to learn.
  • Block go-live, cheap: #4 and #5. Both are small code changes with disproportionate consequences, and #5 becomes a contractual problem the moment InfoSec reads the design.
  • Follow-up: #7's idempotency should land with the #2 fix, but a formal reconciliation cadence can follow in the first month. The written pattern rationale is documentation debt, not a defect.

The opening two sentences

"Thanks for sending this before building it — that is the right instinct and it is going to save us both a lot of time. The overall shape is sound; most of what I have is about failure modes that are specific to how this API behaves, and a couple of them are the kind nobody catches until they bite in production."
How to think about it

The design is competent, which is exactly why it is dangerous. Nothing here is careless — it is what a good engineer writes when they have not yet learned three platform-specific facts: links expire, deletes do not accumulate, and temporal filtering is not a cursor. Reviewing it as though it were sloppy will lose the engineer, and losing the engineer means the fixes do not get made.

Lead with the symptom, not the mechanism. "Your window has a boundary defect" invites debate. "You will get intermittent missing styles that you cannot reproduce, and here is why" describes a future that engineer does not want. Naming the symptom first is what makes a review land, and it is the same move as citing the report a merchandiser would file rather than the field that caused it.

Separate "wrong data, silently" from everything else. That is the ranking principle, not severity in the abstract. Problems 1, 2 and 6 all produce incorrect downstream data with no signal — the customer's first indication is money already spent. A noisy failure is comparatively cheap because it gets fixed.

Challenge the premise behind #1, gently. "Merch rarely drops styles" is a business assumption smuggled into an architecture. It is also wrong at the worst possible time: the line review, the single moment the platform exists to make good, is a mass-deletion event. Point at the moment, not at the assumption.

The two sentences are doing real work. Credit the behaviour you want repeated (sending it early), affirm the parts that are fine, and frame the findings as platform-specific rather than as competence failures — because they genuinely are. Get this wrong and you win the argument and lose the relationship, which in Week 10's terms is the defining failure mode of an architecture review: you were right and the engineer stopped listening.

What a weak answer looks like: finding six problems and presenting them as a flat list. An unranked list of seven defects reads as "your design is bad" and gets triaged by the engineer's own judgement, which is what produced the design. Rank them, say which two you insist on, and let them win the follow-ups — that is what keeps a reviewer welcome the next time.

Sources
  • Change History Polling — the temporal BETWEEN filtering behind problems 2 and 3, and the last-sync-timestamp pattern the design misapplies.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — problems 1 and 4 — the delete file, the non-accumulation rule, and the 24-hour link expiry.
  • Authentication & API Access — problem 5 — key scoping per environment, key hygiene, and the error codes retry logic must distinguish.
  • Concept 5 — Designing a consumer that survives — the six hazards, each of which this design hits.

Quiz

  1. Give the decision rule for events vs. polling. What does the scope difference mean for a customer who needs all item changes, not just publishes?

    Events for real-time needs, assortment-specific workflows, webhook-capable systems and minimising API calls. Polling for scheduled batch sync, tracking multiple entity types, historical retrieval and systems with no webhook support. The scope difference: publish events cover assortment publishes only. A customer needing all item changes — a colour corrected, a description fixed, anything that never goes through a publish — will not get them from events. That customer needs polling as well, and many real architectures run both: events for latency, polling as the safety net and for everything else.

  2. What are the two required headers, and what does the app: prefix on a key signify?

    X-Api-Key and X-Api-Org (the organisation slug). The app: prefix — as in app:uEL7GCBZ7Xhd4w2z — marks the key as application-scoped rather than a user credential. That matters because app keys are the recommended form: they aren't tied to a person who might leave, and the docs advise preferring them over user keys, keeping separate keys per environment, and rotating regularly.

  3. Why is a 404 from this API not proof that a record doesn't exist?

    Because a 404 from this API can mean the entity doesn't exist, or access was denied, or you used the wrong entity type. All three collapse into the same status code. So "we got a 404, the record isn't there" is an unsafe conclusion — before believing it, check that the API key's permissions cover that entity and that the entity type in the path is right. Treat it as "not found for you, as asked" rather than "not found".

  4. What can you honestly tell a customer about rate limits, and what must a consumer implement regardless?

    Honestly: that the APIs are "rate-limited to ensure system stability", that the specific thresholds are not published, and that customers hitting limits should contact VibeIQ support to discuss higher limits. You cannot promise a throughput number. (And never cite the "15 requests/hour" figure circulating online — that belongs to vibe.co, a different company.) Regardless of the limit, a consumer must implement exponential backoff on 429, plus batching and caching to reduce call volume — that's required design, not optimisation.

  5. Trace the event-driven path from publish to persisted rows, naming each call. What is in the event payload, and why so little?

    (1) Publishing emits an assortment|publish event carrying only {assortmentId, assortmentPublishChangeId}. (2) An Event Workflow forwards it to the customer's endpoint. (3) The consumer calls GET /assortments/{assortmentId}/history/{changeId} with the auth headers to fetch the full AssortmentPublishChange. (4) It downloads the baseline from assortmentBaselineDownloadLink (same auth headers). (5) It iterates baselineData.assortmentItems, transforms, persists, and stores createdOn. The payload is thin because the real data is large and time-sensitive: sending IDs lets the consumer fetch on its own schedule, and re-fetching refreshes the 24-hour S3 links.

  6. Why is "the event never arrived" a workflow investigation, and what would you look at first?

    Because the delivery mechanism is a workflow — "configure an Event Workflow in VibeIQ to forward events to your endpoint." So the outbound integration is a Week 6 artefact with a triggerKey of assortment|publish and an action that POSTs. Look first at the Process List: is there a process at all? If none, the trigger never matched. If there is, read triggerEvent and which path ran, then taskOutputs — a COMPLETE process with a no-op action usually means a missing access grant or a config pointing at the wrong endpoint.

  7. The baseline exposes .item, .item.itemFamily and .projectItem per assortment item. Why does that make mapping a data-model decision rather than a plumbing one?

    Because those three fields are the entity layers from Week 2 — AssortmentItem, Item, its Family and the ProjectItem — sitting in one object. Every mapping decision the customer's engineer makes is therefore a decision about which layer is authoritative for which attribute, not a field-renaming exercise. A team that never learned the layer model maps from whichever field happens to be populated and builds a downstream system that is subtly wrong in ways nobody can later explain. It's the highest-leverage moment for a TAM to be in the room.

  8. Explain why temporal polling loses records. Name the three causes and the four guards.

    Because a timestamp is a guess at a position, whereas a cursor is a position. Three causes: (1) boundary exclusivity — inclusive double-processes, exclusive drops same-instant writes; (2) in-flight writes that commit after your query ran but are timestamped before your cutoff, so they're missed by this poll and precede the next one; (3) clock skew between the consumer's now and the server's. Four guards: overlap the window (lastSync − safetyMargin); make processing idempotent so re-fetching is harmless; advance the timestamp from the maximum createdOn actually returned rather than the local clock; and never advance it on partial failure.

  9. A consumer is down 36 hours across two publishes. Walk the recovery, and say why resuming from the next event is not enough.

    Recovery: identify the gap in processed change IDs, fetch the current change object to get fresh S3 links (they expire in 24h), download the baseline — the assortment's complete state at publish time — and reconcile downstream against it, removing anything present downstream but absent from the baseline. Resuming from the next event isn't enough because deletes do not accumulate: each AssortmentPublishChange carries only its own publish's deletes, so the two missed publishes' removals will never be replayed by any later event. Adds and updates would eventually correct themselves; the deletes never would.

  10. Contrast inbound and outbound webhook security. What would you recommend, and what would you refuse to assert?

    Outbound (VibeIQ → the customer's endpoint) offers three options: API keys in custom headers, HMAC signatures using shared secrets, or IP allowlisting — configured via VibeIQ support. Inbound (external → VibeIQ, POST /api/trigger-webhooks/{ID}) documents only an optional X-Verify-Signature shared-secret header, not an HMAC over the request body, while the webhook ID in the URL acts as a bearer credential. Recommend HMAC for outbound. Refuse to assert anything definite about what inbound verification is actually enforced server-side — take that away and confirm internally. Also flag that outbound setup goes through support, so it's a lead-time item.

Hands-on challenge

Write your integration design review template — the standard questions you'd put to any customer before they build against VibeIQ. Cover: pattern choice and what it misses, polling window construction, idempotency, change-ID vs. URL persistence, delete handling, downtime recovery and reconciliation cadence, key scoping per environment, backoff, webhook verification in both directions, and the attribute ownership map. This is the last piece of your account-inheritance kit — alongside the Week 2 data-model health check, Week 3 publish checklist, Week 4 pre-load checklist and Week 6 workflow review.

The answer

Integration design review template. Put these to any customer or SI before they build. Ten sections; the first two decide most of the rest.

1 · Pattern choice — and what it misses

  • Events or polling, and why? Both is legitimate for different entity types.
  • If events: you are subscribed to assortment publishes only. What about item changes outside a publish — who needs those, and how do they get them?
  • If polling: what latency is acceptable, and does the business actually agree with that number?
  • What does the chosen pattern not cover, written down? Every design misses something; undocumented gaps become incidents.

2 · Polling window construction (skip if events-only)

  • How is the window built, exactly? Show me the expression.
  • Does the watermark advance on start or on successful completion? Start is a silent-data-loss bug.
  • Are boundaries inclusive or exclusive, and what happens to a record landing exactly on one?
  • Is there deliberate overlap? Whose clock generates the timestamps?
  • What happens to the window if a run fails halfway?

3 · Idempotency

  • Is applying the same change twice safe? Demonstrate it, don't assert it.
  • Are processed change IDs recorded — enough to detect a gap, not just remember the last one?
  • Is the processed marker committed in the same transaction as the work?

4 · Change ID vs. URL persistence

  • Is any S3 URL stored anywhere — database, queue message, retry payload, log used for replay? All four are common; all four die after 24 hours.
  • Is the change object re-fetched by ID at processing time?
  • If content must be archived, is the content stored rather than the link?

5 · Delete handling

  • Is deleteDataDownloadLink processed on every publish? And familyItemsRemoved?
  • What does "delete" mean in the target system — hard delete, deactivate, flag? Who decided?
  • Has a deletion been tested end to end, in any environment?
  • Does anyone believe deletes are rare? Line reviews are mass-deletion events.

6 · Downtime recovery and reconciliation

  • What happens after 48 hours down — described, and then: what happened the last time it went down?
  • On detecting a gap, does it resume or re-baseline? Resuming loses the deletes permanently.
  • Is there a scheduled baseline reconciliation independent of events? How often, and does it report or correct?
  • When did it last run and who read the output? An unread report is not a control.

7 · Credentials and environment scoping

  • One key per environment, or one key everywhere?
  • Where does the key live — secrets management, or a config file? Is that file in version control?
  • Who can read it today, what is the rotation procedure, and what happens at offboarding?
  • Does X-Api-Org match the intended org in every environment? A mismatch presents as 401 and gets misdiagnosed as an invalid key.
  • Has anyone run the CLI? Credentials cache to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on every laptop that ever did.

8 · Failure handling and backoff

  • Retry policy — with exponential backoff, or immediate? Bounded?
  • Which error codes are retryable? A 404 here is not proof a record is absent, so it must not be treated as terminal.
  • What are the documented rate limits? There are none published — so the consumer must implement backoff regardless, and must not depend on a specific number.
  • Who is alerted, by name, and on what threshold?
  • Is there a staleness alarm? Zero errors and zero work are indistinguishable without one.

9 · Webhook verification — both directions, separately

  • Outbound (VibeIQ → them): is the HMAC signature verified on receipt, or is the endpoint effectively open?
  • Inbound (them → VibeIQ): the webhook ID in the trigger URL acts as a bearer credential — is it treated as a secret? Where is it stored?
  • Are these two conflated in their security documentation? They have genuinely different postures and conflating them is how a security team gets a wrong answer.

10 · The attribute ownership map

  • Does one exist? If not, stop — the rest of the design is premature.
  • Per attribute: authoritative system, direction, VibeIQ layer, property level, conflict rule.
  • Which fields are written by both systems? Those are the future intermittent bugs.
  • Is any single word — "status", "cost" — covering more than one concept?

How to run it: sections 1, 2, 5 and 10 in the first meeting; the rest can be a written exchange. Ask for the design document and the code, not a verbal walkthrough — people describe the system they meant to build. Close by naming what you would block go-live for, separately from what you would like changed, so the customer can plan rather than negotiate.

How to think about it

Section 10 belongs at the end and gets asked first. Without an ownership map, reviewing retry logic is rearranging furniture — you are validating the mechanics of moving data whose direction nobody has agreed. If the answer is "we'll work that out during build," that is the finding, and it outranks everything else on the list.

Ask for the artefact, then the observation, then the design. "Show me the expression" beats "how do you build the window?"; "what happened last time it went down?" beats "what happens when it goes down?". Every item above is phrased to be answerable by pointing at something. Where it cannot be, that is itself the answer.

Most of this list is one property restated: the change stream is not replayable. Expiring links, non-accumulating deletes, gaps that do not self-heal, watermarks that must not advance on failure — all the same fact from different angles. Teach the property and a good engineer derives half the checklist themselves, which is a far better outcome than them complying with it.

Two items exist purely because silence is the worst failure mode. The staleness alarm (8) and the reconciliation cadence (6) are the only two controls that catch a system which has quietly stopped. Everything else on the list catches something that announces itself.

Be honest about rate limits. The correct answer is that no numeric limits are published, so a consumer must implement backoff regardless and must not tune to a specific number. Do not repeat the "15 requests/hour" figure that circulates online — it belongs to a different company. This is a small thing that quietly establishes whether you are worth believing on the larger ones.

Separate blocking from desirable, explicitly. A review that produces twenty undifferentiated findings gets triaged by the person who wrote the design. Naming the two or three you will not sign off on — and conceding the rest as follow-ups — is what makes the review a decision rather than an opinion, and what gets you invited to the next one.

Sources
  • Integration Patterns — overview — section 1 — the pattern comparison, including the scope row that decides most architectures.
  • Change History Polling — section 2 — temporal filtering, the BETWEEN form, and the last-sync-timestamp pattern.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — sections 4–6 — link expiry, the delete file, and baseline reconciliation.
  • Authentication & API Access — sections 7–8 — headers, key hygiene per environment, and the error codes retry logic must distinguish.
  • Webhooks — section 9 — the inbound trigger-webhook surface and what its ID actually is.

I know this when I can…

  • Pick a pattern from a requirement and name what that choice will not cover.
  • Trace both paths end to end without notes, naming every call and header.
  • Explain the polling seam precisely enough that an engineer changes their code.
  • Read an integration design and find six real problems in it.
  • Answer a security team on webhooks in both directions, and know which claim to refuse.
  • Build and defend an attribute ownership map across VibeIQ, PLM and ERP.
