Module 6 · Week 6

Event Workflows

The deepest technical module in the program, and the one closest to your day job. Workflows are "the main engine for reacting to system events and running apps across the VibeIQ infrastructure" — when a customer says "the automation didn't run," this is the entire surface you have to reason about.

On this page

  1. ⚙️Anatomy
  2. ⚙️The six trigger categories
  3. ⚙️Paths, and the rule that bites
  4. ⚙️Conditional expressions
  5. ⚙️Actions and per-workflow config
  6. ⚙️Concurrency and message grouping
  7. ⚙️Webhooks
  8. ⚙️From app to running workflow
  9. ⚙️Observability, and the "why didn't it fire?" ladder
  10. 📚Recommended reading
  11. ✅Practical exercise
  12. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  13. ✅Quiz
  14. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) read a workflow definition in app.yml and predict exactly what it will do, (2) name all six trigger categories and pick the right one for a requirement, (3) explain path evaluation and the first-match-wins rule that causes silent misbehaviour, (4) diagnose a concurrency race and prescribe the right message-grouping fix, and (5) work the "why didn't it fire?" ladder from the Admin Console down to taskOutputs.

Concept 1 — Anatomy

"Workflows are composed of triggers, conditionals, and actions." A trigger says when, conditionals say which branch, actions say what happens. Workflows are declared in an app's app.yml under a top-level workflows: key.

workflows:
  syncToProduction:
    identifier: 'syncToProduction'
    name: 'Sync Items to Production'
    triggerKey: item|update
    isActive: true
    public: true
    parallelWorkerCount: 5
    workflowDefinition:
      paths:
        - conditional: 'true'
          actionDefinitionConfigs:
            - action: syncItem
              name: Sync Item
PropertyReq.Purpose
identifieryesUnique, stable key — this is what makes cross-org migration possible. Treat it as immutable.
nameyesDisplay name in the Admin Console. This is what a customer will quote at you in a ticket.
triggerKeyyesThe event that activates the workflow.
isActiveyesEnable/disable registration. First thing to check when nothing fires.
publicnoAllow other apps to trigger this workflow.
parallelWorkerCountnoConcurrent process limit, 1–30, default 5.
messageGroupIdnoStatic serialization group (mutually exclusive with the dynamic form).
messageGroupnoStatic bucket name used with dynamic grouping.
dynamicMessageGroupIdnoTemplate string assigning events to queues at runtime.
cronsnoScheduled trigger declarations (name + cron).

Concept 2 — The six trigger categories

1. System events — entity CRUD broadcast when data changes, in the form {entityType}|{action}:

Entity typeTrigger keys
itemitem|create · item|update · item|delete
project-itemproject-item|create · |update · |delete
assortmentassortment|publish
assortment-itemassortment-item|create · |update · |delete
plan-placeholderplan-placeholder|create · |update · |delete
custom-entitycustom-entity|create · |update · |delete
commentcomment|create · comment|update
contentcontent|create · content|update

Note what this list implies. assortment has only publish — there is no assortment|update. If a customer wants automation on "any change to the assortment," the honest answer is that they must hang it off assortment-item events or off publish, and those have very different timing. This is a requirements conversation, not a config task, and catching it early saves a rebuild.

2. Manual — triggerKey: manual. Not tied to an event; used for batch loads, archival jobs and on-demand tasks. Also your safest way to test an action in isolation.

3. Custom app events — react to another app's domain events:

triggerKey: '@{publisherOrgSlug}/{appName}:{EventName}'
# e.g. '@vibeiq/image-loader:ImageAssignmentComplete'

The {EventName} part comes from the publishing app's eventWorkflowTriggerKeyMapping template. This is how apps chain — and how a break in app A silently stops automation in app B.

4. Declared custom trigger keys — domain-specific triggers an app declares:

triggers:
  CopyDownToLinkedItems:
    key: item|linked-copy-down
    name: Trigger copy down to linked item copies
    description: Triggers a workflow to copy down item updates to linked item copies.

Fired programmatically by creating an external-event entity:

await new Entities().create({
  entityName: 'external-event',
  object: {
    details: { eventType: 'item' },
    actionType: 'linked-copy-down',
    // ...payload fields the conditionals and actions will read
  },
});

5. Scheduled — crons declarations using standard CRON syntax.

6. Webhook — external HTTP callbacks, covered in Concept 7.

Concept 3 — Paths, and the rule that bites

workflowDefinition.paths is an ordered array. Each path pairs a conditional with a list of actionDefinitionConfigs.

First match wins

"Only the first path whose conditional evaluates to true is executed; the remaining paths are skipped." Paths are an if / else-if chain, not a set of independent rules. Actions within a path run sequentially.

paths:
  - conditional: '{newData.status} === "active"'
    actionDefinitionConfigs:
      - action: notifySlack
        name: Notify Slack (Active)
      - action: logItem
        name: Log Item
  - conditional: 'true'          # catch-all — only reached if the above was false
    actionDefinitionConfigs:
      - action: archiveItem
        name: Archive Item

The classic authoring bug

Someone writes several paths intending "run all that apply," and puts a broad or true conditional near the top. Everything below it becomes dead code. Nothing errors, the process completes successfully, and the customer reports "only some of the automation runs." Whenever you review a workflow, read the paths top-down and find the first one that can always be true — everything after it is unreachable.

Concept 4 — Conditional expressions

Conditionals are JavaScript expressions evaluated against the event payload, with interpolations wrapped in { ... }. Available inputs:

InputWhat it holds
{newData.<slug>}Entity state after the change
{oldData.<slug>}State before the change — absent on creates
{propertyDiffs.<slug>.<propertyName|oldValue|newValue>}Map of changed-property metadata
{changeObjects}Array of { id, changes } for bulk-change workflows
 
conditional: 'true'                                          # always run
conditional: '{newData.roles}.includes("family")'            # family-level only
conditional: '{propertyDiffs.status.propertyName} != null'   # did status change at all?
conditional: '{newData.status} !== {oldData.status}'         # old vs new
conditional: '{newData.status} === "approved" && {newData.roles}.includes("family")'

For bulk propagation, changeObjects lets one conditional test many entities at once:

conditional: >-
  {changeObjects}.some(obj =>
    ["sizeGroup","merchDivisionCode","brand","primaryViewableId"].some(prop =>
      Object.keys(obj.changes).includes(prop)))

{oldData} being absent on creates is a genuine trap. A conditional like {newData.status} !== {oldData.status} behaves differently on a create than an update — and a bulk load creates thousands of entities at once. "The workflow fired on every row of the initial load and spammed everyone" is a real incident, and this is usually why. When reviewing, always ask what the conditional does on a create.

Concept 5 — Actions and per-workflow config

Task execution is strictly serial, and fails closed

"For each time a workflow is triggered, a process is created. Each process contains one or more tasks." And crucially: "Only one task runs at a time in a process, and tasks always run in order. If one task fails, none of the subsequent tasks are run." So a three-action path that fails on action one leaves actions two and three unexecuted — not failed, not retried, simply never run. Order your actions so the riskiest one is last, and never assume a later action ran because the process exists.

Each entry in actionDefinitionConfigs needs action (a key matching the app's actions section) and name (the label shown in the Admin UI), plus optional config. Actions exposing actionConfiguration can be reused with different parameters per workflow:

actions:
  syncItem:
    name: 'Sync Item'
    async: true
    actionConfiguration:
      endpoint:
        label: 'Target API Endpoint'
        propertyType: 'string'

# ...then, per workflow:
    - action: syncItem
      name: Sync Item
      config:
        endpoint: 'https://prod.example.com/items'

Config values are "merged with runtime context and passed to the action's execute function via the config argument." One action, many environments — which is exactly how a staging endpoint ends up wired into a production workflow. Check config values during any environment-promotion review.

Access grants

Actions can only touch entities the app has been granted:

accessGrants:
  - entityType: item
    accessLevel: write        # get, query, create, update, delete
  - entityType: project-item
    accessLevel: read         # get, query

A workflow that fires, runs, and "does nothing" is often a missing or read-only access grant.

Concept 6 — Concurrency and message grouping

This is the part that produces the hardest bugs you will see, because they are intermittent and load-dependent.

Two serialization strategies:

Static serialization — every event for this workflow runs strictly one at a time:

copyDownToLinkedItemsWorkflow:
  triggerKey: item|linked-copy-down
  messageGroupId: copyDownToLinkedItems
  parallelWorkerCount: 1

Dynamic grouping — events sharing a key serialize; different keys run in parallel:

propagateRegionalForecasts:
  triggerKey: project-item|update
  parallelWorkerCount: 30
  messageGroup: propagateRegionalForecasts
  dynamicMessageGroupId: 'propagateRegionalForecasts-{newData.itemFamilyId}'

The idea worth internalising

Dynamic grouping lets you serialize only where it matters. Keying on itemFamilyId means two events touching the same family queue behind each other — no lost update — while events on different families run concurrently up to parallelWorkerCount. You get correctness where entities collide and throughput everywhere else. Static messageGroupId with parallelWorkerCount: 1 is the safe, slow hammer; dynamic grouping is the right tool once volume matters.

LimitDefault
parallelWorkerCount range1–30, default 5
Concurrent processes per event workflow25
Concurrent processes per organization60

The per-workflow and per-org ceilings are adjustable via the Customer Success Manager. Separately, "workflows can be configured to have limits on their run per minute" — a rate limit distinct from the concurrency ceiling, and the right lever when a workflow is correct but too chatty for a downstream system to absorb.

Disable, don't delete

"Workflows can be disabled without deleting them. This is useful when you wish to test out various workflows for a limited period of time, and keep them for historical record without changing their trigger." Deletion is different in kind: "Once deleted, the process list, task list, and logs for a workflow is lost." So deleting a workflow destroys its entire evidence trail instantly — not after 90 days, immediately. Before any cleanup of "old workflows," ask whether anyone might need to explain something that happened last month.

Two things to carry into customer conversations. First, the per-org ceiling of 60 is shared — one chatty workflow during a bulk load can starve every other workflow in the org, and the symptom is "unrelated automation got slow today." Second, raising parallelWorkerCount to fix slowness without a message group is how you convert a performance complaint into a data-corruption incident. Throughput and correctness are the same dial here; never turn it without asking what collides.

Concept 7 — Webhooks

Create one via CLI (contrail webhooks create -j) or by POSTing to https://api.vibeiq.com/prod/api/webhooks. The response carries an id you need later. Authentication uses two headers:

  • X-Api-Key — your API key
  • X-Api-Org — your organization key

Link a webhook to a workflow by setting the trigger to "webhookId:WEBHOOK_ID". Multiple workflows can hang off one webhook. To fire it:

curl --request POST \
  --url https://api.vibeiq.com/prod/api/trigger-webhooks/4X0bETHdBKw87l6h \
  --header 'X-Verify-Signature: my-secret-key'

The workflow receives the request as an event shaped:

{
  "body":    { /* your POST data */ },
  "headers": { /* request headers */ }
}

The trigger endpoint returns an array of the workflow processes it created, with statuses, IDs and initial event data — useful for confirming a webhook actually started something.

Security posture — flag this one

This is the inbound direction (external system → VibeIQ), and its posture is the weaker of the two. X-Verify-Signature is documented as optional and, in the example, is a shared secret compared as a header value — not an HMAC over the request body — while a webhook ID in a URL is effectively a bearer credential. The outbound direction (VibeIQ → the customer's endpoint, covered in Week 5) does support HMAC signing. Keep the two apart when a security team asks, treat trigger URLs as secrets, and confirm internally what inbound verification is actually enforced.

Concept 8 — From app to running workflow

  1. Workflows are declared in app.yml.
  2. contrail app publish turns them into Workflow Template Definitions attached to that app version.
  3. An installing org runs contrail workflow-template-definitions install to instantiate the templates as live workflows, preserving config values end to end.

Workflows are versioned artefacts of an app, not settings someone edits in a console. So "can you just tweak that automation?" is a release question: change the app, publish a version, install it. That distinction is the config-vs-customization boundary (Week 7) showing up in practice, and it sets a customer's expectations about turnaround.

Concept 9 — Observability, and the "why didn't it fire?" ladder

The Admin Console shows a Process List of "recently executed processes" in "reverse chronological order, with the most recent process at the top." Each process contains tasks, and "every task within a process has an Output. The Output is the result of the task."

UI statusAPI status
PendingPENDING
ProcessingACTIVE
AwaitingAWAITING
CompletedCOMPLETE
FailedFAILED

The console's query bar does a "case-insensitive substring match on a stringified version of the event and output fields" — so you can search for a style number or an error fragment and find the processes that touched it. Programmatically, fetch a process by ID or query many via the SDK, and read status, taskOutputs, triggerEvent, startTime and endTime.

90-day retention

"After 90 days, workflow logs (including the list of processes and tasks) are permanently deleted from the VibeIQ system." Any post-mortem on a slow-burning data problem has a hard evidence horizon. When a customer reports something that started "a few months ago," check retention before promising root cause — and tell them to preserve exports if the investigation will be long.

The diagnostic ladder — work it in order

  1. Is there a process at all? Filter the Process List on the entity. No process means the trigger never matched — go to 2. A process means it fired — jump to 5.
  2. Is isActive true, and is the workflow actually installed in this org from the app version they think?
  3. Is the triggerKey the right event? The classic miss: expecting assortment|update, which does not exist.
  4. Did the change actually emit that event? A bulk load creating entities emits create, not update.
  5. Which path ran? Read triggerEvent, then evaluate the conditionals top-down yourself. Remember first-match-wins and that {oldData} is absent on creates.
  6. Did the actions succeed? Read taskOutputs per task. A COMPLETE process with a no-op action usually means a missing access grant or a config pointing somewhere harmless.
  7. Is it a timing problem? Intermittent, load-correlated, "sometimes the value is stale" — suspect concurrency and message grouping, not logic.

Recommended reading

  • Workflows — introduction
  • Creating Workflows — the densest page in the docs. Read it twice; most of this module comes from it.
  • Observing Workflows — statuses, the process list, retention.
  • Webhooks

Practical exercise

Write the app.yml workflow block for this requirement, in full:

"When a family-level item's status changes to approved, push it to the PLM endpoint and post to Slack. If a family-level item changes in any other way, just log it. Ignore option-level items entirely. Don't let two events for the same item family process at the same time, but keep throughput up across different families."

Then answer three questions about your own definition: what does it do on a create? What happens if the paths are reordered? And what breaks if you drop dynamicMessageGroupId but keep parallelWorkerCount: 30?

The answer
workflows:
  familyStatusRouter:
    identifier: 'familyStatusRouter'
    name: 'Family item status router'
    triggerKey: item|update
    isActive: true
    public: true
    parallelWorkerCount: 10
    dynamicMessageGroupId: '{newData.itemFamilyId}'
    workflowDefinition:
      paths:
        # 1 — approved, and it actually just changed to approved
        - conditional: '{newData.roles}.includes("family")
                       && {newData.status} === "approved"
                       && {oldData.status} !== "approved"'
          actionDefinitionConfigs:
            - actionKey: pushToPlm
            - actionKey: postToSlack
        # 2 — any other family-level change
        - conditional: '{newData.roles}.includes("family")'
          actionDefinitionConfigs:
            - actionKey: logChange
        # 3 — options fall through with no path, so nothing runs

Options are ignored by having no matching path rather than by an explicit exclusion — simpler, and it fails safe if the roles model ever changes.

What does it do on a create?

Nothing — the trigger is item|update, so a create does not fire this workflow at all. But the question is really about the trap one step further in: oldData is absent on creates. If someone later adds item|create to get "new approved styles" working, {oldData.status} is undefined, undefined !== "approved" evaluates true, and path 1 fires for every newly created approved item — including 20,000 of them during a bulk load. Any conditional comparing old to new needs a create-safe form, and this is the single most common authoring bug in workflows.

What happens if the paths are reordered?

Path 2 becomes a catch-all that swallows everything and path 1 never runs. Only the first path whose conditional evaluates true is executed; the rest are skipped. Path 2's condition is a strict superset of path 1's, so with path 2 first, no family item ever reaches PLM or Slack — and nothing errors. The workflow runs successfully, every time, doing the wrong thing. Paths are an if / else-if chain, and ordering them broad-to-narrow is a silent failure.

What breaks if you drop dynamicMessageGroupId but keep parallelWorkerCount: 30?

Two events for the same item family can now execute concurrently, and a copy-down or read-modify-write action becomes a race: two workers read the family state, both compute from a now-stale snapshot, and the second write clobbers the first. You get lost updates, and — where an action writes several properties — values from one style landing on another.

Three properties make this the worst class of bug to inherit:

  • It is load-dependent. Low volume never collides. It works in testing and for months in production, then a bulk update triggers it.
  • It is non-deterministic. A different set of records breaks each run, so it looks like flakiness rather than a defect.
  • Nothing fails. Every process reports COMPLETE. There is no error to find.

And raising parallelWorkerCount is the standard response to "the workflow is slow" — so the usual fix for a performance complaint converts a slow workflow into a corrupting one. That is the trade the two fields exist to let you make deliberately: grouping gives you correctness within a key, worker count gives you throughput across keys.

How to think about it

Read the requirement for the words that map to fields. "Don't let two events for the same item family process at the same time" is dynamicMessageGroupId keyed on the family. "But keep throughput up across different families" is parallelWorkerCount above 1. Those two clauses are a precise specification of dynamic grouping, and a requirement written that way is usually written by someone who has already been burned.

Order paths narrow-to-broad, always. First-match-wins makes path order semantically significant, and the natural way to write requirements — general case first, then exceptions — produces exactly the wrong order. When reviewing someone else's workflow, checking for a path whose conditional is a superset of a later one is a thirty-second check that finds real bugs.

The oldData.status !== "approved" clause is not defensive padding. Without it, any update to an already-approved item re-fires the push and the Slack post. Someone fixes a typo in a description and PLM gets another push. "Status changed to approved" and "status is approved" are different conditions, and the requirement says changes to.

Concurrency bugs are diagnosed by shape, not by content. "Different records each time, only under load, no errors" is the signature — you can name the class before looking at a single record. That is why Week 9 leads with symptom shape, and it is the fastest inference available anywhere in the platform.

Remember what a workflow is, before promising a change. These are versioned artefacts of an app, declared in app.yml and installed as templates — not settings someone edits in a console. "Can you just add a condition?" is a release conversation. Knowing that shapes what you commit to on a call.

What a weak answer looks like: a correct YAML block with the three questions answered thinly. The block is the easy part; the questions are the module. Each one names a failure that produces no error — a create-path storm, an unreachable path, a silent race — and those three account for most workflow escalations you will see.

Sources
  • Creating Workflows — paths and first-match-wins, conditional inputs including newData/oldData/propertyDiffs, and the messageGroupId / dynamicMessageGroupId / parallelWorkerCount fields. The densest page in the docs; most of this answer is on it.
  • Workflows — introduction — triggers, conditionals and actions, and the {entityType}|{action} trigger form.
  • Concept 6 — Concurrency and message grouping — static vs. dynamic serialization and the org-level ceilings the worker count draws against.
  • Key findings — Failures that produce no error — the unreachable path and the concurrency race both appear there.

Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic. They have a workflow that copies approved family-level attributes down to linked item copies across regional projects. It has run fine for four months. Last Tuesday they ran a bulk update of 3,000 items ahead of a line review. A merchandiser reports:

"About 60 styles have the wrong regional pricing — it looks like values from a different style. And some are just missing. It was fine on Monday. We re-ran the update this morning and a different set of styles broke."

Their workflow has parallelWorkerCount: 30 and no message grouping of any kind. Two other integrations run in the same org.

Give: your hypothesis and why the "different set broke on re-run" detail is the strongest clue; the two fields you'd check first in the Process List and what you'd expect to see; the fix, written as the actual YAML change; and the second-order risk your fix introduces at the org level. Then one sentence to the merchandiser today.

The answer

Hypothesis: a concurrency race. parallelWorkerCount: 30 with no message grouping means up to 30 events for the same item family execute simultaneously. The copy-down action is read-modify-write: each worker reads the family state, computes the regional values, and writes. Under a 3,000-item bulk update, workers interleave — one writes values computed from a snapshot another has already superseded. Hence "values from a different style" (a write landing against the wrong parent) and "some are just missing" (a lost update, overwritten before it was read).

Why "a different set broke on re-run" is the strongest clue. It is the one detail that rules out every deterministic cause at once. A bad conditional, a wrong property level, a malformed row, a broken mapping — all of these are functions of the data, so re-running the same update breaks the same records every time. A different set breaking means the outcome depends on timing, not on content. Only concurrency does that. It also explains "fine for four months": at normal volume, two events for one family rarely overlap, so the defect was always present and never had the opportunity to fire.

The two Process List fields I'd check first

  • status — and I expect to see COMPLETE, not FAILED, across the board. That expectation is the point: confirming nothing failed eliminates the entire "an action errored" branch and is strong positive evidence for a race. If I find failures instead, my hypothesis is wrong and I am looking at a different problem.
  • startTime / endTime — filtered to one affected item family. I expect overlapping execution windows for the same family: two or more processes whose intervals intersect. That is the race, observed directly rather than inferred. triggerEvent confirms they were genuinely events for the same family.

Those two together turn "I think it is concurrency" into "here are two processes for style SH-4471 that ran between 02:14:06 and 02:14:09, both COMPLETE" — which is what makes it arguable with an engineer.

The fix, as YAML

copyDownToLinkedItemsWorkflow:
    triggerKey: item|linked-copy-down
    isActive: true
-   parallelWorkerCount: 30
+   parallelWorkerCount: 8
+   dynamicMessageGroupId: '{newData.itemFamilyId}'

Dynamic grouping serialises events sharing a family key while letting different families run in parallel — correctness within a key, throughput across keys. parallelWorkerCount: 1 with a static messageGroupId would also be correct and would serialise everything globally, which for 3,000 items before a line review is likely too slow.

The second-order risk at org level

Concurrency is a shared, org-wide budget: 25 concurrent processes per workflow and 60 per organisation, and two other integrations run in this org. Grouping does not reduce the number of processes — it changes which may run at once, so events queue behind their group key and processes live longer. A long-running backlog holds capacity, and the org ceiling is what the other two integrations are also drawing against. The realistic failure is that the nightly publish integration starts lagging or timing out during a bulk update, and nobody connects it to this change.

So: lower the worker count as part of the same change, look at what else runs at that hour, and get the org's utilisation checked against the ceiling before the next bulk update. Raising the ceiling is possible via the Customer Success Manager — worth opening now rather than during the next line review.

One sentence to the merchandiser today

"This is a system fault, not anything your team did, and it only shows up on big updates — which is why it looked fine until Tuesday; I know the mechanism, I have the fix, and I'll tell you by the end of the day which styles need re-checking before the review."
How to think about it

Shape before content. Three facts in the report — intermittent, load-correlated, different records each time — identify the class of cause before you look at a single style. That is the fastest inference in the platform and it comes from distribution, not detail. A TAM who starts by examining the 60 broken styles will spend a day finding nothing, because the records themselves are unremarkable.

"It was fine for four months" is not evidence against a defect. Latent concurrency bugs are defined by needing volume to express. Treating longevity as proof of correctness is how these survive — and it is the argument an engineer will make in the review, sincerely.

Design checks that can disprove you. Expecting COMPLETE rather than FAILED feels backwards, and it is the strongest check available: if processes did fail, the hypothesis dies immediately and cheaply. Overlapping timestamps then confirm it positively. Two fields, one confirming and one falsifying, is a good shape for any first look.

Every fix has a blast radius — name it before someone else does. The grouping change is correct and it consumes org capacity differently. Handing over a fix without its second-order cost is how you cause next month's incident while resolving this one. Saying it up front also demonstrates you understand the platform as a shared resource, which is most of what earns technical credibility.

The invisible constraint worth raising separately: workflow evidence is retained 90 days. This happened last Tuesday so the evidence exists — but it is worth telling the customer that a concurrency bug reported four months late is not investigable, which is a real argument for the failure-trend monitoring in Week 8.

The sentence to the merchandiser does three things. Removes blame, explains the timing (which is the part that makes them doubt you), and commits to a specific deliverable by a specific time. What it deliberately does not do is explain concurrency. They need to know it is understood and bounded, not how it works.

What a weak answer looks like: "increase parallelWorkerCount so it keeps up," which is the intuitive response to anything described as breaking under load, and here it makes the corruption worse.

Sources
  • Creating Workflows — dynamicMessageGroupId, messageGroupId and parallelWorkerCount — the serialization strategies and their defaults.
  • Observing Workflows — the Process List fields checked above — status, triggerEvent, task outputs, start/end times — and the 90-day retention.
  • Concept 6 — Concurrency and message grouping — the ceilings: 25 per workflow, 60 per org, adjustable via the CSM.
  • Week 9 — Symptom shape → class of cause — "intermittent, load-correlated, different records each time" → concurrency race.

Quiz

  1. Name the three components of a workflow and where workflows are declared.

    Triggers, conditionals and actions — a trigger says when, conditionals say which branch, actions say what happens. They are declared in an app's app.yml under a top-level workflows: key. That location matters: workflows ship inside apps, so they are versioned artefacts rather than console settings.

  2. List the six trigger categories. Which would you use for a nightly archival job, and which for reacting to another app finishing its work?

    (1) System events, (2) manual, (3) custom app events, (4) declared custom trigger keys, (5) scheduled (cron), (6) webhook. Nightly archival job → scheduled, via a crons declaration with standard CRON syntax (manual is also defensible if something external drives it). Reacting to another app finishing → custom app events, using triggerKey: '@{publisherOrgSlug}/{appName}:{EventName}' — e.g. '@vibeiq/image-loader:ImageAssignmentComplete'.

  3. What is the {entityType}|{action} pattern, and what is notable about the triggers available for assortment?

    System events are broadcast as {entityType}|{action} — item|create, item|update, item|delete, and equivalents for project-item, assortment-item, plan-placeholder, custom-entity, comment and content. What's notable about assortment: it has only assortment|publish — there is no assortment|update. So a requirement like "automate on any change to the assortment" cannot be met directly; it must hang off assortment-item events or off publish, which have very different timing. That's a requirements conversation, not a config task.

  4. Explain the first-match-wins rule. Describe the authoring bug it causes and how you'd spot it in review.

    "Only the first path whose conditional evaluates to true is executed; the remaining paths are skipped" — paths are an if / else-if chain, not independent rules. The bug: someone writes several paths intending "run all that apply" and puts a broad or true conditional near the top, making everything below it dead code. Nothing errors and the process reports success, so it presents as "only some of the automation runs." Spotting it: read the paths top-down and find the first conditional that can always be true — everything after it is unreachable.

  5. What are newData, oldData, propertyDiffs and changeObjects? Which is missing on a create, and why does that matter during a bulk load?

    newData — entity state after the change. oldData — state before. propertyDiffs — a map of changed-property metadata (propertyName, oldValue, newValue). changeObjects — an array of {id, changes} for bulk-change workflows. oldData is absent on creates. That matters during a bulk load because a load creates many entities at once: a conditional like {newData.status} !== {oldData.status} evaluates differently for new rows than for existing ones in the same file, so the workflow fires for part of the batch and not the rest — which looks like a bug and isn't.

  6. How can one action be reused across two workflows with different targets? What does that imply for environment promotion?

    By declaring actionConfiguration properties on the action, then supplying per-workflow config values — so one syncItem action can point at a production endpoint in one workflow and staging in another. Config values are "merged with runtime context and passed to the action's execute function via the config argument." For environment promotion: those values travel with the workflow definition, so a promoted workflow can carry a staging endpoint into production. Always check config values during any promotion review — and note it compounds with using one API key across environments.

  7. Contrast static messageGroupId with dynamicMessageGroupId. Why is dynamic grouping usually the better answer at volume?

    Static messageGroupId puts every event for the workflow in one queue — with parallelWorkerCount: 1 that's strict one-at-a-time processing. Dynamic (messageGroup + dynamicMessageGroupId: 'prefix-{newData.itemFamilyId}') serialises only events sharing the computed key, letting different keys run in parallel up to parallelWorkerCount. Dynamic is usually better at volume because it gives correctness exactly where entities can collide and throughput everywhere else — static grouping is the safe, slow hammer that turns a concurrency problem into a queue backlog.

  8. What are the default concurrency limits per workflow and per org, and why does the org limit matter to a customer running an unrelated integration?

    25 concurrent processes per event workflow, 60 per organization (both adjustable via the Customer Success Manager); parallelWorkerCount is 1–30 with a default of 5. The org limit matters because it's a shared budget across every workflow, integration and bulk load in the org, with no documented way for a customer to see their own utilisation. So one chatty workflow — or a bulk load firing thousands of events — can starve an unrelated integration, and the symptom is "our other integration got slow on Tuesday" with nothing wrong in that integration at all.

  9. Trace a workflow from app.yml to running in a customer org. Why does that make "just tweak the automation" a release conversation?

    Workflows are declared in app.yml → contrail app publish turns them into Workflow Template Definitions attached to that app version → an installing org runs contrail workflow-template-definitions install to instantiate them as live workflows, preserving config values end to end. So "just tweak the automation" is a release conversation: change the app, publish a version, install it. Workflows are versioned artefacts of an app, not settings someone edits in a console — which sets the customer's expectations about turnaround, and is the config/customization boundary (Week 7) showing up in practice.

  10. A customer reports automation that silently stopped "a few months ago." What do you check first, and what constraint might already have destroyed your evidence?

    Check first: the Process List — is there any process for that workflow at all? Then isActive, whether it's still installed in this org and from which app version, and whether the app was republished or promoted around that date. The constraint that may have destroyed the evidence: "after 90 days, workflow logs (including the list of processes and tasks) are permanently deleted." So a problem that began "a few months ago" may have no surviving process records. Establish what evidence still exists before promising root cause — and note that deleting a workflow destroys its logs immediately, not after 90 days.

Hands-on challenge

Build your workflow review checklist — the things you'd inspect before signing off any customer workflow going to production. Cover at minimum: unreachable paths, create-vs-update behaviour, access grants, config values pointing at the right environment, message grouping vs. what can collide, org-level concurrency budget, and who is alerted on FAILED. This joins the Week 2 data-model health check and the Week 3 publish checklist to complete your account-inheritance kit.

The answer

Workflow review checklist. Run before any customer workflow goes to production, and on every workflow you inherit.

1 · Paths and reachability

  • Read the paths in order. Is any conditional a superset of a later one? If so, the later path is dead — first match wins and nothing errors.
  • Are paths ordered narrow-to-broad? Requirements are written broad-to-narrow, so this inverts naturally and wrongly.
  • Is there an intended catch-all, and is it last?
  • What happens when no path matches — is that deliberate, or did someone assume a default?

2 · Create vs. update behaviour

  • Does any conditional reference {oldData.…}? On a create oldData is absent, so comparisons like {oldData.x} !== "y" evaluate true and the path fires for everything.
  • Is the workflow bound to |create, |update, or both — and was that a decision or a default?
  • What does this do during a 20,000-row load? Every upsert emits an event; every bound workflow fires once per row. Walk the create path specifically, with that number in mind.
  • Does it distinguish "status is approved" from "status changed to approved"? The first re-fires on every unrelated edit.

3 · Access grants

  • Which entity types does the app declare it may read and write? Does that match what the actions actually touch?
  • Is anything granted that is no longer used? Grants accumulate and nobody removes them.
  • A missing grant frequently presents as an action silently doing nothing rather than as an obvious permission error — check the grants before concluding the logic is wrong.

4 · Configuration values and environment

  • Every URL, endpoint, channel and key in the workflow config — does it point at the environment this org actually is?
  • A workflow promoted from staging with a staging endpoint succeeds: it posts correctly, to the wrong place. No failure anywhere.
  • Are secrets referenced rather than inlined?
  • Remember configuration and apps promote by two different mechanisms — confirm both moved.

5 · Concurrency and grouping

  • Does any action read-modify-write, or touch shared parent state (copy-down, rollups, sequence numbers)? If yes, grouping is required, not advisable.
  • What is the group key, and does it match the unit that must not collide — usually the family or the parent entity?
  • Is parallelWorkerCount above 1 without any grouping? That is a latent race: works at low volume, corrupts under bulk.
  • Has anyone raised the worker count to fix a speed complaint? That is the standard move and it converts slow into corrupting.

6 · Org concurrency budget

  • 25 concurrent processes per workflow, 60 per org — shared with every other integration and load.
  • What else runs at the same hour? Add up the intended worker counts across all workflows and compare to 60.
  • Does grouping create long-lived queued processes that hold capacity?
  • Is a ceiling increase needed, and has anyone asked the CSM? Do it before the season, not during it.

7 · Serial task execution

  • Tasks run strictly in order and fail closed: if task one fails, tasks two and three never run — not failed, not retried, simply never executed.
  • Is the most failure-prone action (usually the external call) ordered after the ones that must happen regardless?
  • Would a partial execution leave inconsistent state, and is that recoverable?

8 · Failure visibility

  • Who is alerted on FAILED, by name? "The team watches the dashboard" means nobody.
  • Is anyone watching the failure trend? A rising line is visible weeks before a ticket.
  • Does the customer know evidence is retained 90 days, so an unexamined failure eventually becomes unexplainable?
  • Do task outputs contain enough to diagnose from, or just "error"?

9 · Change control

  • Workflows are versioned artefacts of an app, not console settings — who can publish a new app version, and what is the path to production?
  • Is the deployed version the one in source control?
  • Never delete a workflow to disable it. Deletion destroys its processes, tasks and logs immediately — the evidence you would need. Disable instead.

Scoring: sections 1, 2 and 5 produce wrong data with no error and block go-live. Section 4 produces correct behaviour aimed at the wrong environment — also blocking, and the easiest to miss. Sections 6–8 are operational risk. Section 9 is governance, and the one customers thank you for a year later.

How to think about it

Nearly every item here is a failure that produces no error. An unreachable path, a create-path storm, a staging endpoint in production, a lost update — all report success. That is what makes workflows worth a dedicated checklist: you cannot review them by looking for failures, because the dangerous ones do not generate any.

Section 2 is where load and automation meet, and reviewers routinely miss it. A workflow behaves reasonably in daily use and catastrophically during a bulk load, and the two are reviewed by different people at different times. Ask "what does this do to 20,000 rows?" of every workflow, even ones that look unrelated to loading — that single question would have prevented the 1,200 Slack messages in Week 4's scenario.

Section 5 asks about the action, not the setting. "Is grouping configured?" invites a yes/no. "Does any action read-modify-write?" identifies whether grouping is required — and if it is, an ungrouped workflow with a high worker count is a defect regardless of how long it has run without incident.

Section 6 is the item that makes this a platform review rather than a code review. Every workflow looks fine in isolation; capacity is the only thing that can only be assessed across all of them at once. Nobody owns the org budget by default, which is precisely why it should be on a TAM's checklist.

The delete-vs-disable rule in section 9 is small and irreversible. Someone tidying up a customer's workflow list destroys the log history you would need to explain last month's incident — instantly, with no undo. It costs one sentence to say and it is the kind of thing a customer only learns from you.

Pair the score with a recommendation, not a count. Sections 1, 2, 4 and 5 are the blocking set; the rest can be a plan. A twenty-item findings list with no priority gets triaged by the person who wrote the workflow — see the Week 5 review for why that matters more than being right.

Sources
  • Creating Workflows — sections 1, 2, 5 and 7 — paths and first-match-wins, conditional inputs, grouping and worker count, and serial task execution.
  • Observing Workflows — section 8 — statuses, task outputs, the Process List, and the 90-day retention.
  • Workflows — introduction — section 3 — access grants and the app-declared surface a workflow may touch.
  • Configuration Copy — section 4 — why environment promotion is two mechanisms, and what each one moves.
  • Key findings — Decisions you cannot undo — section 9 — deleting a workflow destroys its evidence immediately.

I know this when I can…

  • Read a workflow block in app.yml and predict its behaviour on both create and update.
  • Choose a trigger category from a requirement, and spot requirements no trigger supports.
  • Find unreachable paths by inspection.
  • Recognise a concurrency race from the shape of the symptom and write the grouping fix.
  • Work the diagnostic ladder from Process List to taskOutputs without guessing.
  • Explain to a customer why changing a workflow is a release, not a setting.
