Module 7 · Week 7

Apps, Extensions & the config/customization boundary

How far you can go without code, where the line actually is, and what crossing it costs in money, time and people. This is the judgement call you'll be asked to make on nearly every account — usually phrased as "can the platform just do X?"

On this page

  1. 🤝The spectrum
  2. ⚙️Formulas
  3. ⚙️Rule Sets
  4. ⚙️The rest of the configuration layer
  5. ⚙️Configuration Copy, and how environments actually work
  6. ⚙️Apps: identity, ownership, versions
  7. ⚙️Extensions
  8. 🤝Holding the line
  9. 📚Recommended reading
  10. ✅Practical exercise
  11. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  12. ✅Quiz
  13. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) place any customer request on the configuration→customization spectrum and justify it, (2) write and debug formulas and rule sets, including their silent failure modes, (3) explain how configuration moves between orgs and what that means for environment strategy, (4) describe app identity, ownership and versioning — and the decisions at app creation that can never be undone, and (5) say what an extension is, what it costs to run, and when it's the wrong answer.

Concept 1 — The spectrum

"Configuration vs. customization" is a false binary. It's a ladder, and each rung costs more to build, more to change, and involves more people.

Your default question for any "can it do X?" is "what is the lowest rung that solves this?" Customers and implementation partners both drift upward — engineers reach for code because code is what they do. Every rung you avoid climbing is change latency the customer keeps and support surface you don't inherit. Equally, forcing a Level 1 answer onto a Level 2 problem produces a fragile pile of formulas nobody can maintain. The skill is naming the right rung, not the lowest one.

Concept 2 — Formulas

Formulas assign property values programmatically from entity state. Inputs vary by entity type:

EntityAvailable inputs
All entitiesobj (the entity's properties), context.previousObj (pre-update state)
Itemsplus obj.roles — the array distinguishing family from option
Plan placeholdersobj.itemFamily, obj.itemOption, obj.itemType
Project itemsobj.item, context.project
Assortment itemsobj.item (option-preferred, falling back to family)

Return types must match the property: "If the Property Type is \"number\", then the formula should return a numeric value," and dates must be ISO strings via toISOstring().

Evaluation timing

  • Run on create and update for all entities.
  • ProjectItem formulas re-run on Item updates.
  • Plan placeholder formulas run on load, on placeholder updates, and on any row cell modification.
  • Plan publish triggers all Item, ProjectItem and AssortmentItem formulas.

Three silent failure modes — memorise these

  1. Misspelled slugs return undefined, silently. The docs are explicit: "Verify every slug against the live Type before saving a formula." No error, no warning — just an empty or wrong value. This is the single most common formula bug, and it looks exactly like a data problem.
  2. "Updates to Reference Data does not cause Formulas to re-run." So a formula that derives from reference data produces a value that is correct when written and quietly stale forever after. The customer changes a lookup, nothing recalculates, and they discover it a season later.
  3. "On the Frontend, only Formulas in Plans are run." Plus only one level of object hydration — you cannot reach nested references beyond immediate properties. A formula that works in one surface may not evaluate in another.

Concept 3 — Rule Sets

Rule Sets "control whether a property is editable, required, what option values are allowed in a dropdown, and what min/max range is permitted on a number — based on the current state of the entity."

A rule set is JSON with a name (used by CLI and Configuration Copy), an identifier (immutable, unique per org), and a ruleSet array of conditional blocks. Each block has criteria (when it applies) and rules (what it constrains). A rule targets a property by slug and can set:

FieldEffect
editablefalse locks the property; true is the default
requiredBlocks save when empty
optionSetValuesAllow-list of permitted values
minValue / maxValueNumeric boundaries
validationFunctionOverrides default validation

"ALL" applies a rule to every property; excludedSlugs carves out exceptions. Filter conditions include equals, not_equal_to, starts_with, contains, less_than, greater_than, is_empty, is_not_empty, is_any_of — validity depending on property type.

Evaluation semantics — get these exactly right

  • The outer array is OR — each block tests independently.
  • Inner criteria are AND — all conditions in a block must match.
  • "Editability is 'locked wins'" — if any matching rule says editable: false, the property is locked. You cannot re-open it with a later rule.
  • Validation constraints accumulate across all matching rules.

Rule Sets are a UX guardrail, not a data-integrity guarantee

The docs state rule sets "evaluate client-side, affecting editor experience for all users." That strongly implies the Loader and the API are not bound by them — a load can write a value a rule set forbids in the UI. Treat this as the working assumption, because the alternative (assuming enforcement) is the dangerous error, and it matches the pattern where "the UI wouldn't let us do that, so how is this value here?" turns out to be an integration write. Confirm the exact enforcement boundary internally before you assert it to a customer — it belongs on your colleague-questions list, and it changes how you design validation for any integrated field.

Concept 4 — The rest of the configuration layer

  • Validation Functions — custom validation, referenceable from a rule via validationFunction.
  • Blueprints — templated structures for repeatable setup (the docs carry a placeholder-colour example).
  • Uniqueness — constraints preventing duplicate values. Worth pairing mentally with Week 4: uniqueness rules and federatedId upsert are different mechanisms answering related questions, and a customer may need both.
  • Option Sets — the value lists behind Single/Multi Select. Recall Week 4: display labels in a CSV are converted to internal option values during preprocessing, so option-set drift breaks loads.

Option Set Hierarchies — cascading dropdowns, and two silent failures

An Option Set Hierarchy (OSH) creates a parent-child cascade between single-select properties: "selecting a parent value narrows the values available in its child dropdown." Choosing Merch Size Group = APPAREL might restrict Merch Division Code to M-APP, W-APP, K-APP. Chains can be two levels or many — Segment → Category → SubCategory — and "the order of the parent → child chain is fixed by the hierarchy list. Changing the order in that list changes which property filters which."

When a user opens a child dropdown the engine finds the child's position in the chain, walks the options tree filtering by the parent values currently selected, and returns what is allowed at that depth. If a parent has no selection yet, it flattens all branches and shows every descendant value.

Two ways an OSH fails without saying so

  1. An empty dropdown. "If the parent holds a value that isn't in the hierarchy, the child dropdown will appear empty." So a legacy or imported parent value that predates the hierarchy — or one loaded straight past the UI — makes the child unusable, and the user reports "the dropdown is broken." It isn't; the parent is off-hierarchy.
  2. A config that silently doesn't load. The hierarchy is stored as an OptionSetHierarchy record plus a TypeOptionSetHierarchyLink attaching it to a Type, and it is keyed on TypePropertyIds — internal IDs, not slugs. "A slug-based config silently fails to load." Someone hand-writing or hand-editing the JSON with readable slugs gets no error and no hierarchy.

Loading one is a two-step CLI flow — parseOptionSetsHierarchyValuesToKeysLocalFile converts display values to keys, then parseOptionSetsHierarchyLocalFile generates the JSON — after which you create the hierarchy in the Admin Console and assign it to the Item and Plan Placeholder types.

Both failures present as user-facing bugs and are neither. "The dropdown is empty for some products" is an off-hierarchy parent value, which usually means data arrived through the Loader that the hierarchy was never told about — a Week 4 problem surfacing in Week 7's territory. Worth adding to your data-model health check: does every parent value in the data exist in the hierarchy?

Concept 5 — Configuration Copy, and how environments actually work

This answers a question that has been open since Week 1: how does configuration move between orgs?

contrail types getAll     # export this org's configuration to the working directory
contrail types loadAll    # import into the destination org

Eleven categories are exported: option set hierarchies · option sets · rule sets · type policies · type property policies · types · rule set links · type option set hierarchy links · type policy links · type property policy links · usage info. Output is a folder tree of .yml files. Identifiers matter: types are keyed by typePath (e.g. item:footwear), while option set hierarchies and policies use identifier.

To promote between environments, you point the configuration at a different org and run loadAll.

Three things follow, and all three are worth saying out loud to a customer early:

  • Configuration is exportable as text, so it can live in version control. Customers who commit their types export get review, history and rollback on their configuration. Recommend this on day one; it is the single cheapest maturity upgrade available to them.
  • It is a whole-org export, not a cherry-pick. Promotion is a bulk operation, so environments drift apart unless promotion is disciplined and one-directional.
  • Configuration Copy moves configuration; apps move separately. Types, rule sets and policies go via types loadAll; workflows and actions go via app publish and install. A "promotion" is therefore two mechanisms that must stay in step — and a mismatch between them is a real and confusing failure mode.

Concept 6 — Apps: identity, ownership, versions

Above the configuration layer, everything ships inside an app. Three facts about app creation are permanent, and you should be in the room when they're decided.

Three irreversible decisions

  1. The identifier is global and immutable. "Creating a contrail app (contrail app create) reserves the app's identifier globally. Since no two apps can share the same identifier, and app identifier can not be altered after creation, it is important to be diligent about choosing the correct identifier." Format is org@domain/app-name.
  2. Ownership is permanent and non-transferable. "Each app is owned by an org. The org that first creates an app is the owner of the app. App ownership can not be modified." Only the owner can modify, publish or delete it.
  3. Publishing creates versions, and installs are what put a version into an org.

Point 2 is a commercial landmine, not a technical footnote. If a systems integrator or VibeIQ PS creates the customer's app in their org, the customer can never own it — not at renewal, not when they change partners, not ever. The remedy after the fact is rebuilding under a new identifier, which also can't reuse the name. Ask "whose org is this app being created in, and is that where it should live in three years?" before anyone types contrail app create. Nobody thanks you for raising it; everyone would have thanked you later.

Visibility is set in app.yml:

visibility: private            # default

visibility: private           # private, shared with named orgs
accessGrants:
  - orgSlug: recipient-org
    permissions:
      - install

visibility: public            # marketplace candidate

Public listing isn't self-serve: "you can submit a request to include your app on the public App Marketplace," which requires VibeIQ approval via the customer success manager. Another lead-time item for an implementation plan.

Installation happens through the Marketplace or the CLI (contrail app install). And from Week 6: publishing turns declared workflows into Workflow Template Definitions on that app version, which an installing org instantiates with contrail workflow-template-definitions install.

Concept 7 — Extensions

Extensions add UI. They ship inside apps and are declared in the manifest:

extensions:
  - identifier: hello-world
    name: Hello World
    userApps: [BOARDS, PLAN]
    display:
      type: modal
      dimensions: {width: 660px, height: 300px}
    iframeUrl: https://example.com
    extensionType: DOCUMENT_AUTOMATION
extensionTypeWhat it does
DOCUMENT_AUTOMATIONReads document context and performs actions on boards, plans and showcases
CONTEXTUAL_ACTIONPresented dynamically based on the user's current context
ADMIN_UTILITYExtends the Admin Console

Display is either a modal — "a centered dialog with a backdrop. Best for focused tasks" — or a side-menu, "a panel docked to the right edge with no backdrop," which lets the user keep working in the document. Side-menu suits inspector-style tools; modal suits a task you start and finish.

The runtime API: AppExtension.registerAppExtension() establishes the iframe connection, getAppContext() reads the current board/plan/showcase and the selected elements, and BoardsApp / PlanApp / ShowcaseApp perform document modifications.

The hosting consequence

"The extension is shown to the user in an iframe" and requires public HTTPS hosting — self-hosted (Netlify, Vercel) or VibeIQ's managed option. So an extension is a service the customer runs: it has uptime, TLS certificates, a deployment process and an on-call owner. When it is down, users see a broken panel inside VibeIQ and will report it as a VibeIQ outage. Establish who owns that hosting before the extension ships, and make sure it is monitored by someone.

Concept 8 — Holding the line

A practical routing table for "can it do X?":

If the request is…Reach for
A derived value from data on the same entityFormula
"This field should be locked / required / limited in these situations"Rule Set
Custom validation logic on a fieldValidation Function via a rule
"When X happens, do Y" — reacting to changeWorkflow (Week 6)
"Send it to another system"Workflow action or integration (Week 5)
"Users need a screen to do Z"Extension — and a hosting conversation
BOM costing rollups, PO managementWrong system (Week 1). Not a feature request.

The most valuable sentence you can offer isn't yes or no — it's "that's possible, and here's what it costs to own." A rule set is a config change an admin makes on a Tuesday. An extension is a hosted service with an on-call rota. Both are legitimate; they are not the same commitment, and customers routinely agree to the second while believing they're buying the first. Making the ownership cost explicit at the decision point is the whole job here.

Recommended reading

  • Configuration — getting started, then Formulas and Rule Sets properly.
  • Configuration Copy — the environment-promotion mechanism.
  • App Lifecycle — identity, ownership, versions, visibility.
  • Extensions — types, display modes, the context API.

Practical exercise

Route these seven requests to a rung, and for each write one sentence on cost of ownership:

  1. "Margin % should calculate automatically from cost and retail price."
  2. "Once a style is approved, nobody should be able to change its category."
  3. "When a style is approved, create a record in our PLM."
  4. "Merchandisers need a panel showing last season's sell-through next to each row while they plan."
  5. "Only these six colours should be selectable for the outlet channel."
  6. "We need the landed cost recalculated whenever the FX rate table changes."
  7. "We want to manage supplier purchase orders in VibeIQ."

Number 6 is the interesting one. Explain precisely why the obvious answer fails, and what you'd propose instead.

The answer

1 · "Margin % should calculate automatically from cost and retail price."
Level 1 — Formula. A derived value from data on the same entity, which is the definition of a formula.
Cost of ownership: minutes to change, an admin can do it, no deploy — but it is a silent-failure surface: if an input is null the output is quietly wrong rather than erroring, and nobody notices until a margin looks odd.

2 · "Once a style is approved, nobody should be able to change its category."
Level 1 — Rule Set. Conditional editability based on current entity state is precisely what rule sets control.
Cost of ownership: cheap and admin-editable — but rule sets are documented as evaluating client-side, so this constrains the editor, not the API or the Loader. If the requirement is really "this value must never change after approval, by any means," a rule set alone does not deliver it.

3 · "When a style is approved, create a record in our PLM."
Level 2 — Workflow. "When X happens, do Y" reacting to a change, with an action calling out.
Cost of ownership: lives in an app, so changes are a release cycle, not a console edit — and it inherits everything from Week 6: create-vs-update behaviour, grouping, org concurrency budget, and someone to alert on FAILED.

4 · "A panel showing last season's sell-through next to each row while they plan."
Level 3 — Extension (Document Automation, reading document context in Plan).
Cost of ownership: the expensive one. It is code plus a hosted UI on someone's public HTTPS host — so hosting, TLS, uptime, on-call, and a security review, forever. Ask who operates it in three years before agreeing to build it.

5 · "Only these six colours should be selectable for the outlet channel."
Level 1 — Rule Set restricting allowed option values conditionally.
Cost of ownership: cheap, but the same client-side caveat as #2 — and worth checking whether "outlet channel" is knowable from the entity being edited, since a rule set evaluates against current entity state.

6 · "Recalculate landed cost whenever the FX rate table changes." — see below.

7 · "Manage supplier purchase orders in VibeIQ."
Not a rung — wrong system. POs are transactional ERP territory, downstream of the decision phase entirely. This is the Week 1 boundary, and the answer is a redirect, not an estimate.
Cost of ownership: building it would mean owning a second system of record for transactions, permanently, against a platform not designed for it — and it does not become cheaper by being built at a lower rung.

6 · Why the obvious answer fails

The obvious answer is a formula: landed cost = base cost × FX rate. It fails for a reason that is easy to miss and expensive to discover.

A formula computes from the entity's own state, and it runs when that entity changes. The FX rate table is not part of the item. When the rate changes, nothing happens to any item — no item was written, so no formula re-evaluates. Ten thousand products keep their old landed cost indefinitely, and the values are not obviously wrong; they are stale. They will silently update, one at a time, whenever someone happens to edit an unrelated field on a style — which produces a data set where landed cost was computed at a dozen different exchange rates, with no way to tell which.

That is the deeper trap: this is the silent formula failure mode that produces a value correct today and wrong next season. A formula referencing anything that changes independently of the entity bakes in a snapshot and never revisits it.

What I'd propose instead

Level 2 — a workflow triggered by the change to the rate table, which recalculates the affected items. The trigger has to be the thing that actually changed. Concretely: model the FX rates as a Custom Entity so that updating them emits custom-entity|update, and bind a workflow to it that walks the affected items and writes the new landed cost. Alternatively, if rates arrive on a schedule, a scheduled workflow that recalculates nightly — simpler, and it makes staleness bounded and predictable rather than unbounded.

Two things to settle with the customer before building either: volume (recalculating 10,000 items is an event storm against a 60-process org budget — grouping and worker count matter), and whether recalculation should be retroactive. Re-pricing a season that has already been costed and committed may be actively wrong; "recalculate whenever the FX rate changes" is usually shorthand for "for the season currently being planned," and nobody says so out loud.

How to think about it

The question is "what is the lowest rung that actually solves this?" — with the emphasis on actually. The skill is naming the right rung, not the lowest one. Forcing a Level 1 answer onto a Level 2 problem produces a pile of formulas nobody can maintain, which is exactly what request 6 tempts you into.

Test every candidate against its trigger, not just its logic. Formula logic for landed cost is trivially correct; the mechanism never fires. When routing a request, ask two separate questions — can this rung express the calculation? and will this rung run at the right moment? Requests phrased "whenever X changes" are a reliable signal that the answer is a workflow, because they name a trigger explicitly and formulas do not have one.

Cost of ownership is a headcount and latency question, not a build estimate. The honest comparison for #4 is not "two weeks of work" but "who runs this server in three years, and what happens when it is down during a line review?" Every rung you avoid climbing is change latency the customer keeps and support surface you do not inherit. Customers and SIs both drift upward — engineers reach for code because code is what they do.

Two requests here have a caveat you must state rather than assume away. Rule sets evaluate client-side, so #2 and #5 constrain the editor and — on the documented evidence — not the API or Loader. Whether they are enforced on write paths is genuinely unconfirmed, so the correct move is "let me confirm," not a confident answer in either direction. Saying it that way is what makes you believable on everything else.

Request 7 is a category error, and answering it as an estimate is the failure. The reflex to be helpful turns "we want POs in VibeIQ" into a scoping conversation. It is a wrong-system conversation, and the redirect is cheaper for everyone now than after someone has half-built it.

What a weak answer looks like: routing all seven correctly with no cost-of-ownership sentences. The rung is the easy half. The sentence about who maintains it, how long a change takes, and what fails silently is what turns this into an artefact a customer's product owner can use — which is the point of the routing guide.

Sources
  • Formulas — what inputs a formula receives and when it evaluates — the mechanism behind why request 6 fails.
  • Rule Sets — editability, required, allowed values and ranges based on entity state — requests 2 and 5, and the client-side evaluation note.
  • Creating Workflows — the trigger categories behind the request-6 alternative, including custom-entity and scheduled triggers.
  • Extensions — request 4 — the three extension types, and the hosted-iframe requirement that drives its cost of ownership.
  • Week 1 — Where VibeIQ sits — request 7 — the boundary that makes purchase orders a wrong-system conversation.

Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic is 8 weeks from go-live. Their systems integrator sends a design note:

"For the pricing rules we'll build a Document Automation extension — a modal where merchants enter the price and our service validates it against the margin floor before writing back. We've already run contrail app create under our integrator org so we can iterate fast; we'll transfer it to Summit at handover. We're hosting the extension on our own Vercel account for now. Also, we noticed rule sets can enforce the min value, but our load overwrites those prices anyway, so the extension is the safer place for the rule."

There are at least four problems here, of very different kinds — one permanent, one operational, one architectural, and one that is actually a correct observation pointing at the wrong conclusion. Identify each; say which is unfixable after go-live and why; give the alternative design; and note which of their claims you would verify internally before responding rather than accepting or disputing on the spot.

The answer

Problem 1 — permanent: the app was created in the integrator's org.

"We'll transfer it to Summit at handover" is not possible. The org that first creates an app is the owner, and app ownership cannot be modified. On top of that the app identifier is reserved globally and immutably at creation, so it cannot even be recreated under the same name. Summit's pricing capability would be permanently owned by a vendor they may not be working with in two years — with no technical route to reclaim it.

Problem 2 — operational: the extension is hosted on the integrator's Vercel account.

Extensions run in an iframe from a public HTTPS host. That host is on Summit's critical path during line reviews, and it belongs to a third party — their uptime, their TLS certificate, their billing, their staff. Summit's InfoSec will also want to know that document context crosses into it. "For now" is doing a lot of work in that sentence; "for now" arrangements survive go-live routinely.

Problem 3 — architectural: a Level 3 extension for a Level 1 problem.

A margin floor on a price field is a rule set — conditional validation on entity state, admin-editable, no deploy, nothing to host. They are proposing code plus a hosted UI plus a service, which the customer must then own forever, to enforce a minimum value. Every rung climbed is change latency Summit keeps and support surface they inherit.

Problem 4 — the correct observation, wrong conclusion.

"Rule sets can enforce the min value, but our load overwrites those prices anyway" is probably right about the mechanism — rule sets are documented as evaluating client-side, affecting the editor experience, which implies the Loader is not bound by them. The conclusion is what fails: an extension does not fix that either. An extension is also a UI-layer control. If the Loader can write a below-floor price past a rule set, it can write one past the extension too — because the load never goes through either. They have correctly identified that the UI cannot govern the write path, then proposed a different UI.

Which is unfixable after go-live: problem 1. Every other item is a refactor — annoying, scoped, doable. App ownership and the global identifier are decided once, at contrail app create, and there is no later remedy. This is why it has to be raised now, at eight weeks, and why it outranks the architectural objection even though the architecture is the more interesting problem.

The alternative design

  1. Create the app under Summit's org, today. The integrator gets developer access and can iterate exactly as fast. This is the one item I would not leave the meeting without.
  2. Enforce the margin floor with a rule set (min value, conditional on channel or status), optionally with a validation function if the logic exceeds what a rule can express. No hosting, no deploy, admin-editable.
  3. Fix the real problem — the load — at the load. If below-floor prices arrive via the Loader, the control belongs in the pipeline: correct the source, or use a conditional column, or validate before the file is submitted. UI controls cannot govern a write path that does not touch the UI.
  4. Drop the extension unless a genuine UI need survives — a panel showing something merchants cannot otherwise see. If one does, host it on infrastructure Summit owns, and agree who is on call.

What I would verify internally before responding

Whether rule sets are enforced on the API and Loader write paths. The docs say client-side, which implies not — but "implies" is not good enough to build a design on, and the integrator has effectively asked the question. If rule sets are enforced server-side, item 2 above solves the whole problem and the load argument evaporates. If they are not, item 3 is mandatory. I would say "that is a good question and I want to give you a definite answer rather than a guess" and come back within a day — rather than agreeing on the call, or disputing it.

I would also confirm current guidance on developer access to an app owned by another org, so that recommendation 1 comes with a workable process attached rather than as an obstacle.

How to think about it

Sort the findings by reversibility before saying any of them. Three of these four can be fixed next quarter. One cannot be fixed at all after someone runs a single CLI command. That ordering — not severity, not how wrong the architecture is — decides what you lead with and what you are willing to be unpopular about.

Problem 4 is the one that separates a good reviewer from a checklist. It is tempting to mark the whole note wrong, but the integrator has noticed something real and important: the UI is not the write path. Rejecting the observation along with the conclusion loses you the person and the insight. The move is to say "you are right about the mechanism, and it argues against your fix as well" — which is both accurate and disarming.

Notice where each proposed control sits relative to the data path. A rule set and an extension are both UI-layer. A load bypasses the UI entirely. Once you draw that line, the design error is obvious and does not require any argument about extensions being expensive. Ask "which write paths does this control actually intercept?" of every validation proposal.

Say "let me confirm" and mean it. This is the single most valuable habit in the module. The rule-set enforcement boundary is genuinely unsettled in the public documentation, and you are eight weeks from go-live with a decision resting on it. Guessing right still teaches the customer that you guess. Coming back in a day with a definite answer is what makes your other assertions worth trusting — and this one is on the standing colleague-questions list precisely because it keeps mattering.

"For now" is a review flag, not a qualifier. "Under our org for now", "on our Vercel for now" — temporary arrangements made under delivery pressure become permanent by default, because nothing forces revisiting them. The question that surfaces this is "what is the event that would change this, and who owns it?" Usually there is no answer.

What a weak answer looks like: leading with the architectural objection because it is the most intellectually satisfying finding. The extension being over-engineered is real and fixable. The app ownership is a single command away from being permanent, and if the meeting runs short and you covered architecture, you have failed the customer in the one way that cannot be undone.

Sources
  • App Lifecycle — problem 1 — the global immutable identifier and the rule that app ownership cannot be modified.
  • Extensions — problems 2 and 3 — extension types, the hosted-iframe model, and what the customer takes on by running one.
  • Rule Sets — problems 3 and 4 — what a rule set can enforce, and the client-side evaluation wording the verification question turns on.
  • Questions for VibeIQ colleagues — the rule-set enforcement boundary is on the standing list — this scenario is why it matters commercially, not just technically.
  • Key findings — Decisions you cannot undo — app ownership and app identifier both appear there.

Quiz

  1. Describe the five rungs of the spectrum, and give the question you ask before choosing one.

    L0 types & properties (admin console) · L1 formulas, rule sets, validation functions, blueprints (config, no deploy) · L2 workflows & actions (code, inside an app) · L3 extensions (code plus hosted UI) · L4 external systems. Cost, change latency and headcount all rise together — minutes and one admin at L0, a release cycle and engineers plus hosting and on-call at L3. The question: "what is the lowest rung that actually solves this?" — not the lowest rung outright, since a Level 1 answer to a Level 2 problem is a fragile pile of formulas nobody can maintain.

  2. What inputs does a formula get on a ProjectItem, and when does a plan publish cause formulas to run?

    On a ProjectItem a formula gets obj (the entity's properties), context.previousObj (pre-update state), plus obj.item and context.project. Formulas run on create and update for all entities, and ProjectItem formulas additionally re-run on Item updates. A plan publish triggers all Item, ProjectItem and AssortmentItem formulas — worth remembering, because publish is already a heavy moment and this adds recalculation to it.

  3. Name the three silent formula failure modes. Which produces a value that is correct today and wrong next season?

    (1) Misspelled slugs return undefined silently — "verify every slug against the live Type before saving a formula." (2) Reference data updates don't re-run formulas. (3) Only Plan formulas run on the frontend, with one level of object hydration, so a formula may not evaluate on every surface. Correct today, wrong next season: number 2 — a value derived from reference data is right when written and then quietly stale forever, so changing an FX table or a lookup recalculates nothing.

  4. What can a rule set control? Explain OR/AND evaluation and what "locked wins" means in practice.

    A rule set controls whether a property is editable, required, which option values are allowed, and min/max on a number — based on the entity's current state, with an optional validationFunction override. Evaluation: the outer array is OR (each block tests independently) and inner criteria are AND (all must match). "Locked wins" means that if any matching rule declares editable: false, the property is locked — you cannot re-open it with a later rule, and validation constraints accumulate across all matching rules rather than the last one winning.

  5. Why should you not describe rule sets to a customer as a data-integrity guarantee — and what would you confirm before saying anything definite?

    Because they "evaluate client-side, affecting editor experience for all users" — which strongly implies the API and Loader are not bound by them, so a load can write a value the UI would forbid. Treat that as the working assumption, since assuming enforcement is the more dangerous error and it matches the pattern where "the UI wouldn't let us do that, so how is this value here?" turns out to be an integration write. Confirm internally exactly where enforcement sits before saying anything definite — it decides how validation must be designed for every integrated field.

  6. What do contrail types getAll / loadAll do, and what are the two mechanisms a full environment promotion actually requires?

    contrail types getAll exports an org's configuration to the working directory as a YAML folder tree — eleven categories including types, option sets, option set hierarchies, rule sets, type policies and their links. contrail types loadAll imports it into a destination org. A full environment promotion needs two mechanisms: configuration via types getAll/loadAll, and apps (workflows, actions, extensions) via contrail app publish and install. They must stay in step; a mismatch between them is a real and confusing failure mode.

  7. Why is committing the configuration export to version control such a cheap win?

    Because the configuration exports as plain YAML text, so committing it gives the customer review, history, diffing and rollback on their configuration for essentially no effort. It also turns "what changed?" from a guess into a diff — which matters because environments have no reconciliation mechanism and drift silently. It's the single cheapest maturity upgrade available to a customer, and worth recommending on day one rather than after the first unexplained config change.

  8. Which two decisions at contrail app create can never be undone, and what is the commercial risk in the second?

    (1) The app identifier — "reserves the app's identifier globally… cannot be altered after creation." (2) App ownership — "the org that first creates an app is the owner… App ownership can not be modified." The commercial risk in the second: if a systems integrator or PS team runs contrail app create in their org, the customer can never own that app — not at renewal, not when they change partners. Only the owner can modify, publish or delete it. The only remedy afterwards is rebuilding under a new identifier, which can't reuse the name. Ask "whose org is this being created in?" before anyone runs the command.

  9. Name the three extension types and the two display modes. When is a side-menu the right choice?

    Types: DOCUMENT_AUTOMATION (reads document context and acts on boards, plans, showcases), CONTEXTUAL_ACTION (presented dynamically by user context), ADMIN_UTILITY (extends the Admin Console). Display modes: modal — "a centered dialog with a backdrop. Best for focused tasks" — and side-menu — "a panel docked to the right edge with no backdrop." Side-menu is right when the user needs to keep working in the document while the extension is open: inspector-style tools, reference panels, anything consulted rather than completed.

  10. A customer wants a small UI for a weekly task. What do you tell them it costs to own, beyond the build?

    That it's a service they will run, not a feature they will receive. An extension is shown in an iframe from public HTTPS hosting — self-hosted or VibeIQ-managed — so it carries uptime, TLS certificates, a deployment process, dependency and runtime upkeep, and someone on call. And when it breaks, users see a broken panel inside VibeIQ and report it as a VibeIQ outage. Settle who owns and monitors the hosting before it ships. The useful sentence is not yes or no but "that's possible, and here's what it costs to own".

Hands-on challenge

Write your "can the platform do X?" routing guide — a one-page decision aid you could hand a customer's product owner. For each rung: what it's for, who can change it, how long a change takes, what it costs to own, and one worked example. Include the two "wrong system" boundaries from Week 1. This is the last artefact in your account-inheritance kit, and the one most likely to be read by someone other than you.

The answer

"Can the platform do X?" — a routing guide. Written for a customer's product owner, not for an engineer. One page, five rungs plus two boundaries.

Level 0 — Types & properties

  • For: new fields, new entity types, changing what data exists at all.
  • Who changes it: an admin, in the console.
  • How long: minutes.
  • Cost to own: near zero, but property-level choices here are load-bearing — get Family vs. Override wrong and you get overwritten data later.
  • Example: "We need a sustainability rating on every style."

Level 1 — Formulas, rule sets, validation functions

  • For: derived values, and constraints on what may be entered, based on the entity's own state.
  • Who: an admin. No deploy.
  • How long: minutes to hours.
  • Cost to own: low — with two caveats worth stating out loud. Formulas fail silently (a null input yields a quietly wrong value, not an error), and they only run when the entity changes, so anything depending on an external table goes stale. Rule sets are documented as evaluating client-side: they shape the editor, and on current evidence do not govern API or Loader writes.
  • Example: "Margin % should calculate itself." · "Category locks once approved."

Level 2 — Workflows & actions

  • For: "when X happens, do Y" — reacting to change, and sending things to other systems.
  • Who: engineers. It lives in an app.
  • How long: a release cycle. Not a console edit — "can you just tweak that automation?" is a versioned app change.
  • Cost to own: moderate and ongoing — someone must watch failures, and every workflow draws on the org's shared concurrency budget. A bulk load fires them once per row.
  • Example: "Push approved styles to PLM." · "Recalculate when the FX table changes."

Level 3 — Extensions

  • For: a screen or panel that does not exist — new UI inside Board, Plan or Showcase.
  • Who: engineers, plus whoever runs the hosting.
  • How long: a project, then a release cycle per change.
  • Cost to own: the big step up. Code plus a public HTTPS host: uptime, TLS, on-call, a security review, and a bill — permanently. Ask who operates this in three years before agreeing to build it.
  • Example: "Show last season's sell-through beside each row while planning."

Level 4 — External systems

  • For: capability that belongs in someone else's estate and integrates back.
  • Who: their teams, their roadmap.
  • How long: their release cycle.
  • Cost to own: highest, and mostly not yours — which is the point. The integration seam becomes the thing to design well.
  • Example: "Financial planning scenarios across the whole business."

The two boundaries — requests that are not a rung at all

  • "How do we make it?" Tech packs, BOMs, materials, costing, sourcing, samples. That is PLM. VibeIQ decides what to make, not how.
  • "What did we transact?" Purchase orders, inventory, invoices, receipts. That is ERP.

Both answers are a redirect, not an estimate. Scoping them as build work is how a platform ends up as a second, worse system of record.

How to use this in a conversation

  1. Restate the request as an outcome, not a solution. People arrive with "we need an extension"; the underlying need is usually two rungs lower.
  2. Ask what the lowest rung that actually solves it is — with the emphasis on actually. Check both that the rung can express the logic and that it will run at the right moment.
  3. Ask which write paths need to be governed. If data arrives by Loader or API, a UI-layer control does not cover it, whatever rung it sits on.
  4. State the cost of ownership before agreeing. Change latency and who maintains it, not build effort.
  5. Name it as a boundary if it is one, early and plainly.
How to think about it

This artefact is written for someone else to read without you — which changes how it must be written. It is the one most likely to be forwarded to a product owner or an SI lead. That means no internal shorthand, no unexplained jargon, and honest caveats: if you overstate what rule sets enforce, someone builds on it and the error is yours.

The columns are chosen to answer the question a product owner is actually asking. Not "how hard is this to build?" but "how fast can we change it later, and who has to be involved?" Change latency and headcount are what they feel every quarter; build effort is felt once.

Say the drift out loud. Customers and implementation partners both climb the ladder unprompted — engineers reach for code because code is what they do, and an SI billing for a build has no incentive to propose a rule set. Naming that dynamic in the guide makes it discussable rather than adversarial.

The lowest rung is not automatically the right one. Forcing a Level 1 answer onto a Level 2 problem produces a fragile pile of formulas nobody can maintain — and the FX-rate request is the clean example: a perfectly correct formula that never fires. The guide must therefore teach a test, not a preference.

Including the two boundaries is what makes it a routing guide rather than a capability list. The most valuable answer this document gives is sometimes "no, and here is where that belongs" — delivered early, once, kindly. A guide that only routes to rungs implies everything is buildable somewhere on the ladder.

Keep the caveats current. The client-side rule-set note is honest today and is on the colleague questions list. If it gets confirmed either way, this document changes — and an artefact that circulates outside your team needs a date and an owner, or it becomes a source of confidently wrong answers after you have moved on.

Sources
  • Configuration — getting started — Levels 0 and 1: the configurable surface and what an admin can change without a deploy.
  • Formulas — the Level 1 caveats — inputs, evaluation timing, and the silent failure modes.
  • Rule Sets — what a rule set can control, and the client-side evaluation wording behind the write-path caveat.
  • Workflows — introduction — Level 2: what belongs in a workflow, and why it is an app artefact rather than a setting.
  • Extensions — Level 3: extension types and the hosting model that drives the cost step.

I know this when I can…

  • Route any request to a rung and defend the choice on cost of ownership, not capability.
  • Write a formula and predict when it will and won't re-evaluate.
  • Read a rule set and say exactly which properties end up locked.
  • Explain environment promotion as two mechanisms that must stay in step.
  • Raise app ownership before creation, and explain why it can't be fixed later.
  • Tell a customer what an extension costs to run, not just to build.
