Module 8 · Week 8

Platform, security & operations

The conversation you'll have with a customer's IT and security teams — and the one where being honest about what you don't know matters more than sounding complete. This module is deliberately split between what the documentation establishes and what it doesn't, because that boundary is the operational picture.

On this page

  1. ⚙️Tenancy: the org is the boundary
  2. ⚙️The permission model, layer by layer
  3. ⚙️Credentials: where they live and how they leak
  4. ⚙️Where the data actually goes
  5. ⚙️Runtime and platform upgrades
  6. ⚙️What you can actually see
  7. ⚙️Capacity as a shared, invisible resource
  8. 🤝Answering what you don't know
  9. 📚Recommended reading
  10. ✅Practical exercise
  11. ✅Realistic customer scenario
  12. ✅Quiz
  13. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) describe the org as the tenancy boundary and how environments are actually maintained, (2) walk the layered permission model from org down to individual property, (3) trace where customer data physically travels and where credentials live, (4) say precisely what monitoring exists and over what horizon, and (5) answer a security questionnaire without bluffing — knowing which questions you must take away.

Concept 1 — Tenancy: the org is the boundary

Every authenticated request carries X-Api-Org. Every app is owned by an org. Configuration is exported and imported per org. Concurrency ceilings are per org. The org is the unit of isolation, ownership, configuration and capacity — hold that and most platform questions resolve.

Separate environments are real: the Node migration guide instructs you to "thoroughly test your app in a development org before installing it on production orgs," which confirms multiple orgs per customer as the intended pattern. But note what that implies — an environment is just another org. There is no environment abstraction layered above orgs; dev/staging/prod are a convention the customer maintains by discipline.

From Week 7: promotion is two mechanisms — contrail types getAll/loadAll for configuration, publish/install for apps. Combine that with "an environment is just an org" and you get the real risk: environments drift silently, and nothing in the platform tells you they have. Ask any established customer, "when did you last verify that staging matches production, and how?" The usual honest answer is never, and the usual consequence is a change that tested clean and broke on release.

Concept 2 — The permission model, layer by layer

Access control is layered, and each layer answers a different question:

LayerControlsWhere it's set
Org membershipWhether you exist in this tenant at allAdmin Console
App accessGrantsWhich orgs may install an app. "Currently, the only available permission is install"app.yml
Workflow accessGrantsWhich entity types an app's actions may touch, read or writeapp.yml
Type Policies"Access control statements defining READ, CREATE, UPDATE, DELETE permissions" per typeConfig; exported by Configuration Copy
Type Property Policies"Property-level access restrictions" — finer than the typeConfig; exported by Configuration Copy
API keysMachine access, app-scoped (app:…)contrail app getApiKey

A correction worth making early

A common first impression — including the one in this program's own Week 1 open questions — is that VibeIQ documents only app-level grants and API-key auth, with no real end-user permission model. That is not right. Type Policies and Type Property Policies exist and are first-class enough to be among the eleven categories Configuration Copy exports. What's missing is documentation, not capability. So the accurate statement to a customer is: "there is a policy-based model with type- and property-level permissions; I'll get you the specifics" — not "permissions are thin."

Still genuinely unknown

How policies map to end users and roles inside Boards, Plan and Showcase is not publicly documented. Whether there are named roles, group membership, or per-project principals — take that away and confirm. Do not improvise an answer to "can we restrict merchandisers to their own division?", because the shape of the answer determines their rollout design.

Concept 3 — Credentials: where they live and how they leak

Three credential surfaces, each with a different exposure:

  • API keys — format app:uEL7GCBZ7Xhd4w2z, app-scoped, long-lived. Docs are explicit: never commit them, use environment variables or secrets management, keep separate keys per environment, prefer app keys over user keys, and rotate regularly.
  • CLI credentials — authentication caches to ~/.vibeiq/configs/. That is a plaintext credential store on every developer and consultant laptop that has ever run the CLI.
  • Webhook trigger URLs — the webhook ID sits in the URL and functions as a bearer credential (Week 5/6).

The CLI cache is the one nobody raises and every security team cares about. When a consultant rolls off, or a laptop is lost, ~/.vibeiq/configs/ still holds working access. Offboarding must include credential revocation, not just removing someone from a channel. And it composes with Week 7's ownership problem: an integrator's engineer may hold both the app ownership and cached credentials to the customer's org. Raise revocation as part of any handover plan.

Concept 4 — Where the data actually goes

  1. Uploads — creating a File entity returns an uploadPost with an S3 URL and form parameters; the binary goes directly to S3 (us-east-1 in the documented examples).
  2. Publish payloads — baseline and delete data are JSON files on S3, reached by links that expire after 24 hours.
  3. Extensions — run in an iframe from the customer's own public HTTPS host, so document context crosses into infrastructure VibeIQ does not operate.
  4. Workflow actions — can POST anywhere the config endpoint points.

Two things a security team will ask that the docs don't answer

  • Data residency. Examples show us-east-1. Whether EU or other regions are available is undocumented. For a European brand this can be a procurement blocker, so find out early rather than at contract stage.
  • Compliance certifications. SOC 2 and similar attestations are not in the public docs. You will be asked for them in the first security review of every enterprise deal.

The 24-hour link expiry is usually presented as an operational constraint (Week 3: store the change ID, not the URL). Reframe it for a security audience and it becomes a control: payload links are short-lived rather than permanent. That is a genuinely good answer to "how long is our product data sitting on a URL?" — and it's the kind of translation that makes you useful in a room full of security people.

Concept 5 — Runtime and platform upgrades

Apps declare their runtime in the manifest: nodeRuntime: "nodejs22.x". Migration from Node 18 to 22 requires updating that field, auditing for deprecated APIs, testing locally on Node 22, and rebuilding native modules via npm install and npm rebuild.

Runtime deprecations are a recurring, schedulable obligation that customers reliably forget, because the app "still works" right up until it doesn't. Every customer with a custom app has a maintenance commitment, and nobody told the person who signs off on budgets. Track which of your accounts have custom apps and what runtime they're on — that list is a quiet early-warning system, and surfacing it before a forced migration is exactly the sort of thing that earns a TAM trust.

Concept 6 — What you can actually see

SurfaceShowsHorizon
Event Workflows Dashboard"Two charts that show the history of workflows triggered and workflows that have failed""going back up to 90 days"
Process ListIndividual processes, tasks, statuses, triggerEvent, taskOutputs; substring search over event and output90 days
Workflow ArtifactsFiles a developer attached to a process, downloadable or shareable by linkDeleted with the process at 90 days
LoaderProcessLoad status, config used, per-step artefacts—

Artifacts are created by setting a process as a file's owner — via the ownedByReference property, or createAndUploadFileFromBuffer. Shared links still require the recipient to be "logged into VibeIQ and have the appropriate organization permissions," so a link is not a public URL.

The failure chart is the one to institutionalise

A trend line of failed workflows over 90 days is the closest thing to a platform health metric available to a customer, and almost nobody looks at it. Get it into a recurring rhythm — a glance before every check-in, a screenshot in every QBR. A rising failure count is visible weeks before anyone files a ticket, which converts you from reactive to predictive at essentially zero cost.

The honest gap: nothing here is alerting

Everything above is something a human chooses to look at. Whether customers get proactive alerting on load, publish or workflow failures is not documented — and if the answer is no, then a failed nightly load is discovered by whoever notices missing data, which is usually a merchandiser in a review. Confirm this internally; it is one of the highest-value unknowns on your list, because the remedy (customers building their own monitoring on the process APIs) is something you'd want to recommend at implementation time, not after an incident.

Concept 7 — Capacity as a shared, invisible resource

Recall the numbers: parallelWorkerCount of 1–30 (default 5), 25 concurrent processes per workflow, and 60 per organization — the last adjustable via the Customer Success Manager.

Framed operationally: the org's 60 is a budget shared by every integration, workflow and bulk load the customer runs. There is no per-team allocation and no documented way for a customer to see their own utilisation. So capacity contention is invisible until it manifests as unexplained slowness in something unrelated.

Treat it as capacity planning, and do it before the customer needs it. Maintain a picture of what runs in their org, what it's triggered by, and when it's heaviest — then avoid scheduling bulk loads on top of the nightly integration window. "We moved the load to 03:30 and the sync stopped timing out" is a small win that looks like magic, and it is available to you purely from knowing the ceiling exists.

Concept 8 — Answering what you don't know

The standing list

Six things a security or IT team will ask that the public documentation does not settle. Take them away rather than improvising:

  1. End-user roles and permissions in Boards / Plan / Showcase, and how Type Policies map to them.
  2. Numeric API rate limits and any SLA commitments.
  3. Data residency options beyond us-east-1.
  4. Compliance certifications — SOC 2 and equivalents.
  5. Proactive alerting available to customers on load / publish / workflow failure.
  6. Whether rule sets are enforced on API and Loader writes (Week 7) and what inbound webhook verification is actually enforced (Weeks 5–6).

"I don't know, I'll find out by Thursday" is a strong answer when it's specific and it's kept. Bluffing a compliance answer is unrecoverable — security teams verify, and being wrong once means everything else you say gets checked too. The discipline that makes this work is closing the loop: write the question down, name a date, come back with a documented answer. Do that three times and you are the person they route questions to.

Recommended reading

  • Authentication & API Access — key hygiene and error codes.
  • Configuration Copy — read the category list as a map of what is configurable and governable.
  • Event Workflows Dashboard & Workflow Artifacts — the monitoring surface.
  • Node 18 → 22 migration — the shape of a runtime obligation.

Practical exercise

Write the platform section of an implementation runbook for a new customer: environment/org topology and how promotion happens; who holds which credentials and how they're rotated and revoked at offboarding; where data travels; what monitoring exists and who looks at it, how often; the runtime obligations of any custom app; and the org's concurrency budget with what runs when. Keep it to two pages — it should be handed to a customer's IT lead, not read only by you.

The answer

Platform runbook — Summit Athletic. Owner: [TAM name]. Reviewed quarterly. Written for Summit's IT lead.

1 · Environments and promotion

An "environment" in VibeIQ is another org — there is no separate environment abstraction. Every authenticated request carries X-Api-Org, and the org is the unit of isolation, ownership, configuration and capacity.

OrgPurposeWho has access
summit-devIntegration development and testingSI engineers, TAM
summit-prodProductionNamed admins only

Promotion is two mechanisms, and both must run:

  • Configuration — contrail types getAll in the source org, contrail types loadAll in the target. Eleven categories move, including types, option sets, rule sets, and type/property policies.
  • Apps (and therefore workflows and extensions) — publish the app version, then install it in the target org.

Failure mode to watch: promoting one and not the other. Environments then drift silently and a workflow behaves differently in production for reasons nobody can see. Record the config export date and app version live in each org, in this document.

2 · Credentials

CredentialHeld byStored inRotationRevoke on offboarding
API key — devSI integration teamSecrets managerQuarterlyReissue
API key — prodSummit integration ownerSecrets managerQuarterlyReissue
Webhook IDs (inbound)Integration ownerSecrets managerOn suspicionRecreate webhook
CLI credentialsEvery laptop that ever ran the CLI~/.vibeiq/configs/n/aMust be wiped — see below

Rules: one key per environment, never shared; app-scoped keys (app: prefix) rather than user keys; never committed to version control; environment variables or a secrets manager only.

The offboarding gap nobody raises: CLI authentication caches to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on the local machine. Anyone who has ever run the CLI has a credential on their laptop that survives them leaving. Maintain a list of who has run it, and include cache removal in the offboarding checklist. Inbound webhook IDs are also effectively bearer credentials — the ID in the trigger URL is what authorises the call — so treat them as secrets, not as configuration.

3 · Where data travels

  • Uploads: creating a File entity returns an S3 uploadPost; the binary goes directly to S3 (us-east-1 in the documented examples).
  • Publish payloads: baseline and delete data are JSON files on S3, reached by links that expire after 24 hours.
  • Extensions: run in an iframe served from a public HTTPS host. Document context crosses into that host — so if a third party operates it, customer data reaches a third party. List every extension and its host here, by name.
  • Outbound webhooks: to endpoints Summit specifies. List them.

Open: residency options beyond us-east-1 are not established in public documentation. If EU residency is a requirement, it is a question for VibeIQ before contract, not an assumption.

4 · Monitoring — what exists, who looks, how often

SurfaceShowsHorizonOwnerCadence
Event Workflows DashboardWorkflows triggered and failed, as trend charts90 daysTAMDaily, 20 min
Process ListIndividual processes, tasks, statuses, task outputs90 daysTAM / SIOn incident
LoaderProcess recordsLoad status, failureArea, step artefactsNot documentedIntegration ownerDaily after nightly load
Consumer-side alertingSummit's own integration healthSummit'sSummitContinuous

Two things to state plainly. Monitoring is pull: someone must look. There is no established proactive customer alerting on load, publish or workflow failure, so absence of a report is not evidence of health — which is why the daily check has a named owner. And the 90-day horizon is hard: evidence is permanently deleted, so a problem reported four months late cannot be root-caused. If any metric needs a longer trend, snapshot it monthly starting in week one.

5 · Runtime obligations

  • Custom app runtime is declared in the manifest (e.g. nodeRuntime: "nodejs22.x").
  • Runtime deprecations are recurring and schedulable. Migration means updating the field, auditing deprecated APIs, testing on the new runtime, and rebuilding native modules.
  • Named owner and a calendar reminder. Customers reliably forget this because the app keeps working right up until it does not.
  • Record here: current runtime, app version, who can publish a new version.

6 · Concurrency budget

Ceilings: parallelWorkerCount 1–30 (default 5) · 25 concurrent processes per workflow · 60 per organisation, adjustable via the Customer Success Manager.

The org's 60 is a single budget shared by every workflow, integration and bulk load. There is no per-team allocation, and no documented way for a customer to see their own utilisation. So it must be managed on paper:

TimeWhat runsIntended workers
02:00Nightly PLM load—
02:00–03:00Entity workflows fired by that loadOnce per row
On publishOutbound integration workflow5
Ad hocCopy-down workflow8

Rule: before any bulk load, check what else runs in that window. A large load and the nightly integration competing for the same 60 is how both get slow and one starts timing out. Request a ceiling increase before a season, not during one.

How to think about it

Write it for the IT lead, which means the honest gaps have to be in it. A runbook that quietly omits residency, proactive alerting and rate limits will be read as complete — and the first incident teaches them it was not. Marking something "open, and here is who is asking" is a stronger document than one that reads seamlessly, because it tells the reader which parts they can rely on.

Everything here resolves to the org. Isolation, ownership, configuration, capacity, and the fact that environments are just more orgs. Leading with that makes the rest of the document derivable rather than a list of facts to memorise — and it explains drift, promotion and the shared budget in one idea.

Two mechanisms for promotion is the detail that causes silent production differences. Someone runs the config export, sees it succeed, and believes the environment is promoted. The app — and therefore every workflow — did not move. Recording the config date and app version per org in the runbook is what makes drift visible without an investigation.

Every monitoring row needs an owner and a cadence, or it is decorative. Because monitoring is pull-based with no proactive alerting, an unwatched dashboard is identical to no dashboard. "The team watches it" means nobody. This is also where the 90-day horizon belongs — it converts "we should look at this" into "if we do not look within 90 days the evidence is gone."

The CLI credential cache is the item that earns the document its credibility. It is a genuine offboarding gap that customers do not raise because they do not know it exists, and no vendor volunteers it. Putting it in a runbook — with a wipe step — is the kind of thing that makes an IT lead treat you as their technical reference rather than as an account contact.

Two pages is a constraint, not a suggestion. A runbook nobody finishes is not a control. Everything above is tables and short rules for that reason; narrative belongs in the modules, not here.

Sources
  • Authentication & API Access — section 2 — the app: key format, the two required headers, key hygiene and per-environment scoping.
  • Configuration Copy — section 1 — the export/import commands and the eleven categories that move.
  • Event Workflows Dashboard & Workflow Artifacts — section 4 — the monitoring surfaces and their 90-day horizon.
  • Node 18 → 22 migration — section 5 — the shape of a runtime obligation and what a migration involves.
  • Creating Workflows — section 6 — worker counts and the per-workflow and per-org concurrency ceilings.

Realistic customer scenario

Summit Athletic's InfoSec lead sends a pre-contract questionnaire and copies the VP of Product, who wants it back in 48 hours:

  1. "Where is our product data stored geographically, and can it be kept in the EU?"
  2. "Provide your SOC 2 Type II report."
  3. "Describe the role-based access model. Can we restrict a merchandiser to their own division?"
  4. "How are API credentials issued, rotated and revoked?"
  5. "What is your API rate limit and uptime SLA?"
  6. "Are webhook payloads signed?"
  7. "What happens to our data on a third-party extension?"

For each: answer it, or say exactly what you'd take away and who you'd ask. Then — sort them: which can you answer confidently today, which are partially answerable (say what you'd commit to and what you'd qualify), and which must you not answer at all? Finally, write the covering note to the VP in three sentences, given you cannot fully answer inside 48 hours and they are trying to close a deal.

The answer

Question by question

1 · "Where is our product data stored geographically, and can it be kept in the EU?"
Partially answerable. I can describe the data paths accurately: file uploads go directly to S3 (us-east-1 in the documented examples), publish payloads are JSON on S3 behind 24-hour links, and extension content reaches whatever host runs the extension. What I cannot say is whether EU residency is available. Take away: residency options and any EU region — to VibeIQ product/infrastructure via the CSM. This one is likely a deal gate, so it goes first.

2 · "Provide your SOC 2 Type II report."
Must not answer. I do not confirm, deny or characterise compliance certifications. Not "I believe we have one." Take away: to whoever owns compliance/trust — via the CSM or account exec — and route the actual report through the proper channel, under NDA if required.

3 · "Describe the role-based access model. Can we restrict a merchandiser to their own division?"
Partially answerable, and the trap question. The wrong answer is "VibeIQ has no end-user permission model" — that is false. Access control is layered: org membership, app accessGrants, workflow accessGrants, Type Policies (READ/CREATE/UPDATE/DELETE) and Type Property Policies, the last two both being exported categories in Configuration Copy. So the capability exists. What is not documented is how those policies map to named roles in Boards/Plan/Showcase, which is exactly what the division question asks. Take away: the end-user role model and whether policies can scope by a data attribute such as division.

4 · "How are API credentials issued, rotated and revoked?"
Answer confidently. Keys are app-scoped (app: prefix), obtained via contrail app getApiKey, and sent with X-Api-Key plus X-Api-Org on every request. Documented practice: never commit them, use environment variables or secrets management, separate keys per environment, prefer app keys over user keys, rotate regularly. I would proactively add the CLI credential cache at ~/.vibeiq/configs/ and its offboarding implication — they have not asked, and it is the honest answer to "revoked."

5 · "What is your API rate limit and uptime SLA?"
Must not answer. No numeric rate limits or SLA commitments are established publicly. I will not quote a number — in particular not the "15 requests/hour" figure that circulates online, which belongs to a different company. What I can say is that a consumer should implement exponential backoff regardless and not tune to a specific limit. Take away: real limits and contractual SLA — CSM and the contract, since the SLA is a commercial commitment, not a technical fact.

6 · "Are webhook payloads signed?"
Partially answerable — and the two directions must be kept apart. Outbound (VibeIQ → Summit) supports HMAC signing, which is what this question most likely means. Inbound (Summit → VibeIQ) documents an optional X-Verify-Signature header compared as a shared secret rather than an HMAC over the body, and the webhook ID in the trigger URL functions as a bearer credential. Conflating these gives a security team a wrong answer. Take away: what inbound verification is actually enforced server-side, and whether request signing is available.

7 · "What happens to our data on a third-party extension?"
Answer confidently, and it is worth their attention. Extensions render in an iframe from a public HTTPS host and receive document context — so data reaches whoever operates that host. If a systems integrator hosts it, that is a third-party processor and belongs in their vendor assessment. Concretely: list every extension in scope, name its host, and decide who should own that hosting. This connects directly to the Week 7 design note.

The sort

ConfidentPartial — commit and qualifyDo not answer
4 · Credential issuance and hygiene
7 · Extension data exposure		1 · Data paths yes, residency no
3 · Layers yes, role mapping no
6 · Outbound yes, inbound qualified		2 · SOC 2
5 · Rate limits and SLA

The covering note to the VP — three sentences

"I can answer four of these seven properly today and I have sent those to [InfoSec lead] already — credentials, extension data handling, the data paths, and the access-control model as far as it is documented. Three need a definitive answer rather than my best reading: EU data residency, the SOC 2 report, and contractual rate limits and SLA — I have them with the right owners internally and I will not guess at a compliance answer your team is going to verify. You will have those three by [specific date]; if residency in the EU is a hard requirement rather than a preference, tell me now and I will push that one ahead of the others."
How to think about it

Sorting is the actual skill, not answering. Seven questions with a 48-hour clock and a VP watching is a triage problem. Answering four fully and naming three with owners and a date is a complete response; a document that hedges all seven reads as evasive and helps nobody.

Bluffing a compliance answer is unrecoverable. Security teams verify — that is the job. One wrong answer means every other statement you have made gets checked, and you have converted a technical relationship into an audit. This is why SOC 2 and SLA are "do not answer" rather than "answer carefully": there is no careful version of a certification claim you are not authorised to make.

Question 3 is where a new TAM most often loses credibility, in both directions. The tempting answers are "yes, full RBAC" (unsupported) and "no real permission model" (false, and it can lose a deal). The accurate answer is that a layered model exists including type and property policies, and that the mapping to named end-user roles is undocumented. Precision here is worth more than confidence.

Volunteer the CLI cache under question 4. They asked about revocation and the honest answer includes a gap they do not know to ask about. Raising it costs you nothing — it is an operational practice, not a product flaw — and it is the single fastest way to be read as candid rather than as a vendor managing a questionnaire.

Give the VP a decision, not just a status. The last sentence asks whether EU residency is a hard requirement, because that single answer changes what you chase first and may change whether the deal is viable at all. A covering note that only reports progress leaves the commercial owner with nothing to do; one that surfaces the gating question makes them a participant.

Be specific about dates, and then keep them. "I'll find out" is worth nothing; "you will have those three by Thursday" is a commitment that builds the trust the whole role runs on. Write the question down, name a date, come back with a documented answer — do that three times and you become the person they route questions to.

Sources
  • Authentication & API Access — question 4 — key format, headers, and documented key hygiene.
  • Configuration Copy — question 3 — Type Policies and Type Property Policies appear among the exported categories, which is the evidence that an end-user permission model exists.
  • Webhooks — question 6 — the inbound trigger-webhook surface and its optional verification header.
  • Extensions — question 7 — the hosted-iframe model and the context passed into it.
  • Questions for VibeIQ colleagues — four of these seven map directly onto the standing colleague-questions list — this scenario is why that page is a work item rather than a reading list.

Quiz

  1. What does the org boundary govern? Name four distinct things it scopes.

    The org scopes isolation (every request carries X-Api-Org), ownership (every app is owned by an org, permanently), configuration (Configuration Copy exports and imports per org), and capacity (the 60-concurrent-process ceiling is per org). Also defensible: identity/membership, and API key scope. Holding "the org is the unit of isolation, ownership, configuration and capacity" resolves most platform questions.

  2. What is an "environment" in VibeIQ terms, and why do environments drift silently?

    An environment is just another org — there is no environment abstraction layered above orgs. The Node migration guide confirms the intended pattern ("test your app in a development org before installing it on production orgs"), but dev/staging/prod is a convention the customer maintains by discipline. They drift silently because configuration and apps promote by two different mechanisms (types loadAll vs publish/install), promotion is a whole-org bulk operation rather than a cherry-pick, and nothing in the platform reconciles them or reports divergence. So "it tested fine in staging" is a structural risk, not bad luck.

  3. List the permission layers from org membership down to a single property.

    Org membership (do you exist in this tenant) → app accessGrants (which orgs may install an app; "the only available permission is install") → workflow accessGrants (which entity types an app's actions may read or write) → Type Policies (READ/CREATE/UPDATE/DELETE per type) → Type Property Policies (property-level restrictions). API keys sit alongside as the machine-access surface, app-scoped.

  4. Why is "VibeIQ has no real end-user permission model" a wrong statement, and what is the accurate version?

    Because Type Policies and Type Property Policies exist — "access control statements defining READ, CREATE, UPDATE, DELETE permissions" and "property-level access restrictions" — and they're first-class enough to be among the eleven categories Configuration Copy exports. What's missing is documentation, not capability. The accurate version: "there is a policy-based model with type- and property-level permissions; I'll get you the specifics on how it maps to roles." What genuinely remains unknown is how those policies map to named roles or groups inside Boards, Plan and Showcase.

  5. Name the three credential surfaces. Which one is most often missed at offboarding, and why does it matter?

    API keys (long-lived, app-scoped), CLI cached credentials at ~/.vibeiq/configs/, and webhook trigger URLs (the ID in the URL acts as a bearer credential). Most often missed: the CLI cache. It sits on every developer and consultant laptop that ever ran the CLI, so when someone rolls off an engagement or loses a laptop, working access to the customer's org persists unless it's explicitly revoked. Offboarding has to include credential revocation, not just removing someone from a channel — and it compounds with an integrator who may also hold app ownership.

  6. Trace where a customer's data physically travels. Which two residency/compliance questions can you not answer from the docs?

    Uploads: creating a File entity returns an uploadPost and the binary goes directly to S3 (us-east-1 in the documented examples). Publish payloads: baseline and delete JSON files on S3, reached by links expiring in 24 hours. Extensions: run in an iframe from the customer's own public HTTPS host. Workflow actions: can POST wherever config points. The two you cannot answer from the docs: data residency (whether EU or other regions are available — a potential procurement blocker for a European brand) and compliance certifications (SOC 2 and equivalents).

  7. Reframe the 24-hour link expiry as a security control rather than a constraint.

    Instead of "the link dies in a day, so store the change ID," say: payload links are short-lived by design rather than permanent. Product data is not sitting on a durable, guessable URL indefinitely — access windows are bounded and re-issuing requires an authenticated fetch of the change object. That's a genuinely good answer to "how long is our data exposed on a URL?", and it's the kind of translation that makes you useful in a room full of security people rather than just accurate.

  8. What does the Event Workflows Dashboard show, over what horizon, and why should it be in a QBR?

    It shows "two charts… the history of workflows triggered and workflows that have failed," going back up to 90 days. It belongs in a QBR because a rising failure trend is operational health made visible — it appears weeks before anyone files a ticket, so bringing it converts you from reactive to predictive at essentially zero cost. It also gives you the thing that most changes a QBR's dynamic: one risk you found before the customer did.

  9. What is the crucial difference between everything in the monitoring table and actual alerting?

    None of it is alerting. The dashboard, the Process List, Workflow Artifacts and LoaderProcess records are all things a human must choose to look at. Whether customers get proactive notification of load, publish or workflow failure is not documented — and if the answer is no, a failed nightly load is discovered by whoever notices missing data, typically a merchandiser in a review. Confirm it internally; if absent, recommend the customer build monitoring on the process APIs at implementation time rather than after an incident.

  10. Explain the 60-process org ceiling as a capacity-planning problem. What would you do with that knowledge before a customer notices anything?

    The 60 concurrent processes are a shared budget across every workflow, integration and bulk load in the org, with no documented way for the customer to see their own utilisation — so contention is invisible until it surfaces as unexplained slowness somewhere unrelated. Treat it as capacity planning: maintain a picture of what runs in the org, what triggers it and when it's heaviest, then avoid stacking bulk loads on top of the nightly integration window. "We moved the load to 03:30 and the sync stopped timing out" is a small win that looks like magic and is available purely from knowing the ceiling exists.

Hands-on challenge

Build your security questionnaire response kit: the standard enterprise security questions, split into answer confidently, answer with qualification, and must escalate — with your actual wording for each in the first two groups, and the named internal owner for the third. Keep the escalate list short and current; every item you move out of it is a deal cycle shortened. This joins the account-inheritance kit from Weeks 2–7.

The answer

Security questionnaire response kit. Three groups. Group A and B carry wording you can send; group C carries an owner and a date, never an improvised answer.

A · Answer confidently

"How is API access authenticated?"

"Every REST request carries two headers: X-Api-Key and X-Api-Org. Keys are application-scoped — they carry an app: prefix and are issued per app rather than tied to a user account — and are obtained through the Contrail CLI. The org header scopes the request to a single tenant; a key is only valid for the org it belongs to."

"How are credentials stored, rotated and revoked?"

"Documented practice is: never commit keys to source control, hold them in environment variables or a secrets manager, use a separate key per environment, prefer app-scoped keys over user keys, and rotate on a schedule. Revocation is by reissuing the key. One item we raise proactively: the Contrail CLI caches authentication to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on the local machine, so any workstation that has ever run the CLI holds a credential. We recommend tracking who has run it and including cache removal in your offboarding checklist."

"What is the tenancy model?"

"The organisation is the isolation boundary. Every authenticated request is scoped to one org; apps are owned by an org; configuration is exported and imported per org; and concurrency limits are per org. Separate environments are implemented as separate orgs."

"Where does file and payload data travel?"

"File uploads go directly to Amazon S3 — the client requests an upload target and posts the binary to S3. Assortment publish payloads are JSON files on S3, accessed through links that expire after 24 hours. Documented examples use us-east-1."

"What happens to our data in a third-party extension?"

"Extensions render in an iframe served from a public HTTPS host and receive document context from the surrounding app. That host is therefore a data processor and belongs in your vendor assessment. We can list every extension in scope with the party operating it; where an implementation partner is hosting one, we recommend deciding deliberately whether that should move to infrastructure you control."

"What audit trail exists for automation?"

"Workflow executions are recorded as processes with per-task status and outputs, viewable in the Admin Console, with failure trend charts. Retention is 90 days, after which records are permanently deleted — so if you need a longer audit horizon, it has to be exported on a schedule."

B · Answer with qualification — say the documented part, name the boundary

"Describe the role-based access model."

"Access control is layered: org membership determines tenancy; app access grants control which orgs may install an app; workflow access grants control which entity types an app's actions may read or write; and Type Policies and Type Property Policies control create, read, update and delete at entity-type and individual-property level. Those policy categories are first-class configuration — they are among the categories exported by Configuration Copy. What I want to confirm rather than assert is how those policies map onto named end-user roles in Boards, Plan and Showcase, and whether they can be scoped by a data attribute such as division. I will come back with a definitive answer rather than a reading."

"Are webhook payloads signed?"

"These are two different questions and we keep them apart. Outbound — VibeIQ notifying your endpoint — supports HMAC signing, which is what you would verify on receipt. Inbound — your system triggering a workflow in VibeIQ — documents an optional verification header compared as a shared secret, and the webhook identifier in the trigger URL functions as a bearer credential and should be handled as a secret. What is enforced server-side on the inbound path is the part I am confirming."

"Where is data stored geographically?"

"I can describe the paths precisely — S3 for uploads and publish payloads, us-east-1 in documented examples, plus whatever host runs an extension. What I will not do is state residency options or an EU region from inference; that is a question for VibeIQ directly and I have it with them."

"What monitoring and alerting do we get?"

"You get a workflow dashboard, a process list with task-level outputs, and loader process records with a failure area field — all pull-based, 90-day retention on the workflow surfaces. I am confirming what proactive alerting is available to customers on load, publish and workflow failure; today we cover it with a named owner checking daily rather than assuming a notification will arrive."

C · Must escalate — never improvise

Uptime SLA and contractual commitmentsCSM / account execCommercial commitment, not a technical fact.
Numeric API rate limitsCSM / productNone published. Never quote the "15 requests/hour" figure — it belongs to a different company.

Pen-test results, vulnerability disclosureSecurity, via CSMNot public.
End-user role model in the appsProductCapability exists; the mapping is undocumented. Strong candidate to move to group B once answered.
Rule-set enforcement on API / Loader writesEngineeringChanges how validation must be designed. Candidate to move.
Inbound webhook verification actually enforcedEngineeringCandidate to move.
Customer-facing proactive alertingProductCandidate to move.

How to run the kit: keep group C short and dated. Every item that moves from C to B is a deal cycle shortened, so review it monthly and chase the four marked as candidates — those are answerable internally and are currently costing you time on every questionnaire. Record the date each answer was confirmed and by whom; a security answer with no provenance decays into folklore within a year.

How to think about it

The kit exists to make the answer automatic under pressure. Security questionnaires arrive with deadlines and a VP watching, which is exactly the condition under which people improvise. Pre-writing the wording means the decision about what you may claim was made calmly, in advance, rather than in the moment.

Group C is a work backlog, not a permanent list. That reframing is the point of the artefact. Four of the ten items are answerable by asking a colleague — they sit in C because nobody has asked, and each one costs you days on every deal. Treating the list as static is the failure; a shrinking C is the measure of the kit working.

Qualification is a technique, not a hedge. The pattern in group B is consistent: state the documented part precisely, name the exact boundary, commit to closing it. That reads as expertise. "I think so" reads as guessing, and "yes" that turns out to be wrong reads as either incompetence or misrepresentation — a security team cannot tell the difference and will assume the worse one.

Volunteer the uncomfortable facts. The CLI credential cache, the 90-day retention limit, extension hosting as third-party processing. None of these is asked in the standard questionnaire and all three are material. Raising them costs nothing, is the correct thing to do, and is what converts you from a vendor contact into the person their security team routes questions to.

The rate-limit row is a discipline test. There is a number available online and it is wrong — it belongs to another company entirely. The temptation to fill an awkward silence with a plausible figure is real, and quoting it once means every other number you give gets checked. Write the prohibition into the artefact so it does not depend on remembering.

Date and attribute every answer. Platform facts change — a residency option ships, a certification lands, a limit gets published. A kit without provenance quietly becomes a source of confidently wrong answers after you have moved to another account, which is worse than having no kit at all.

Sources
  • Authentication & API Access — group A — key format, the two headers, and documented key hygiene.
  • Configuration Copy — group B — Type Policies and Type Property Policies among the exported categories, the evidence behind the permission-model answer.
  • Webhooks — group B — the inbound trigger-webhook surface and its optional verification header.
  • Extensions — group A — the hosted-iframe model behind the third-party processing answer.
  • Event Workflows Dashboard & Workflow Artifacts — group A — the audit surfaces and the 90-day retention.

I know this when I can…

  • Explain org-as-boundary and why environment drift is the customer's problem to manage.
  • Walk the permission layers, and correct the "no RBAC" misconception accurately.
  • Name every place a credential rests, including the one on laptops.
  • Trace the data path and state the residency and compliance unknowns without flinching.
  • Describe the monitoring surface and its 90-day horizon — and its lack of alerting.
  • Handle a security questionnaire without bluffing a single answer.
