Module 9 · Week 9

Troubleshooting & escalation methodology

Everything from Weeks 2–8, turned into one repeatable method. This module introduces almost no new facts — its value is entirely in sequence and discipline. The goal is that an unfamiliar problem stops being a puzzle and becomes a route you walk.

On this page

  1. ⚙️Localise before theorising
  2. 🤝The universal question set
  3. ⚙️The three ladders
  4. ⚙️Symptom shape tells you the class of cause
  5. ⚙️Read evidence, don't reason from descriptions
  6. 🤝Escalating well
  7. 🤝Holding the customer while it's open
  8. ✅Practical exercise
  9. ✅Realistic customer scenario — a live incident
  10. ✅Quiz
  11. ✅Hands-on challenge

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) localise any failure to one segment of the pipeline before proposing a cause, (2) run the right ladder for that segment from memory, (3) read the platform's evidence rather than reasoning from a customer's description, (4) recognise a class of cause from the shape of a symptom, (5) write an escalation that gets acted on, and (6) hold a customer while it's open.

Concept 1 — Localise before theorising

The single most common failure in technical support is proposing a cause before establishing where the problem is. A theory arrives early, everyone starts testing it, and two days go to a hypothesis that was never constrained by evidence.

The rule

Your first job is not "why is this happening?" It is "which segment of the pipeline is this in?" Only once the segment is fixed do you ask why. Localisation is cheap — the platform records almost everything — and it eliminates whole categories of cause in a single check.

Bisect rather than walking left to right. Start at CP3 — look at the entity itself. If the value is correct there, the problem is downstream (CP4–CP6). If it's wrong there, the problem is upstream (CP1–CP2). One look halves the search space, and CP3 is usually the fastest thing to check because it's just opening the record. Walking the pipeline in order is the instinct; bisecting is the discipline.

Concept 2 — The universal question set

Six questions that apply to almost any data-correctness report. Ask them before touching anything:

QuestionWhy it eliminates whole causesWk
Which entity layer? Item, ProjectItem or AssortmentItemA value written to Item won't appear where an app shows an AssortmentItem override2
Which property level? Family, Option, All, OverrideExplains copy-down clobber and "the system won't let us"2
Which federatedId? And what generates itExplains duplicates and orphans2 · 4
Create or update?Changes conditional behaviour, formula timing and event type4 · 6
Which process? Get the ID — LoaderProcess or workflow processReplaces description with evidence4 · 6
When did it last work?Bounds the change set, and tests the 90-day evidence horizon8

Ask these in the customer's language, not the platform's. "Is this a style-level or colour-level field?" gets a better answer from a merchandiser than "is this Family or Option?" — and it's the same question. Translating your diagnostic model into their vocabulary is most of what makes a TAM feel easy to work with.

Concept 3 — The three ladders

Once localised, run the ladder for that segment. These are consolidated from Weeks 4, 6 and 3/5 — learn them as three, not as scattered notes.

Ladder A — a load went wrong CP2

  1. Get the LoaderProcess ID. Read failureArea first — PARSING_CSV, VALIDATING_DATA or TRANSFORMING_DATA localises it in one field. Then the configuration recorded on it.
  2. Compare that config against the last known-good run — especially if CLI and API runs are mixed.
  3. Read the per-step artefacts loader-process-step-<Step>.json. Diff consecutive steps to find where a value changed.
  4. Check column matching: unmatched columns are ignored silently.
  5. Check option-set alignment: display labels convert to internal values in steps 4/6.
  6. Check federatedId presence and stability — including anything computed in conditionalColumns.
  7. Check the four flags: shouldSkipAssortmentPublish, partialAssortmentUpdate, assortmentSplit, workspaceIdentifier. A "successful" load can be invisible downstream or can have replaced an assortment.
  8. If values are right in the artefacts but wrong in the app, the load is exonerated — move to CP3/CP4.

Ladder B — automation didn't happen CP4

  1. Is there a process at all? Filter the Process List on the entity. None → step 2. Some → step 5.
  2. isActive true, installed in this org, from the expected app version?
  3. Right triggerKey? (assortment has only publish.)
  4. Did the change emit that event? A load emits create, not update.
  5. Which path ran? Read triggerEvent, evaluate conditionals top-down. First match wins; oldData absent on creates.
  6. Did actions succeed? Read taskOutputs. COMPLETE-but-no-op → access grant, or config pointing somewhere harmless.
  7. Intermittent and load-correlated? → concurrency and message grouping, not logic.

Ladder C — it didn't reach the downstream system CP5 · CP6

  1. Did a publish happen? Is there an AssortmentPublishChange? If the change came from a load, check shouldSkipAssortmentPublish — when true no publish event is emitted at all.
  2. Did the Plan rows actually reach the Assortment? (The internal commit is separate from the sync.)
  3. Did the delivery workflow fire? — this is Ladder B, because the integration is a workflow.
  4. Did the consumer receive or poll it? Check their logs, and their last-processed change ID.
  5. Did it process everything — including the delete file?
  6. Any gap in processed change IDs? Missed deletes never replay; re-baseline.

Concept 4 — Symptom shape tells you the class of cause

Before content, look at shape. How a problem is distributed in time and across records narrows the cause faster than any detail about the records themselves.

ShapeClass of causeGo straight to
Intermittent, load-correlated, different records each timeConcurrency raceparallelWorkerCount and message grouping
Consistent, silent, affects every record of a kindConfigurationProperty level, formula slug, rule set, mapping
Affects part of a batch, not the restCreate vs. update divergenceConditionals using oldData; which rows were new
Started on a date, nothing "changed"A deploy, a config promotion, or an expiryApp version installed, types loadAll, runtime deprecation
Sporadic missing records, unreproducibleIntegration seamPolling window construction; missed events
Correct at write, wrong laterRecalculation or copy-downFormula re-evaluation timing; a Family reload
Only some users see itPermission or client-side rulesType/Type Property Policies; rule set criteria
Everything slow, nothing brokenShared capacityThe 60-process org ceiling; what else ran

Get the shape from the customer explicitly, because they will not volunteer it. Three questions do it: "Does it affect every record or some?" "Did it start at a particular time?" "If you retry, does the same record fail?" That last one is the highest-yield question in troubleshooting — same record failing means logic or config; a different record means timing or concurrency.

Concept 5 — Read evidence, don't reason from descriptions

The platform records more than most people use. Anchor every claim to one of these:

EvidenceAnswersLifetime
LoaderProcess + per-step artefactsWhat the load received, and what each step did to it—
Process List: status, triggerEvent, taskOutputs, startTime/endTimeWhether automation ran, on what, and what it produced90 days
Event Workflows DashboardWhether failures are trending90 days
Workflow ArtifactsFiles a developer attached to a run90 days
AssortmentPublishChange + baselineExactly what was published, and the state at that momentLinks 24h; refetch to refresh
Config export (types getAll)What the configuration actually is, versus what people believe—

The evidence horizon is real

Workflow logs and artifacts are "permanently deleted" after 90 days. If a customer reports something that began months ago, establish what evidence still exists before you commit to finding root cause — and if an investigation will be long, tell them to preserve exports now. Promising a root cause you can no longer reach is a self-inflicted wound.

Concept 6 — Escalating well

Escalation is not "I couldn't solve it." It is a handover, and its quality determines whether the next person starts where you stopped or starts again.

What a good escalation contains

  1. The segment. "Localised to CP4 — the workflow fires but the action no-ops." Not "publishing is broken."
  2. Identifiers. Process IDs, LoaderProcess IDs, federatedIds, change IDs, org slug, app version. Anything the next person would otherwise have to ask for.
  3. What you ruled out, and how. This is the part that saves the most time and the part most often omitted.
  4. The shape. Intermittent or consistent, all records or some, since when.
  5. Business impact and clock. Who is blocked, and what deadline it threatens.
  6. Your hypothesis, marked as one. Offer it, don't assert it — a confident wrong theory in an escalation costs more than none.

Point 3 is what separates a TAM from a ticket relay. "I checked the Process List: it fired 47 times, all COMPLETE, taskOutputs empty. Access grants show item: read where the action attempts a write — I believe that's it, but I can't confirm the grant is the blocker from outside" is a handover an engineer can act on in minutes. Forwarding the customer's email is not.

Routing

Goes toWhen
You (TAM)Configuration, data model, integration design, adoption, "how should this work" — the majority
SupportReproducible platform defects with evidence attached
EngineeringConfirmed defects, data corruption, anything needing platform-side inspection
ProductCapability gaps and roadmap questions — including "no trigger exists for this"
Professional ServicesBuild work: apps, extensions, integration implementation
Customer Success ManagerConcurrency ceiling increases; marketplace approval; commercial framing

Confirm the real routing internally

The table above is a sensible default built from what the platform documents, not from VibeIQ's actual internal process — which is not public. In your first weeks, establish: the real escalation path and tooling, the practical TAM/Support/PS/Engineering/Product boundary, who owns integration builds (PS or the customer), and what severity definitions and response expectations exist. Until you know, say "let me route this correctly" rather than guessing and creating a false expectation about timing.

Concept 7 — Holding the customer while it's open

The technical work is half the job. The other half is that a customer with an open incident is anxious, usually accountable to someone else, and judging you on communication rather than diagnosis.

  • Separate what you know from what you suspect, explicitly and every time. "Confirmed: the publish happened. Suspected: their consumer skipped the delete file. Unknown: why it skipped."
  • Commit to a next update time, not a resolution time. "I'll come back by 4pm with what I've found" is keepable. "It'll be fixed today" usually isn't.
  • Give them something to do where you can — preserve an export, pause a load, note affected style numbers. Agency reduces anxiety.
  • Say the impact back to them in their terms: "so this blocks Thursday's line review for the outlet assortment" tells them you understand what actually matters.
  • Close the loop with the mechanism, not just "fixed." The explanation is what stops a repeat and is what they'll repeat to their leadership.

Never answer "who's wrong?" — the question Summit Athletic's PLM lead asked in Week 3. Answer "here's what happened and here's what we change." Adjudicating blame between a customer's teams costs you the neutrality that makes you useful to both, and it is never the question they actually need answered.

Practical exercise

Write your one-page triage card, from memory first and then checked: the six checkpoints, the universal question set, the three ladders in outline, and the symptom-shape table. Then test it — take three scenarios from earlier modules (Week 2's copy-down clobber, Week 4's three-symptom load, Week 6's concurrency race) and confirm your card would have routed each correctly without you already knowing the answer. Fix whatever it misses.

The answer

The triage card — one page.

0 · Before anything: bisect at CP3. Open the record. Is the value correct on the entity itself? Correct → the problem is downstream (CP4–CP6). Wrong → upstream (CP1–CP2). One look halves the search space.

1 · The six checkpoints

SegmentWhat you look at
CP1Source / CSVIs the value right in the file at all?
CP2LoaderfailureArea, config used, per-step artefacts
CP3EntitiesWhich layer, which property level, expected federatedId?
CP4Workflows / formulasProcess List: did it fire, which path, taskOutputs?
CP5PublishIs there an AssortmentPublishChange? Did Plan rows reach the Assortment?
CP6ConsumerReceived, processed — including the deletes?

2 · Universal question set — ask before touching anything

  1. Which entity layer — Item, ProjectItem, AssortmentItem? (W2)
  2. Which property level — Family, Option, All, Override? (W2)
  3. Who wrote it — user, load, workflow, formula? (W4/6/7)
  4. When, and what else happened at that time? (W4/6)
  5. How is it distributed across records and time? (W9)
  6. What changed — config, app version, file, volume? (W7/8)

3 · The three ladders, in outline

A — a load went wrong (CP2). failureArea first → config recorded on the process, diffed against last known-good → per-step artefacts, diff consecutive steps → column matching (unmatched columns ignored silently) → option-set alignment → federatedId presence and stability, including anything computed → the four flags. Values right in the artefacts but wrong in the app → load exonerated, go to CP3/CP4.

B — automation didn't happen (CP4). Is there a process at all? → none: isActive, installed in this org, expected app version, right triggerKey, did the change emit that event (a load emits create, not update) → some: which path ran, first match wins, oldData absent on creates → did actions succeed, read taskOutputs; COMPLETE-but-no-op means access grant or config pointing somewhere harmless. Intermittent and load-correlated → concurrency, not logic.

C — it didn't reach downstream (CP5/CP6). Did a publish happen — is there an AssortmentPublishChange? If the change came from a load, check shouldSkipAssortmentPublish — when true, no publish event is emitted at all → was the event delivered (it is a workflow, so run Ladder B on it) → did the consumer process it, including the delete file → is this a gap from a missed publish, which does not self-heal?

4 · Symptom shape → class of cause

ShapeClassGo to
Intermittent, load-correlated, different records each timeConcurrency raceparallelWorkerCount, message grouping
Consistent, silent, every record of a kindConfigurationProperty level, mapping, flags
Started on a date, nothing beforeA changeApp version, config, file format
One record, no patternDataThat row, that entity
Everything downstream, nothing upstreamDelivery / consumptionCP5, CP6

5 · Evidence and its lifetime — LoaderProcess + step artefacts · Process List (90 days) · Workflows Dashboard (90 days) · publish change objects (S3 links 24h) · config export. Check the horizon before promising root cause on an old problem.

Testing the card against three known scenarios

W2 — copy-down clobber. Shape row 2: consistent, silent, every option-level price. → configuration. Question 2 asks the property level; CP3 confirms the value is wrong on the entity, so upstream. Ladder A reaches the config and the four flags. Routed correctly — and note the card gets there via shape before any ladder runs.

W4 — the three-symptom load. Here the card partially fails, usefully. Ladder A handles the blanks and the duplicates. The stale FlexPLM is Ladder C, and the Slack storm is Ladder B. A card that assumes one ladder per incident routes you into A and leaves two symptoms unexamined. Fix: add a rule at the top — "Enumerate symptoms first. Assume separate causes until evidence connects them. Run one ladder per symptom." That is the single most valuable line on the card and only appeared under test.

W6 — the concurrency race. Shape row 1 fires immediately: intermittent, load-correlated, different records on re-run. Straight to grouping and worker count without a ladder at all. Routed correctly, and fastest of the three — which is the argument for putting the shape table before the ladders on the page.

What the test changed: the multi-symptom rule, and reordering the card so shape sits above ladders. Both were invisible until the card was run against problems whose answers were already known.

How to think about it

Writing it from memory first is the whole exercise. Copying the module produces a document you have transcribed, not one you have internalised — and during a live incident you use the version in your head, not the PDF. What you fail to recall is the diagnostic result: those are the parts to rehearse.

Bisecting is the discipline; walking the pipeline is the instinct. CP3 first is not arbitrary — opening the record is usually the cheapest look available and it eliminates half the pipeline in one action. Under pressure people start at CP1 because it feels orderly, and spend twenty minutes proving the file was fine.

Shape belongs above the ladders, and the test proved it. Two of the three scenarios were classified correctly from distribution alone, before any checkpoint was opened. Distribution is cheap information the customer has already given you for free in how they phrase the report — "different ones each time," "it was fine Monday," "all of them."

The multi-symptom rule is the one a card will not have unless it is tested. Every module teaches one problem at a time; real incidents arrive as three at once, and the instinct to unify them into a single elegant cause is strong and wrong. Week 4's scenario exists precisely to break that instinct, and it broke the card too — which is how you know the test was worth running.

Test with problems whose answers you already know. The point is not to solve them again; it is to check that the card routes correctly without you supplying the answer. Be honest about where you shortcut using knowledge the card does not contain — that gap is the missing item.

Include the evidence horizons on the card itself. 90 days and 24 hours are not background facts; they change what you can promise a customer in the first five minutes. A card that routes you perfectly to evidence that was deleted last month has still failed.

Sources
  • Validation Rules & Error Handling — Ladder A — failureArea and the error taxonomy.
  • Preprocessing Pipeline — Ladder A — the per-step artefacts and the diffing technique.
  • Observing Workflows — Ladder B — the Process List, statuses, taskOutputs, and the 90-day retention on the card.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — Ladder C — the change object, the delete file, and the 24-hour link expiry.
  • Key findings — Symptom shape → class of cause — the table in section 4, and the argument for putting it first.

Realistic customer scenario — a live incident

Summit Athletic. Thursday 09:40. The VP of Product calls you directly — not the usual channel — and you have a line review at 14:00 that this affects.

"The SS27 outlet assortment is a mess. Some styles show last season's pricing. A few styles the team definitely cut are back. And two of our merchandisers say they can't edit the margin field at all now, which they could on Monday. We did do a big data load Tuesday night, and I think IT installed something last week. I need this right for 2pm."

You have: Admin Console access, the customer's Loader configs, their app version history, and their integration engineer available on Slack.

Produce: (1) your first three actions in the first ten minutes, and why those three; (2) which checkpoint each of the three symptoms most likely sits at, and the evidence you'd pull for each; (3) which symptoms are plausibly one root cause and which are certainly separate, with reasoning; (4) what you tell the VP at 10:00, given you will not know the answer yet; (5) what you would do to make the 14:00 review survivable even if the root cause isn't fixed; and (6) the escalation you'd file if you're not converging by 11:30 — written out, with the fields from Concept 6.

The answer

1 · First three actions, first ten minutes

  1. Open one affected record and look at it (CP3). One outlet style showing last season's price: which layer holds the price, what is the value, at what property level. This bisects the pipeline in a single look — right on the entity means the problem is downstream, wrong means upstream — and it costs a minute.
  2. Get the two change events pinned to a timeline. The LoaderProcess ID and finish time for Tuesday night, and the app version history for "IT installed something last week" — what was installed, when, by whom. Two candidate causes are already named in the report; establishing exactly when each landed is what lets me attribute symptoms to them or rule them out.
  3. Message the integration engineer on Slack now, not later. "Anything change your end since Monday — file format, app version, config? And can you send me Tuesday's file and the last known-good one?" This runs in parallel while I work; it is the longest-latency dependency I have and the answer resolves several branches at once.

Why these three: one bisects, one builds the timeline, one starts the slowest thread. None of them is "form a theory."

2 · Checkpoint and evidence per symptom

SymptomLikely checkpointEvidence to pull
Styles show last season's pricingCP2/CP3Property level for the price field; whether Tuesday's file carried a price column; the per-step artefacts around it. Copy-down over option-level values, or a stale source column.
Cut styles are back in the assortmentCP2 → CP5partialAssortmentUpdate and the assortment load config; whether the file re-asserted membership. Then whether an AssortmentPublishChange exists for Tuesday.
Merchandisers cannot edit marginCP4 / configurationApp version history and what the install changed — a rule set making the field non-editable conditionally, or a type property policy. Configuration Copy categories, not the Process List.

3 · One cause or several

Symptoms 1 and 2 are plausibly one cause. Both are assortment-content symptoms, both consistent with Tuesday's load: a load that re-asserted membership and re-applied style-level prices produces exactly this pair. A wrong partialAssortmentUpdate plus Family-level copy-down is one file behaving as configured.

Symptom 3 is certainly separate. A data load cannot change field editability. Editability is a configuration concern — rule sets, type property policies — and arrives via an app install or a configuration change, not via a CSV. It also matches the other reported event ("IT installed something last week") and a different timeline. Treating all three as one root cause would send me hunting a loader explanation for a permissions symptom and I would not find one.

The reasoning that matters: ask what mechanism could produce each symptom. Two share a plausible mechanism; the third has none in common with them.

4 · What I tell the VP at 10:00

"Here is where I am. Confirmed: there are at least two separate problems, not one — the pricing and the missing styles look like they came from Tuesday night's load, and the editing problem is a configuration change, almost certainly from what was installed last week. Suspected: the load re-applied style-level prices over your team's outlet-specific ones and re-added styles that had been cut. Unknown: exactly which setting, and whether the old prices are recoverable. I am not going to guess at those.

What I need from you: for 2pm, is it more important that the pricing is right, or that the cut styles are out? If I can only fix one before the review, I want to fix the one that matters. I will come back at 11:30 either way."

5 · Making 14:00 survivable

  • Get the correct numbers, even if the system is still wrong. Publish history and the pre-load state tell you what the outlet prices were on Monday. A correct list in the reviewer's hand beats a broken system every time.
  • Fix the smaller, safer thing. Removing the wrongly re-added styles from the assortment is a bounded, reversible correction. Bulk-restoring 30 prices under time pressure with an unconfirmed root cause is not — and if the nightly load runs again it undoes the work anyway.
  • Stop the recurrence before the review, not after. If Tuesday's load is implicated, disable tonight's run until the config is confirmed. This is the highest-value action available and it takes one conversation.
  • Tell the merchandisers the margin field is a known issue with a workaround (someone with rights makes the edit), so it does not surface as a surprise in the room.
  • Give the VP a one-paragraph brief for the review — what is wrong, what is correct in the deck, what is being fixed. Their credibility in that room is the thing actually at stake.

6 · The escalation, filed at 11:30

SUMMARY   Summit Athletic — SS27 outlet assortment: option-level prices
          reverted and cut styles restored after Tue 02:00 load.
          Separate: margin field became non-editable after app install.
          Customer line review 14:00 today.

SEGMENT   Two issues.
          A) CP2/CP3 — loader config + property level. Values wrong on
             the entity, so upstream of the apps.
          B) CP4/config — field editability. Not load-related.

IDENTIFIERS
          Org: summit-prod
          LoaderProcess: <id>  (Tue 02:03–02:41, status COMPLETE,
                                failureArea NONE)
          Assortment: <id>  · Affected federatedIds: <list, 30>
          App version installed <date>: <app>@<version>
          AssortmentPublishChange: <id or "none found for window">

RULED OUT and how
          - Load failure: failureArea NONE, no errors, all rows applied.
          - Concurrency: symptoms are consistent across all 30 records
            and reproduce identically; not intermittent, not load-varying.
          - User error: merchandisers report no edits since Monday;
            change timing matches the load window, not working hours.
          - Single root cause: a load cannot alter field editability.

EVIDENCE PULLED
          - Property level for <priceField>: <value>
          - partialAssortmentUpdate on config used: <value>
          - Step artefacts <n> and <n+1> diffed: <finding>
          - Config export diffed against last known-good: <finding>

OPEN QUESTION
          Which of the installed app version's rule sets or type property
          policies governs margin editability, and was that intended?

ASK       Confirm whether <flag/level> produces the observed behaviour,
          and whether the pre-load values are recoverable from publish
          history for <assortment>.

IMPACT    Line review 14:00 today. Nightly load disabled pending answer.
          Workaround in place for cut styles.
How to think about it

The VP calling you directly is information about the incident's politics, not its technology. It means normal channels are considered too slow and someone senior is personally exposed. Nothing about your diagnosis should change; a great deal about your communication cadence should. Commit to a time and hit it — that is what buys you the room to work.

The strongest move in the first ten minutes is refusing to theorise. Three symptoms and two named recent changes make it very tempting to construct a story immediately. A theory that arrives before evidence gets tested by everyone for two days. Bisect at CP3, build the timeline, start the slow thread — none of those requires knowing the answer, and all three constrain it.

Symptom 3 is the discipline test. "We did a big load Tuesday" is a gift the customer hands you, and it explains two of three symptoms beautifully. The pull to make it explain the third is strong — and a load cannot alter field editability, so any theory that tries is wrong before it starts. Asking what mechanism could produce this? separates them in seconds.

Separate confirmed / suspected / unknown, explicitly and every time. Under pressure the two failure modes are stonewalling ("I don't know yet") and over-committing (a confident guess). Naming the three tiers gives the VP something real to act on while protecting you from a claim you cannot support, and it is the habit that makes you believable when the news is bad.

Ask the VP to prioritise — do not decide for them. "Pricing or cut styles, if I can only fix one" turns a passive escalation into a shared decision and gets you the business context you lack. It also means whatever you fix is the thing that mattered, which is the difference between a good outcome and a correct one.

Stabilise, contain, then diagnose. Disabling tonight's load is the single highest-value action in the whole scenario and requires no root cause at all — it stops the problem from re-occurring and from undoing any manual fix. New TAMs consistently under-value containment because it feels like avoidance rather than engineering.

The escalation is a handover, and "ruled out and how" is the field that makes it one. Anyone can list symptoms; what saves the next person a day is knowing which four branches are already closed and on what evidence. Filing at 11:30 rather than at 16:00 is itself a judgement — escalate while there is still time for the answer to be useful.

Sources
  • Loader Configuration Reference — partialAssortmentUpdate and the flags behind symptoms 1 and 2.
  • Property Type Handling — the property level that determines whether option-level prices survive a load.
  • Rule Sets — symptom 3 — conditional editability, and why an app install can remove a field's editability.
  • Observing Workflows — the Process List evidence, and the 90-day horizon worth stating while the incident is fresh.
  • Concept 6 — Escalating well — the fields the escalation above is built from.

Quiz

  1. State the localisation rule. Why is proposing a cause early expensive?

    Localise before theorising: the first question is not "why is this happening?" but "which segment of the pipeline is this in?" Only once the segment is fixed do you ask why. An early cause is expensive because a theory arriving before evidence anchors everyone — the team starts testing it, and two days go to a hypothesis that was never constrained by anything. Localisation is cheap (the platform records almost everything) and eliminates whole categories of cause in a single check.

  2. Name the six checkpoints. Which do you check first, and why does that beat walking the pipeline in order?

    CP1 source file · CP2 loader · CP3 entities · CP4 workflows/formulas · CP5 publish · CP6 consumer. Check CP3 first — look at the entity itself. If the value is correct there, the problem is downstream (CP4–CP6); if wrong, it's upstream (CP1–CP2). That beats walking in order because it bisects: one look halves the search space, and CP3 is usually the cheapest check available since it's just opening the record. Walking left to right is the instinct; bisecting is the discipline.

  3. Give the six universal questions. Translate two of them into a merchandiser's language.

    Which entity layer? · Which property level? · Which federatedId, and what generates it? · Create or update? · Which process ID? · When did it last work? Translated: "is this a style-level or a colour-level field?" (property level) and "were these products already in the system, or did they arrive with this file?" (create vs update). Asking in the customer's vocabulary gets better answers from a merchandiser than platform terms do — and it's the same question.

  4. Run Ladder B from memory. At which step do you jump if a process already exists?

    (1) Is there a process at all? (2) isActive, installed in this org, expected app version? (3) Right triggerKey? (4) Did the change emit that event? (5) Which path ran — read triggerEvent, evaluate conditionals top-down, remembering first-match-wins and absent oldData on creates. (6) Did actions succeed — read taskOutputs. (7) Intermittent and load-correlated → concurrency, not logic. If a process already exists, jump to step 5 — the trigger clearly matched, so the question becomes which path ran and what the actions did.

  5. What does it mean when a workflow process is COMPLETE but nothing happened?

    It means the workflow fired and ran to completion without doing anything useful. The usual causes are a missing or read-only accessGrant — the action attempted a write it wasn't permitted — or a config value pointing somewhere harmless, such as a staging endpoint promoted into production. Read taskOutputs per task to see what each actually produced. Also remember tasks are strictly serial: if an early task failed, later ones were never run at all rather than failing.

  6. Why is "if you retry, does the same record fail?" such a high-yield question?

    Because the answer splits the possibility space in half in two words. Same record fails → logic or configuration — something deterministic about that record or the config is wrong. Different record each time → timing or concurrency — a race, a message-grouping gap, or an integration seam. Customers never volunteer this, and it's more informative than any amount of detail about the records themselves. Ask it alongside "does it affect every record or some?" and "did it start at a particular time?".

  7. A problem started on a specific date and "nothing changed." Name three things that change without anyone changing them.

    A deploy — an app version published or installed, changing workflows or actions. A configuration promotion — someone ran types loadAll, or a rule set or property level changed. An expiry — a runtime deprecation (nodeRuntime), a credential rotation, or a certificate. Also valid: seasonal boundaries (a new project/season starting changes which entities are in play) and volume crossing a concurrency threshold that was always there.

  8. What evidence exists, and what is its lifetime? What must you establish before promising root cause on an old problem?

    LoaderProcess + per-step artefacts (no documented expiry) · Process List — status, triggerEvent, taskOutputs, timings — 90 days · Event Workflows Dashboard — 90 days · Workflow Artifacts — deleted with the process at 90 days · AssortmentPublishChange + baseline — links expire in 24h but refetching refreshes them · config export (current state, on demand). Before promising root cause on an old problem, establish what evidence still exists — and tell the customer to preserve exports now if the investigation will be long. Note deleting a workflow destroys its logs immediately, not at 90 days.

  9. Name the six elements of a good escalation. Which is most often omitted, and what does omitting it cost?

    (1) The segment it's localised to. (2) Identifiers — process IDs, federatedIds, change IDs, org slug, app version. (3) What you ruled out, and how. (4) The shape — intermittent or consistent, all records or some, since when. (5) Business impact and the clock. (6) Your hypothesis, marked as a hypothesis. Most often omitted: number 3 — and omitting it means the next person repeats every check you already did, which is usually the largest single cost in an escalation. It's also what separates a TAM from a ticket relay.

  10. Why should you never answer "who's wrong?", and what do you answer instead?

    Because adjudicating blame between a customer's own teams costs you the neutrality that makes you useful to both — and it is never the question they actually need answered. The merchandiser and the PLM team both have to keep working together after you leave the call. Answer instead: "here's what happened, and here's what we change." Describe the mechanism, name the fix for the immediate problem and the change that stops it recurring, and let the account of what happened speak for itself.

Hands-on challenge

Assemble the full account-inheritance kit — the artefacts built across this program: the Week 2 data-model health check, Week 3 publish integration review, Week 4 pre-load checklist, Week 5 integration design review, Week 6 workflow review, Week 7 routing guide, Week 8 security response kit, and this module's triage card. Put them in one document with a one-paragraph note on when each is used. This is the thing you actually carry into the job, and the capstone assumes you have it.

The answer

The account-inheritance kit. One document, eight artefacts, ordered by when you use them rather than by the week that produced them.

Front matter — one page

"This kit is what I run on an account, in this order. Sections 1–4 are for inheriting or reviewing an account. Sections 5–7 are for a request or a design. Section 8 is for an incident. Everything here is a checklist of known failure modes, not a general questionnaire — every item exists because it has gone wrong somewhere. Owner: [name]. Last reviewed: [date]."

When to use what

#ArtefactWkTriggerOutput
1Customer landscape discovery1New account, first substantive callSystem map + attribute ownership starting point
2Data-model health check2Inheriting any account, before forming opinionsProperty-level risks, federatedId stability, UI-created records
3Publish integration review3Any account with a publish consumerDelete handling, recovery, reconciliation gaps
4Workflow review6Inheriting, and before any workflow shipsUnreachable paths, create-path storms, concurrency, org budget
5Pre-load checklist4First production load, or any unusually large oneGo / no-go on a load
6Integration design review5Before anyone builds against the APIRanked findings: blocking vs. follow-up
7"Can it do X?" routing guide7Any capability requestThe right rung + cost of ownership
8Security questionnaire kit8InfoSec involvement, usually pre-contractConfident / qualified / escalate, with owners
9Triage card9Any incidentA checkpoint, then a ladder

The paragraph on when each gets used

"Order matters more than completeness. On a new account I run 1 before anything else, because the system landscape decides which of the rest are even relevant. On an inherited account I start with 2 — and before that, a config export, because that is ground truth rather than anyone's memory — then 3 and 4 to find what is already broken but not yet reported. Those three tell me more in a week than three months of tickets.

After that the kit is reactive: 5 before a load, 6 before a build, 7 when someone asks for a feature, 8 when security appears, 9 when something breaks. The reviews (3, 4, 6) share one property worth stating: they are cheapest before go-live and most valuable after an incident, so if I inherit an account mid-flight I run them anyway and expect to find things nobody has reported yet.

Two of these are written for people other than me — 7 goes to a product owner and 8 to a security team — so they carry no internal shorthand and must be dated. The rest are working instruments and can be terse."

The cross-cutting page — what every artefact is really checking

Worth putting at the front, because it is what makes the kit learnable rather than nine lists:

  • Failures that produce no error. Unmatched columns ignored, step-4 silent transforms, unreachable workflow paths, shouldSkipAssortmentPublish, formulas returning quietly wrong values, staging endpoints in production. Almost every item across the kit is looking for one of these.
  • Decisions you cannot undo. App ownership and identifier, deleting a workflow, a replace-mode assortment load. These belong in the first conversation, not the review.
  • Evidence horizons. Workflow evidence 90 days, S3 links 24 hours. They bound what you can ever explain — so any trend you care about needs a monthly snapshot started now.
  • Shared, invisible capacity. 60 concurrent processes per org, no per-team allocation, no utilisation view. Nobody owns this by default, which is why it is on your checklist.
  • Claims to verify, not assert. Rule-set enforcement on write paths, inbound webhook verification, end-user role mapping. Keep this list short and dated; every item you close is a deal cycle shortened.
How to think about it

Assembling is not collating. The value added by this step is the ordering and the triggers — nine documents in a folder is not a kit. The question each artefact answers is easy; knowing which one to reach for, and in what sequence, is the thing that makes someone effective in their first month.

The cross-cutting page is the real deliverable. Once you see that most items across all nine artefacts are hunting the same five underlying properties — silent failures, irreversible decisions, evidence horizons, shared capacity, unverified claims — the kit stops being a memory exercise. You can derive a missing check rather than remembering it, which is what lets you handle a problem no artefact anticipated.

Distinguish the artefacts written for others. The routing guide and the security kit circulate outside your team, which changes their constraints: no shorthand, honest caveats, a date and an owner. An internal checklist that goes stale costs you time; an external one that goes stale becomes a source of confidently wrong answers with your name on it.

Note that reviews are cheapest before go-live and most valuable after an incident. That asymmetry is why an inherited account gets all three reviews run immediately even though nothing is on fire. Every finding is something the customer is currently living with and has not reported — and finding those before they surface is the entire basis of being treated as a technical reference rather than a support contact.

Keep it thin enough to actually run. A kit that takes three weeks to execute does not get executed. Each artefact should be runnable in an hour or two; if one has grown past that, the excess is probably documentation rather than diagnosis.

This is the bridge to the TAM practice page. The service catalogue there is built on exactly these artefacts — naming them as repeatable deliverables is what lets you say "that is a design review, here is what it involves and when you get it" instead of absorbing an unbounded ask. The kit is the raw material; the catalogue is the operating model.

Sources
  • Week 2 — data-model health check — artefact 2, and the pattern all the checklists follow: known failure modes, not general questions.
  • Key findings — the cross-cutting page is a condensed form of this reference view — silent failures, irreversible decisions, numbers to memorise.
  • TAM practice — the service catalogue — where these artefacts become named, repeatable services with turnaround times.
  • Configuration Copy — the config export that precedes artefact 2 on any inherited account.

I know this when I can…

  • Localise before theorising, and bisect at CP3 by reflex.
  • Run all three ladders from memory.
  • Name a class of cause from symptom shape alone, before looking at any data.
  • Anchor every claim to a named piece of evidence, and know when that evidence has expired.
  • Write an escalation an engineer can act on in minutes.
  • Hold an anxious customer through an open incident without over-promising or assigning blame.

Module 9 · a synthesis of Weeks 2–8. Escalation routing is a sensible default built from the platform's own structure; confirm VibeIQ's actual internal process in your first weeks.
