Module 10 · Week 10 · Capstone

Applied TAM practice

Everything, applied to the four situations that actually make up the job: discovery, architecture review, incident, and quarterly review. Nine weeks built the knowledge; this week turns it into behaviour under real conditions — incomplete information, competing priorities, and someone waiting on an answer.

On this page

  1. 🤝The four situations
  2. 🤝Discovery that predicts
  3. 🤝Architecture and workflow review
  4. 🤝QBRs: reporting risk, not activity
  5. 🤝Inheriting an account: the first thirty days
  6. ✅The capstone — Meridian Outdoor, four phases
  7. 📚After Week 10

Learning objectives

By the end of this week you can (1) run a technical discovery that predicts a customer's future problems rather than just cataloguing their systems, (2) review an architecture or a workflow design and disagree in a way that keeps the engineer collaborating, (3) tell a health story in a QBR using evidence the platform actually provides, (4) inherit an unfamiliar account and be useful within thirty days, and (5) do all of it while knowing which questions you must take away.

Concept 1 — The four situations

Nearly everything you do is one of these, and each has a different failure mode:

SituationYou're really doingFails when
DiscoveryPredicting what will break, from how they work todayYou catalogue systems instead of finding constraints
Architecture reviewFinding the flaw before production doesYou're right but the engineer stops listening
IncidentLocalising, then communicating (Week 9)You theorise early, or over-promise timing
QBRTelling a health story with evidenceYou report activity instead of risk

Concept 2 — Discovery that predicts

Week 1's landscape template listed a customer's systems. That's the starting point, not the instrument. A good discovery question has a property: the answer changes what you expect to go wrong. Sharpened, grouped by what each answer predicts:

AskBecause the answer predictsWk
"What generates your style number, and what could ever change it?"Duplicate records and orphans2 · 4
"Which attributes differ between colourways, and which are always the same across a style?"Property-level modelling, and future copy-down clobber2
"Will anyone create products by hand in the UI before the integration is live?"The federatedId gap and the duplicate bug2 · 4
"When a style is cut, what has to happen in PLM — and who notices if it doesn't?"Whether missed deletes will be caught or discovered in a review3
"What changes to products never go through a publish?"Whether events alone will cover them, or polling is also needed5
"Who owns cost, and who owns retail price?"The attribute ownership map, and the future "PLM overwrote our margin" dispute5
"What's your seasonal calendar, and when is the heaviest load?"Concurrency contention and the worst possible incident timing4 · 8
"Who will build and host any custom UI, and who is on call for it?"Extension ownership and a future 'VibeIQ is down' report7
"Whose org will custom apps be created in?"Permanent, unfixable ownership7
"What are your data residency and compliance requirements?"Whether there's a procurement blocker you should surface now8

Notice how many of these are best asked before a decision is made and are worthless afterwards. That is the real argument for TAM involvement in implementation: not oversight, but the handful of questions whose value expires. Frame it that way internally — "there are six questions that cost nothing now and can't be fixed later" is a more persuasive case for your time than "I should be involved."

Concept 3 — Architecture and workflow review

You did a full one in Week 5 and a workflow one in Week 6. The method, generalised:

  1. Establish the requirement first. Half of bad designs are correct solutions to a misunderstood requirement. Ask what happens if this doesn't run, and who notices.
  2. Trace one record end to end through their design, out loud. Most flaws surface here without any need for cleverness.
  3. Test the unhappy paths — down for a day, duplicate delivery, partial failure, bulk load, the first day of a new season.
  4. Check the irreversible decisions — app ownership, identifiers, property levels, federatedId source.
  5. Name the operational cost — who runs it, who's alerted, what happens when the person who built it leaves.
  6. Rank the findings. Go-live blockers vs. follow-ups. A flat list of twelve problems gets ignored; three blockers get fixed.

The workflow review checklist, specifically

Because supporting customer workflows is a core part of this role, this one is worth knowing cold:

  • Unreachable paths — read top-down, find the first conditional that can always be true. Everything below it is dead.
  • Create vs. update — what does every conditional do when oldData is absent?
  • Trigger fitness — does the event they need actually exist? (assortment has only publish.)
  • Access grants — does every action have write where it writes?
  • Config values — is any endpoint pointing at the wrong environment?
  • Message grouping — what can collide, and is it grouped? What does parallelWorkerCount do under a bulk load?
  • Org budget — what else runs in this org, and when?
  • Failure visibility — who sees a FAILED process, and how soon?
  • Change process — do they understand this is a release, not a setting?

On disagreeing well: lead with what's right in their design, be specific about consequence rather than about correctness, and give the symptom rather than the principle. "This will work until someone's consumer is down overnight, and then Centric keeps styles they've cut — which surfaces in a line review" lands where "you should handle idempotency" does not. You are not trying to win; you are trying to have the change made by someone who still calls you next time.

Concept 4 — QBRs: reporting risk, not activity

The failure mode is presenting usage statistics that nobody acts on. A QBR should answer three questions: is this working, what's at risk, and what should we do next?

SignalWhere it comes fromWhat it tells you
Workflow failure trendEvent Workflows Dashboard (90 days)Operational health. A rising line is visible weeks before a ticket.
Load success and volumeLoaderProcess recordsWhether the data pipeline is healthy and growing
Publish cadencePublish history per assortmentWhether decisions are actually being made in VibeIQ
Adoption by teamWho is in Boards vs. Plan vs. ShowcaseWhich team is the weak link (Week 1) — design lagging means creative context arrives late
Export behaviourConversation, not telemetryStill exporting to PowerPoint or Excel? That's the incumbent winning.
Open unknownsYour own listCredibility — showing you track what you owe them

The one slide that matters

Health is not "how much are they using it." It is "are decisions being made here, and is the context surviving downstream?" — the Week 1 value statement, measured. Publish cadence plus downstream sync health answers it directly. Usage minutes do not, and a room full of executives can tell the difference.

Bring one risk you found before they did, every time. It costs you an hour of looking at the failure chart and it is the single most reliable way to be treated as an advisor rather than a vendor contact. It also inverts the meeting: instead of defending the last quarter, you're the person who spotted something.

Concept 5 — Inheriting an account: the first thirty days

You'll rarely start from a greenfield. Run the kit in this order — each step makes the next one cheaper:

  1. Config export. contrail types getAll. Read the property levels, rule sets and policies. This is ground truth, not what anyone remembers.
  2. Data model health check (Week 2). Property-level map, federatedId source and stability, UI-created records, assortment structure.
  3. Workflow inventory (Week 6). What exists, what triggers it, what's grouped, what's failing. Look at the 90-day failure chart.
  4. Integration review (Weeks 3 & 5). Pattern, delete handling, recovery, reconciliation cadence, key scoping.
  5. Platform & ops (Week 8). Org topology, who holds credentials, custom apps and their runtimes, concurrency budget.
  6. The people map. Which team is lagging, who the sceptic is, who has been burned before.
  7. Write down what you found and what you'd change, ranked. Share it. That document is your credibility.

Step 7 is the one people skip because it feels like homework. Do it anyway. An inherited account has a history you weren't part of, and a written, ranked assessment establishes that you understand it better than the last person did — which is the entire basis on which a customer decides whether to trust a new TAM.

The capstone — Meridian Outdoor, four phases

A new account, run end to end. Each phase uses a different part of the program, and later phases are shaped by choices you make in earlier ones. Work them in order and don't read ahead.

The account

Meridian Outdoor — mid-size outdoor apparel and footwear brand, ~1,200 styles per season, two seasons plus a holiday drop. Systems: PTC FlexPLM, SAP, Bynder for assets, Anaplan for financial planning. Line planning today runs in Excel with images pasted in, reviewed in PowerPoint. They have bought VibeIQ, implementation starts in six weeks, and a systems integrator will do the build. You are their TAM from today.

Phase 1 · Discovery before any decision is made

You have one 90-minute call with their VP of Merchandising, a merchandising systems manager, and an integration architect.

  1. Write the twelve questions you will actually ask, ordered, with the reason each is on the list — specifically, what each answer would change.
  2. Mark the three whose value expires if not asked before implementation starts, and say what it costs to miss each.
  3. They will ask "so what does VibeIQ replace?" Write your answer in under sixty seconds of speech.
  4. The integration architect says: "We'll just point our nightly PLM export at it, same as we did for Anaplan." Write your reply — you disagree, but it's minute six of the relationship.
The answer

1 · Twelve questions, ordered, with what each answer changes

  1. "Walk me through your last line review — who was in the room, what were they looking at, how long did someone spend building it?" → Whether VibeIQ makes good on the moment it exists for. Opens with them talking about their work rather than their systems, and everything after is calibrated by the answer.
  2. "What generates your style number, and what could ever change it?" → The federatedId strategy. The integrator plans to key on FlexPLM's internal style ID; this establishes whether that ID is stable and business-meaningful or an internal surrogate.
  3. "For cost, margin, price, image, lifecycle status — which system is authoritative for each, and who edits it there?" → The entire integration design. With FlexPLM, SAP, Bynder and Anaplan in play, this is four-way and nobody has written it down.
  4. "Does a product record ever exist before it's real enough for FlexPLM?" → Whether VibeIQ is a source or a sink. If placeholders have nowhere to live today, that is the strongest fit signal available.
  5. "Which attributes need to flow back into the decision — cost, sell-through, inventory?" → One-way publish vs. two-way integration. Routinely scoped one-way because nobody asked.
  6. "Does the same style ever carry different terms in different channels or regions?" → Whether values must live on the AssortmentItem. Gets modelled too high and discovered in production.
  7. "What does Anaplan own that VibeIQ will need, and how does it get there today?" → Whether there is a fifth integration nobody has scoped. Planning tools are consistently forgotten in the first architecture.
  8. "Walk me through your GTM calendar. What are the fixed dates for the next two seasons?" → The implementation timeline. Seasonal deadlines do not move; the calendar decides what "late" means, not the project plan.
  9. "How many assortments does one line become, and who owns each?" → Project/assortment structure and the scale of the publish surface.
  10. "Once the line is approved, how does it reach FlexPLM today — who does it, how long does it take?" → Integration priority and the quantified business case. Usually a person re-keying for a week.
  11. "Who else is going live in the next six months, and how did the last rollout go?" → Your political map and realistic pace.
  12. "When a decision gets reversed downstream, how does anyone find out why it was made?" → Whether they feel the problem VibeIQ actually solves. Closes on the reason they bought it.

2 · The three whose value expires

Q2 — the identifier. Once the integrator builds and runs a nightly feed keyed on FlexPLM's internal ID, the key is embedded in every record created. Asking later does not undo it. Cost of missing it: if that ID ever changes format, the next load duplicates the catalogue — and the remedy is a data migration, not a config change.

Q3 — attribute ownership. The integrator writes their design in four weeks. Without the map, they will infer ownership from whatever is convenient, and both systems will end up writing the same fields. Cost: intermittent last-writer-wins bugs that present as flakiness and take weeks to attribute.

Q6 — channel/region variance. This determines which values must sit on the AssortmentItem. Discovered after go-live, it is a re-modelling exercise touching every integration that reads those fields. Cost: the highest of the three, because it invalidates work already built and tested.

Why these three and not the calendar or the political map: those stay answerable all year. These three become expensive rather than merely late, because the integrator's build makes them structural.

3 · "So what does VibeIQ replace?" — under sixty seconds

"Not FlexPLM and not SAP — those stay exactly where they are. What it replaces is the way the line actually gets decided today: the Excel file with images pasted in, the deck someone rebuilds before every review, and the mood board from concept that nobody can find by March. Those three become one live workspace, with cost and margin sitting next to the picture at the moment you cut the line. Then the decisions carry forward into FlexPLM with the reasoning attached, instead of being re-keyed. So it is upstream of your PLM, alongside it — it is the decision layer, not another product system."

4 · Replying to "we'll just point our nightly PLM export at it, same as Anaplan"

"That is the right instinct and it will probably be most of the answer — a nightly Loader feed is exactly how this normally works. The one thing I would want to pressure-test before we commit is that Anaplan consumes numbers, and VibeIQ is going to be creating and editing product records that flow back to you. So it is less like a feed and more like a two-way sync, which mostly changes two things: which system wins on each attribute, and what identifier we key on. Can we spend twenty minutes on those two before you finalise the design? If they land where you expect, you have lost nothing and your design is unchanged."
How to think about it

The instruction is "questions you will actually ask." Ninety minutes with three people is roughly twelve real questions if you let them talk — and you must let them talk, because the useful material is in the digressions. A list of thirty is a fantasy; the discipline of exactly twelve forces you to drop everything you could look up later.

Order for the room, not for the taxonomy. Opening with the line review gets the VP describing their own work and establishes you understand their job. Opening with "what generates your style number" makes you an integration consultant and loses the two business people for the rest of the call.

The expiry question is really about who is building. An SI writes a design in week four and starts creating production records shortly after. Every decision that gets embedded in that build is expensive to revisit — so the questions that expire are precisely the ones whose answers become structural. Reversibility, not importance, is the sorting criterion.

Question 4 is minute six of the relationship and that constraint dominates. The architect is not wrong — a nightly feed is most of the answer — and telling them they have misunderstood the platform six minutes in costs you the person who will implement everything you recommend for the next year. Agree with the true part, name the one real difference (bidirectionality), and ask for twenty minutes rather than demanding a redesign. Being right and being listened to are different achievements, and only one of them changes the outcome.

The Anaplan comparison is the tell worth catching. It reveals they are modelling VibeIQ as a reporting sink. That single assumption, left uncorrected, produces a one-way integration and no ownership map — which is Phase 2's design almost exactly.

What a weak answer looks like: a systems inventory. "Which systems do you have?" you already know from the brief. Discovery that only catalogues produces a diagram; discovery that predicts produces a risk list, and that is the difference Week 10 is testing.

Sources
  • Week 1 — customer landscape discovery template — the template these twelve sharpen; the grouping and the "what does the answer change" discipline come from there.
  • Concept 2 — Discovery that predicts — the property a good question has: the answer changes what you expect to go wrong.
  • Data Loading — Getting Started — question 2 — federatedId as the upsert key and why stability is the operative property.
  • Integration Patterns — overview — questions 5 and 10 — the two-way shape behind the reply in part 4.

Phase 2 · Architecture review week 4 of implementation

The integrator sends their design:

  • Nightly Loader feed from FlexPLM, keyed on FlexPLM's internal style ID, run from the CLI by their consultant.
  • All pricing attributes modelled at Family level "since price is a style-level concept."
  • A workflow on item|update that pushes approved styles back to FlexPLM, parallelWorkerCount: 20, no message grouping.
  • A Document Automation extension for margin approval, hosted on the integrator's Vercel account.
  • The app created in the integrator's org "for now."
  • Assortment publishes consumed by polling /api/change-history hourly, from the last successful run time.
  • One API key, stored in their pipeline config, used for dev and prod.
  1. Find every problem. For each: the failure, the symptom Meridian would report, and the fix.
  2. Identify the two that become unfixable after go-live.
  3. Rank: what blocks go-live, what's a follow-up, what you'd let go.
  4. One item requires you to say you don't know and find out. Which, and who do you ask?
  5. Write the opening two paragraphs of your response.
The answer

1 · Every problem — failure, symptom, fix

A · Keyed on FlexPLM's internal style ID
Failure: the upsert key is a surrogate nobody governs. If it is ever reformatted, re-issued, or if a style is recreated in FlexPLM, the key moves and the next load creates duplicates.
Symptom: "We have two of everything."
Fix: key on a stable business identifier, or contractually freeze the FlexPLM ID and document that it is now an integration key.

B · All pricing modelled at Family level "since price is a style-level concept"
Failure: Family copies down and wins. Any colourway-specific price a merchant sets is silently reverted by the next nightly load.
Symptom: "We set outlet prices on Monday and they were gone Tuesday. Nobody touched them."
Fix: Override, not Family — still copies down, but a deliberate option-level value survives. And channel price belongs on the AssortmentItem entirely, not on the Item at any level.

C · Workflow on item|update, parallelWorkerCount: 20, no message grouping
Failure: two problems. (i) A concurrency race — 20 workers on the same item family, read-modify-write, lost updates and cross-contaminated values. (ii) item|update fires once per row on every nightly load: 1,200 styles is 1,200 pushes to FlexPLM whether or not anything was approved.
Symptom: "Some styles have the wrong data, different ones each time" and "FlexPLM gets thousands of updates for styles nobody touched."
Fix: add dynamicMessageGroupId keyed on the item family, lower the worker count, and add a conditional so it only fires on an actual transition to approved (with a create-safe form, since oldData is absent on creates).

D · Document Automation extension hosted on the integrator's Vercel account
Failure: Meridian's margin approval depends on a third party's hosting, uptime, TLS and billing — and document context crosses into it, making the SI a data processor.
Symptom: "Margin approval is down and we cannot reach anyone," during a line review. Or InfoSec blocking at contract.
Fix: host on infrastructure Meridian owns; put the SI in their vendor assessment until then. Also ask whether this needs to be an extension at all — a margin floor is a rule set.

E · App created in the integrator's org "for now"
Failure: the org that first creates an app is the owner, and ownership cannot be modified. The identifier is reserved globally and immutably, so it cannot be recreated under the same name either.
Symptom: two years later, changing integrator, and discovering their automation belongs to the old one.
Fix: create the app under Meridian's org now, with the SI given developer access.

F · Polling /api/change-history hourly from the last successful run time
Failure: temporal filtering is not a cursor. Changes written during a run fall between windows; boundary exclusivity and clock skew drop or duplicate records at the seam.
Symptom: "Some styles just never make it across, different ones each time, we cannot reproduce it."
Fix: advance the watermark only on successful completion, overlap the window deliberately, make processing idempotent, and add a periodic baseline reconciliation.

G · Polling chosen at all, given the assortment publish requirement
Failure: defensible, but nobody has written down what it misses — latency, and the fact that deletes need explicit handling regardless of pattern. There is no mention of the delete file anywhere in this design.
Symptom: "Styles we cut are still active in FlexPLM and sourcing has started costing them."
Fix: confirm delete handling explicitly; document the pattern choice and its trade-offs.

H · One API key for dev and prod, in the pipeline config
Failure: no blast-radius containment, no revocation path, and dev code can write production data. A credential in a config file is probably in version control.
Symptom: "A test run overwrote production prices." Or InfoSec blocking at contract.
Fix: a key per environment, in secrets management, with documented rotation and offboarding.

I · The load runs from the CLI by their consultant
Failure: the CLI cannot use a stored configuration, so the consultant is passing a local copy that drifts from anything reviewed. It also caches credentials to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on their laptop, permanently.
Symptom: "The load behaved differently last night and nobody changed anything." Plus an offboarding gap.
Fix: move production loads to the API with a stored configuration; treat the CLI as a local tool.

2 · The two that become unfixable after go-live

E — app ownership. Genuinely irreversible. Ownership cannot be transferred and the identifier cannot be reused. Everything else on this list is a refactor; this one has no technical remedy at any price.

A — the identifier, practically rather than technically. Once a nightly feed has created every record keyed on the FlexPLM ID, changing the key means re-keying the entire catalogue and reconciling every downstream system that has stored those IDs. Possible; nobody will authorise it.

Honourable mention: B — property level is changeable, but the data destroyed by copy-down between now and then is not recoverable.

3 · Ranking

  • Blocks go-live: E (irreversible, fix today), A (structural), B (silent data destruction), C (wrong data plus a load storm), H (security and blast radius).
  • Blocks go-live, cheap: F and G — both are consumer-side code changes, and both produce silently wrong data downstream.
  • Follow-up: D — the hosting must move, but a plan with a date is acceptable if the extension survives the "should this be a rule set?" question. I — move production loads off the CLI in the first month.
  • Would let go: nothing here, honestly. But if forced, the formal pattern-choice documentation in G is debt, not a defect.

4 · The item where I say I don't know

Whether rule sets are enforced on the API and Loader write paths. It matters here because the margin-floor control (D) and any pricing validation depend on it: if rule sets bind only the editor, then a nightly load can write a below-floor price regardless, and the control has to live in the pipeline instead. The documentation says they evaluate client-side, which implies the answer but does not settle it.

I would ask VibeIQ engineering, via the CSM — and say to the integrator: "Good question, and I want to give you a definite answer rather than a guess. Thursday." It is on the standing colleague-questions list for exactly this reason.

5 · The opening two paragraphs of my response

"Thanks for sending this at week four rather than week ten — most of what I have below is much cheaper to act on now, and a couple of items are genuinely time-limited. The overall shape is right: a nightly Loader feed with an outbound sync is how this normally works, and I am not proposing you redesign it.

One item I would like to resolve today, before anything else, because it is the only thing on the list that cannot be fixed later: the app needs to be created under Meridian's org rather than yours. App ownership cannot be transferred after creation and the identifier is reserved globally, so 'transfer at handover' is not available as an option — it is not a policy question, there is no mechanism. You keep exactly the same development access and iterate exactly as fast. Everything else in this note can be scheduled; this one is a five-minute change today and an unsolvable problem in six weeks."
How to think about it

Sort by reversibility before severity. Nine findings is a lot to land in one message, and the natural instinct is to lead with the most technically interesting — the concurrency race, or the polling seam. But those stay fixable indefinitely. App ownership becomes permanent the moment go-live pressure makes "we'll sort it at handover" sound reasonable. Lead with what expires.

Notice that this design is competent. Nothing here is careless; it is what a good engineer writes without three platform-specific facts (links expire, deletes do not accumulate, ownership is permanent) and one data-modelling fact (Family copies down and wins). Reviewing it as sloppy loses the person who has to implement every fix.

Item B is the one an engineer will defend, and they are half right. "Price is a style-level concept" is true as a business statement and wrong as a modelling decision, because the exception — one colourway priced differently — is legitimate and routine. The Family/Override test is exactly this: is an exception ever legitimate? Framing it as "you are right that it usually follows the style, which is why Override is the correct level" concedes the true part and still gets the change.

Item C is two problems wearing one costume. The concurrency race and the trigger breadth are independent, and fixing only the grouping leaves 1,200 spurious pushes a night. When a single design line produces two symptoms, say so — otherwise half the fix ships and everyone believes it is done.

The "I don't know" item is a strength, deployed deliberately. Nine confident findings followed by one honest "let me confirm by Thursday" makes the nine more credible, not less. It also models the behaviour you want from them at go-live, when guessing gets expensive.

The opening paragraphs do specific work. Credit the timing (behaviour you want repeated), affirm the shape (so the list does not read as a rejection), then isolate the one irreversible item and give it its own paragraph with a plain mechanical reason — "there is no mechanism" is not a negotiating position, and framing it as fact rather than preference is what stops it becoming a debate. Ending with the cost asymmetry — five minutes today, unsolvable in six weeks — is what makes it happen today.

Sources
  • App Lifecycle — item E — the global immutable identifier and the rule that ownership cannot be modified.
  • Property Type Handling — item B — Family vs. Override copy-down semantics.
  • Creating Workflows — item C — grouping, worker count, trigger keys, and oldData on creates.
  • Change History Polling — items F and G — temporal filtering and the last-sync-timestamp pattern.
  • Authentication & API Access — item H — per-environment key scoping and key hygiene.

Phase 3 · Incident week 3 after go-live

Tuesday 08:15. The merchandising systems manager messages:

"Something's wrong with FW27. A lot of styles have the wrong price — it looks like the style price, not the colour-specific ones we set last week. Also our FlexPLM team says they got about 900 update messages overnight for styles nobody touched. And three styles we dropped are still showing in the outlet assortment. Big review Thursday."

  1. Which checkpoint does each of the three symptoms sit at?
  2. Which are one root cause and which are separate? Justify it.
  3. Your first three actions, and the exact evidence you'd pull.
  4. Trace it back to Phase 2: which design decisions you reviewed produced this? If you flagged them and were overruled, how does that change what you say now — and what you say to nobody?
  5. What you tell them at 09:00, and how you make Thursday survivable.
The answer

1 · Checkpoint per symptom

SymptomCPWhy there
Style price showing instead of the colour-specific prices set last weekCP3 (→ CP2)Entity layer and property level. The value is wrong on the record itself, so upstream of the apps.
~900 update messages overnight for untouched stylesCP4Workflow behaviour — trigger breadth, once per row.
Three dropped styles still in the outlet assortmentCP5 / CP6Publish and consumption — did the removal reach the change object, and did the consumer apply the delete file.

2 · One root cause, or several

Symptoms 1 and 2 are one root cause — the nightly load. Phase 2 item B modelled pricing at Family level, so the nightly feed copies the style price down over the colour-specific values merchandisers set last week. That same load upserts 1,200 items, each emitting item|update, and the Phase 2 item C workflow is bound to item|update with no transition condition — so it pushes every touched style to FlexPLM. One load, one mechanism, two symptoms. The timing confirms it: both appeared overnight, together.

Symptom 3 is separate, and near-certainly so. Deletions are not produced by a load re-asserting prices; they are a publish/consumption concern. The most likely explanation is that the polling consumer (item F/G) never handles deletes at all — nothing in that design mentioned the delete file. A secondary possibility is that the polling window dropped the publish containing those removals, which is the same F defect in a different costume.

The reasoning: ask what mechanism could produce each symptom. Copy-down and event volume share one; a missing deletion shares nothing with them. Two of three is not "mostly one cause" — the third needs its own investigation or it does not get fixed.

3 · First three actions and the exact evidence

  1. Open one affected style (CP3) and read the property level for the price field. Evidence: the property configuration — FAMILY or OVERRIDE. This is seconds of work and it either confirms the mechanism outright or kills my hypothesis before I invest in it.
  2. Pull the overnight LoaderProcess. Evidence: failureArea (expect NONE), the configuration recorded on it, and whether the file carried a price column. A load with no price column cannot have clobbered prices however the property is levelled — this separates configuration from payload.
  3. Filter the Process List to the FlexPLM push workflow for the overnight window. Evidence: process count (expect ~900–1,200), triggerEvent on a sample, and status (expect COMPLETE). Confirms symptom 2 is trigger breadth rather than a fault, and rules out a failure-driven explanation.

Then, on a second pass for symptom 3: is there an AssortmentPublishChange containing those three federatedIds in its detail.deletes, and did the consumer's polling window cover that publish?

4 · Tracing it back to Phase 2

Three decisions I reviewed produced this incident:

  • Item B — pricing at Family level → symptom 1, exactly as predicted, in the predicted words ("we set them last week and they are gone").
  • Item C — item|update with no transition condition → symptom 2, at the predicted volume.
  • Items F/G — polling design with no delete handling → symptom 3.

If I flagged them and was overruled, what changes: almost nothing about what I say now, and everything about what I do afterwards.

What I say today is unchanged: the symptoms, the mechanism, the fix, the timeline. Being able to say "I predicted this" is worth nothing to a merchandising systems manager at 08:15 and it costs the trust I need to get the fix approved this week. Volunteering it would read as blame-shifting even if delivered gently, and it invites everyone to relitigate week four instead of fixing FW27.

What I say to nobody: "I told you so," in any of its forms — including the passive ones ("as we discussed in the design review…"). Not in the incident channel, not to the integrator, not to my own management as a way of pre-allocating blame.

What I do instead: the written record exists — the Phase 2 review is dated and specific. I do not need to invoke it now, and it is there if the account ever needs it. Once FW27 is stable, I raise it deliberately and unemotionally in the next review: "three of the four items from the week-four review produced production incidents in the first three weeks. I would like to change how we handle review findings." That is a process conversation, held at a time when nobody is defending themselves, and it is the only version that actually changes anything.

5 · What I tell them at 09:00, and making Thursday survivable

"Three separate things are happening and I know the mechanism for two of them. Confirmed: last night's load overwrote your colour-level prices with the style price — that is a configuration setting on how price inherits, it is fixable, and your team did nothing wrong. The 900 FlexPLM messages are the same load; noisy but harmless. Separate issue: the three styles still showing are a sync problem, not a load problem, and I am checking that now. Unknown: whether last week's prices are recoverable automatically or need re-entering.

Two things from you: I want to stop tonight's load until the setting is changed, so this does not happen again before Thursday — can you confirm that is acceptable? And can someone send me the list of the colour-level prices they set last week, in case we need to restore them by hand?"

Making Thursday survivable

  • Disable tonight's load. Highest-value action available, needs no root cause confirmation, and prevents a second clobber before the review.
  • Change the property level to Override. The actual fix, and small.
  • Restore the prices — from publish history if available, from the merchandisers' list if not. Prioritise the styles in Thursday's review over the whole set.
  • Remove the three ghost styles from FlexPLM manually and re-baseline the outlet assortment. Bounded, safe, fixes the visible problem while the consumer defect is scheduled.
  • Say nothing about the 900 messages to the wider group beyond "known, harmless, being fixed" — it is the loudest symptom and the least consequential, and it will otherwise consume the meeting.
  • Give the systems manager a three-line brief for Thursday: what was wrong, what is corrected, what is still being fixed. Their standing in that room is what is actually at stake.
How to think about it

This is the phase where the capstone's structure pays off. The incident is not random — it is the predictable consequence of three specific design decisions from Phase 2, and it arrives in week three of go-live because that is when the first full nightly cycle meets real merchandiser edits. Reviews are cheapest before go-live and most valuable after an incident, and this is that sentence made concrete.

Do not let the shared cause absorb the third symptom. Two of three have one mechanism, which makes it tempting to call the whole thing "the load." Symptom 3 has no mechanism in common with the others, and if it gets folded in, it is never investigated — the ghost styles stay in FlexPLM and sourcing keeps costing them. Enumerate, then connect only what evidence connects.

Part 4 is the real examination, and it is a character question wearing a technical costume. You were right in week four and were overruled, and now the thing you predicted has happened in front of the person who overruled you. The instinct to establish that on the record is strong and completely counterproductive: it converts an incident into a blame conversation, at the exact moment you need cooperation to ship the fix.

The discipline is separating the moment from the mechanism. The point of the written Phase 2 review is not vindication — it is that you do not need to say anything today, because the record exists. That lets you be entirely constructive in the incident and still raise it properly a week later, in a process conversation where nobody is defending themselves and something can actually change. The people who say it during the incident get the satisfaction and lose the change.

Containment before diagnosis, again. Disabling tonight's load requires no confirmed root cause and prevents the problem recurring before Thursday — including undoing any manual price restoration, which is the trap in fixing data before stopping the cause.

Manage attention deliberately. The 900 messages are the loudest symptom and the least harmful; the three ghost styles are nearly silent and are costing money downstream. Left alone, a room will spend forty minutes on the noisy one. Explicitly naming it "known, harmless, being fixed" is how you spend the meeting on what matters.

Sources
  • Property Type Handling — symptom 1 — Family copy-down, and why Override is the fix.
  • Creating Workflows — symptom 2 — trigger breadth on item|update and firing once per row.
  • Assortment Publish Integrations — symptom 3 — the delete file and the non-accumulation rule.
  • Week 9 — Localise before theorising — the six checkpoints used in part 1, and the bisect-at-CP3 rule behind the first action.
  • Week 9 — Holding the customer — the confirmed / suspected / unknown split used in the 09:00 message.

Phase 4 · QBR end of first quarter

Ninety days live. Merchandising is fully on Plan. Design still works mostly in their own tools and uploads images at the end. Publish volume is healthy. The workflow failure chart shows a slow rise over six weeks. Two of your original unknowns are still unanswered.

  1. Structure the QBR in five slides — title each and say what's on it.
  2. State the health verdict in one sentence, using the Week 1 value definition rather than usage.
  3. Name the risk you found before they did, and how you present it without alarming the room.
  4. Design lagging: is that a technical problem, an adoption problem, or both? What do you propose?
  5. How do you handle the two unanswered unknowns in front of their exec — having now owed them for a quarter?
The answer

1 · The QBR in five slides

Slide 1 — "Where FW27 was decided"
The quarter in one line: the first full season planned in VibeIQ. Line review dates hit, number of styles taken to review and cut, the handoff to FlexPLM that used to take a week. One number the VP of Merchandising would repeat to their own boss. Not logins.

Slide 2 — "Adoption, honestly"
Merchandising fully on Plan. Design still working in their own tools and uploading at the end. Stated as a fact with a consequence attached, not as a complaint: creative context is entering VibeIQ late, which is the specific value they bought that they are not yet getting.

Slide 3 — "Operational health"
The workflow failure trend over 90 days, with the rise visible. Load success rate and volume. Publish volume. One line: the platform is doing the work; one indicator is moving the wrong way and here is what we are doing about it.

Slide 4 — "Risks and what we are doing"
Three items, each with an owner and a date: the failure trend, the design lag, and the two unanswered unknowns. This is the slide the QBR exists for.

Slide 5 — "Next quarter"
Three commitments — one from me, one from Meridian, one joint. Specific and few. Ending on a shared plan rather than a summary.

2 · The health verdict, in one sentence

"Meridian is healthy on the thing they bought — FW27 was decided in one place, on time, and reached FlexPLM without a week of re-keying — and the risk to that is not usage, it is that design is still outside the workspace, so the reasoning behind decisions is still being reconstructed rather than captured."

Using the Week 1 value definition rather than usage: the platform exists so the line is decided in one place with the reasoning preserved. Merchandising logging in daily is not the outcome; a decided line with its context intact is.

3 · The risk I found before they did

The workflow failure rate has been rising for six weeks. Nobody has reported it, because each individual failure is invisible — a task fails, later tasks in that process never run, and the effect is a style that quietly did not sync. It is currently noise; the trend says it will be an incident during FW28 planning, when volume roughly doubles.

How I present it without alarming the room:

"One thing I want to flag while it is still small. Workflow failures have been trending up for about six weeks — they are not causing visible problems yet, and nothing on your side needs to change today. I am raising it now because the pattern is the kind that stays invisible until volume rises, and FW28 planning roughly doubles the load. I would rather find the cause in a quiet week than during your line review. I have time booked with [engineer] on Thursday and I will bring you a cause and a fix at the next monthly."

Three things make that land: it is quantified and bounded ("not causing visible problems yet"), it asks nothing of them, and it arrives with a date and an owner. A risk presented without a plan is just anxiety transferred to the customer.

Two facts to have ready if asked: workflow evidence lives 90 days, so this window is closing — investigating in FW28 would mean the early weeks of the trend are already deleted. And the org's concurrency ceiling is a shared budget, which is one plausible cause worth checking against what else runs at that hour.

4 · Design lagging — technical, adoption, or both?

Both, and the sequence matters: it is an adoption problem caused by a technical gap. Designers are not resisting the tool; the tool does not yet fit the start of their workflow. They work in Adobe and 3D tools, and Bynder holds the assets — so "work in VibeIQ" currently means "stop what you are doing and re-upload." Uploading at the end is the rational response to that.

Treating it as pure adoption produces training nobody needs. Treating it as pure technical produces an integration nobody asked for. What I propose:

  • Ask three designers what happens in their first two hours on a new concept — before proposing anything. Sit with them; do not survey.
  • Look hard at Bynder. If assets flow automatically from the DAM into Boards, most of the manual upload disappears and the cost of participating drops to near zero. This is the likeliest single fix and it was in the original system landscape from Phase 1.
  • Find one moment worth showing up for. Adoption follows a meeting, not a feature — if concept review happens in Board and the decision is captured there, attendance follows.
  • Name an owner on their side. A design lead who wants this, not a mandate from merchandising — which is what makes it their initiative rather than a system being imposed on them.
  • Measure the outcome, not the activity: is creative context present at line review, or still being explained verbally?

5 · The two unanswered unknowns, in front of their exec

"Two things I owe you from the start of the quarter and still do not have definitive answers on: [the end-user permission model and division-level scoping], and [data residency options]. I am not going to dress that up — I have chased both, I have them with named owners internally, and I have not got you answers in ninety days. That is on me, not on you chasing me.

Here is what I am doing differently: both are now on the standing agenda for our monthly with a date against them, and you will see them on that slide every month until they close, whether or not there is news. If either becomes a blocker for something you are planning, tell me and I will escalate it commercially rather than technically — that is a faster path and I should have offered it sooner."

What makes this survivable: own it in one sentence without over-apologising, then move immediately to mechanism. What loses the room is either minimising ("still working on those") or an extended apology — both signal that the pattern will continue. Offering the commercial escalation path is the part that shows you understand the customer's position rather than your own process.

What I would not do: hide them. They are on slide 4, in writing, with the same treatment as every other risk. An exec who discovers a ninety-day-old open item that was not on the risk slide stops believing the risk slide — and that slide is the only reason the QBR is worth holding.

How to think about it

The failure mode of a QBR is presenting activity nobody acts on. Login counts, publishes, tickets closed. A QBR answers three questions — is this working, what is at risk, what do we do next — and every slide should serve one. Slide 4 is the point of the meeting; the other four exist to make it credible.

Report health against the value definition, not usage. "Merchandising uses it daily" is compatible with the platform failing at its purpose. The Week 1 definition — the line decided in one place with the reasoning preserved — is what lets you say a customer is healthy and name the real risk in the same sentence, which is a far stronger position than either alone.

Bringing a risk they have not seen is the whole basis of being a technical reference. The rising failure trend is invisible from their side and visible from yours, and surfacing it while it is still small is the single habit that separates a technical reference from a well-liked contact. It also has to be done in a way that does not read as alarm — quantified, bounded, no ask, with a date.

The 90-day horizon converts "we should look into that" into a deadline. Evidence for the early weeks of the trend is already being deleted. That is worth saying out loud, because it is the difference between a risk that can be investigated and one that becomes permanently unexplainable — and it is exactly the argument for the monthly snapshot habit.

Diagnose adoption problems as workflow problems first. "Design is lagging" invites a training answer, which fails, because designers are behaving rationally given a tool that does not fit the start of their process. Asking what happens in their first two hours — before proposing anything — is the move; the Bynder integration was sitting in the Phase 1 landscape the whole time.

The unknowns question is the hardest thing in the capstone and it is not technical. You have owed a customer answers for a quarter, in front of their exec. Minimising destroys credibility; over-apologising signals it will recur. Own it in one sentence, then spend the rest on mechanism — a standing agenda item with a date, visible every month until it closes. And offering the commercial escalation route is the detail that shows you are optimising for their outcome rather than for your own comfort.

Putting them on the risk slide is the real test. Everything in you wants to leave a ninety-day-old failure off a slide the exec is reading. But a risk slide that omits your own misses is not a risk slide, and the moment they discover one, nothing else on it is believed.

Sources
  • Concept 4 — QBRs: reporting risk, not activity — the three questions a QBR must answer, and which signal answers each.
  • Week 1 — The problem VibeIQ exists to solve — the value definition the health verdict is stated against.
  • Event Workflows Dashboard & Workflow Artifacts — the 90-day failure trend on slide 3, and the retention horizon that makes it urgent.
  • TAM practice — KPIs and indicators — outcome vs. practice vs. leading indicators, and the anti-metrics that keep slide 1 off login counts.
  • Week 8 — Answering what you don't know — the standing-unknowns discipline behind part 5.

The program's final outcome — you're ready when you can…

  • Explain what VibeIQ is, and isn't, to a VP and to an engineer, in their own registers.
  • Name the entity layer and property level behind any data symptom before investigating.
  • Read a workflow definition and predict its behaviour on create, on update and under bulk load.
  • Design or review an integration that survives downtime, duplicates and missed deletes.
  • Route any request to the right rung of the spectrum and price its cost of ownership.
  • Localise a failure to a checkpoint, run the ladder, and escalate with what you ruled out.
  • Answer a security questionnaire without bluffing, and know what you must take away.
  • Tell a customer's exec whether this is working — in terms of decisions and context, not usage.

After Week 10

Two pieces of unfinished business the program deliberately leaves open, because neither can be closed from public documentation:

Close your standing unknowns

The colleague questions page is not a reading list — it is a work item. In your first weeks, get documented answers to: end-user roles and how Type Policies map to them · numeric rate limits and SLAs · data residency beyond us-east-1 · compliance certifications · customer-facing alerting · rule set enforcement on API/Loader writes · inbound webhook verification · the real escalation path and PS/customer boundary · what's GA vs. beta vs. roadmap in the AI layer. Every one you close makes you faster and shortens a deal cycle. Track them with owners and dates.

Keep the kit alive

The eight artefacts you built — health check, publish review, pre-load checklist, integration design review, workflow review, routing guide, security response kit, triage card — are worth more than the modules that produced them, because they're what you actually use. Update them as you learn what the docs don't say. The Key findings page is the same idea: when you discover a new silent failure or an irreversible decision, it belongs there.

Module 10 · capstone. Synthesises Weeks 1–9; introduces no new platform facts by design.

Practice · beyond the curriculum

Building the TAM practice

Weeks 1–10 build the knowledge. This page is how the job is run — written because the role is not defined at VibeIQ yet, which makes the definition itself the first deliverable. Two layers in one document: the charter and the KPIs are written to be sent upward; everything between them is the weekly operating detail. Built for one post-implementation account with a live synchronisation and performance escalation, with an explicit path from that to a repeatable practice.

On this page

  2. 🤝The service catalogue

  4. 🤝The escalation you inherit
  5. 🤝The improvement pipeline
  6. 🤝Growth, technically led
  7. 🤝Relationship & technical reference
