  11. 🤝From one account to a practice
  12. 🤝What the practice needs

The charter on one screen

The role, in one sentence. The TAM is accountable for whether the customer's VibeIQ implementation keeps producing the outcome it was bought for: decisions get made in VibeIQ, and the context behind them survives downstream.

Four accountabilities. Technical health of the account · ownership of escalations · the improvement pipeline · being the technical reference, to the customer and internally.

The line. TAM owns configuration, data model, integration design, adoption and the technical relationship. Support owns reproducible defects; Engineering owns confirmed ones; Product owns capability gaps; PS owns build work; CSM owns the commercial relationship.

Six headline numbers. Account technical health score · escalation rate · P1 recurrence rate · time to first meaningful update · improvement-request cycle time · expansion signals sourced. Definitions, sources and targets in section 9.

1 — Charter: what the role owns

An undefined role is defined by whoever asks first. The single most valuable thing to produce in the first month is not a fix — it is a written statement of what this job is, circulated and agreed, so that every subsequent escalation is not a negotiation about whose problem it is.

The definition below is deliberately anchored to the Week 1 value statement rather than to a list of activities. Activities change; the outcome the customer bought does not. It also makes the job measurable: are decisions being made in VibeIQ, and is context surviving downstream? is a question you can answer with evidence the platform actually produces.

The four accountabilities

  1. Technical health of the account. You know the state of the implementation, in writing, before the customer tells you about it. This is the difference between a TAM and a very responsive support engineer.
  2. Escalation ownership. Not resolution — ownership. Localise the problem, run the communication, close it with a mechanism rather than a status change.
  3. The improvement pipeline. Requests come in constantly and informally. You give them a queue the customer can see, a classification that decides who does the work, and a route out.
  4. Technical reference. The person both sides call first. Internally, you are the voice of this account's actual reality — which is a real deliverable, not a personality trait.

The boundary — a proposal, not a claim

VibeIQ's real internal routing is not documented publicly and is not yet confirmed. Present this table as what you propose and ask for it to be corrected. Getting it corrected is the point; being right about it first is not.

FunctionOwnsHandoff trigger
TAMConfiguration, data model, integration design, adoption, the technical relationship—
SupportReproducible defects, break/fix with a ticketReproduced on demand, and no design question remains
EngineeringConfirmed defects, data corruption, platform behaviourSupport confirms it, or corruption is suspected
ProductCapability gaps — "no trigger exists for that"The requirement is real and no configuration reaches it
Professional ServicesBuild work: loaders, workflows, extensionsScope exceeds advice; someone has to write it
CSM / AECommercial relationship, renewal, ceilings, marketplace approvalMoney, contract, or a limit that needs raising

Say what is not in scope, early

This is the fastest way an undefined role gets eaten. Not in scope: building and owning the customer's integrations · acting as their level-one helpdesk · owning the commercial relationship · approving their internal releases · being on call for code you did not write. Each of these will be asked for, in a friendly way, within the first quarter. Declining them later is much harder than declining them in the charter.

2 — The service catalogue

A practice is a set of named, repeatable deliverables — not availability. Naming them lets you say "that is a design review, here is what it involves and when you get it" instead of absorbing an unbounded ask. Every one of these already has an artefact produced in Weeks 1–10; reuse those rather than inventing new formats.

ServiceTriggerOutputTurnaround
Account technical health checkQuarterly, or on inheriting an accountRanked written report5 working days
Architecture & design reviewAny new integration, workflow or extensionRanked findings, blockers separated from follow-ups3 working days
Escalation managementP1 or P2 raisedLocalisation, update rhythm, post-incident noteFirst update ≤ 4 hours
Improvement-request triageContinuousClassified, ranked, visible backlogWeekly grooming
Integration performance reviewMonthly while the escalation is openLag, volume and failure trendMonthly
Enablement sessionMonthlyOne topic, taught on their dataMonthly
QBR technical sectionQuarterlyRisk story, plus one risk they did not knowQuarterly
Release-readiness noteEach VibeIQ releaseWhat changes for this customer specificallyPer release

The last one is easy to skip and disproportionately valuable: a customer who hears about a platform change from you rather than discovering it treats you as the technical reference by default.

3 — Operating cadence

RhythmWhat happensWhy
Daily — 20 minutesWorkflow failure dashboard, load statuses, open escalationsDegradation shows here days before a ticket
WeeklyCustomer technical sync (30 min: escalations, backlog, one risk) · backlog grooming · internal routing reviewA standing slot means bad news arrives early and small
MonthlyHealth metrics pack · improvement review with the customer · product advocacy packet for anything blocked on a capability gapMakes the queue visible, which is most of what customers want
QuarterlyHealth check refresh · QBR · charter reviewThe role is new — revise it against what the quarter actually demanded

The weekly sync agenda is fixed on purpose. A meeting whose agenda is "how are things going" degrades into status; one whose agenda is escalations, backlog, one risk produces a decision every week.

4 — The escalation you inherit

A live synchronisation and performance escalation is the situation you are walking into, so it deserves method rather than energy. Week 9 applies directly and its first rule is the one people break under pressure: the first question is not "why is this happening?" but "which segment of the pipeline is this in?" Localisation is cheap; theorising is expensive and, when you are new, expensive in credibility too.

Localise first, and bisect at CP3

Six checkpoints — source, loader, entities, workflows and formulas, publish, consumer. Start at the entities (CP3): if the value is correct there, the problem is downstream; if it is wrong, it is upstream. One look halves the search space, and you can do it before you understand the customer's business.

Separate three words the customer uses interchangeably

They sayIt meansLook at
SlowThe operation takes a long timeShared capacity, concurrency ceilings, batch size, "everything slow" as a symptom shape
LateIt arrives, eventuallyPolling window and interval, queue depth, serialization via message grouping
LossyIt never arrives at allThe integration seam, unprocessed deletes, silent skips, filters

Different causes, different owners, different fixes — and "the sync is slow" is used for all three. Ask which one they mean before anything else. Very often the honest answer is "two of them, and we have been treating it as one problem."

The synchronisation checks worth running first

These are the ones that fail silently, which is exactly why a post-implementation escalation reaches a TAM rather than being caught by an alert:

  • Event-driven or polled? Polling has a seam. The fix is overlap, idempotency and data-derived timestamps — not "last successful run time".
  • Did a publish actually happen? Is there an AssortmentPublishChange at all?
  • shouldSkipAssortmentPublish on the loader config bypasses the publish workflow entirely. The load succeeds, and downstream never hears about it.
  • partialAssortmentUpdate defaults to false, which means replace. A partial file wipes everything not in it.
  • Are deletes processed by the consumer? Publish deletes do not accumulate across publishes — miss one and it is gone.
  • Gaps in change IDs mean missed publishes. The recovery is a re-baseline, and baseline links expire in 24 hours (refetching refreshes them).
  • Intermittent, load-correlated, different records each time is a concurrency race. messageGroupId / dynamicMessageGroupId is the fix.
  • Duplicates point at federatedId generation, and at records created by hand in the UI that never had one.

The highest-yield question in the whole method

"If you retry, does the same record fail?" Same record → logic or configuration. Different record → timing or concurrency. One question, asked in the first five minutes, eliminates half the possibility space — and it is a question the customer can answer without you.

Evidence has a lifetime

Process List, the Workflows Dashboard and Workflow Artifacts retain 90 days. Baseline links expire in 24 hours. Establish what still exists before promising root cause on a problem that started months ago — and if the trail is gone, say that plainly rather than producing a theory that cannot be tested.

The communication protocol

How an escalation is run matters more to the relationship than how fast it is fixed, and it is the part entirely within your control:

  • State known / suspected / unknown separately, every single time. Blurring them is how trust is lost.
  • Commit to the next update time, never a resolution time.
  • Always give them something to do. A customer with a task is a participant; one without is an audience.
  • Say the business impact back in their terms, so they know you understood what it costs them.
  • Never answer "who's wrong?" Answer "here is what happened and here is what we change."
  • Close with the mechanism, not "fixed", then write a short post-incident note naming the decision that made the incident possible.

That last note is not ceremony. Recurrence is a KPI in section 9, and a post-incident note is the only thing that reliably moves it.

5 — The improvement pipeline

Post-implementation, improvement requests arrive constantly and informally — in meetings, in chat, at the end of unrelated calls. Without a pipeline they become a private mental list that the customer cannot see and you cannot defend.

Intake — five fixed fields

What outcome is wanted · who is blocked · how often it happens · the current workaround and what it costs · what breaks if nothing is done. Refuse to log a request without them; the discipline itself resolves perhaps a fifth of requests, because the answer to "what breaks if we do nothing" is sometimes "nothing".

Classify on the spectrum — which is also the cost ranking

ClassWho does the workCost of ownership
Types / configurationTAM advises, customer admin appliesLowest
Formulas / rule setsTAM advises, customer configuresLow
Event workflowsPS or customer developerMedium — it is a release, not a setting
ExtensionsCustomer or SI developerHigh — hosting, on-call, an owner who may leave
External systemCustomerHighest
Product gapProduct, via an advocacy packetNot solvable here — say so early

The rule: push work down the ladder wherever the requirement allows. A meaningful share of "we need a workflow for this" turns out to be a property level, a formula or a rule set. Every rung you descend removes a permanent maintenance obligation from the customer, and that is a TAM's most under-recognised contribution.

The advocacy packet

What actually makes Product act, in order: the requirement in one sentence · frequency · how many users and accounts are affected · revenue exposure · the workaround and its annual cost · what the customer does if the answer is no. A request without the last two is a wish; with them it is a business case.

6 — Growth, technically led

Account growth is a legitimate part of this role and it does not require selling. It requires noticing — a TAM sees expansion signals months before anyone with a CRM does, and the contribution is passing them on as qualified signal with the technical context attached.

SignalWhat it usually means
Adoption spreading to a team that was not in scopeOrganic expansion — get ahead of it before it is discovered as a licensing problem
New integration seams being asked aboutThey are extending the system of record; scope a review before someone builds it
A second brand, region or season mentioned in passingThe classic expansion path, and a data-model decision that expires
Marketplace or custom app interestInvestment intent — and an irreversible ownership decision about whose org the app lives in
The export-to-Excel habit disappearingThe truest adoption signal there is: they now trust the system
Questions about limits and ceilingsThey are scaling. Answer, and flag capacity early

Counter-signals are worth as much and are your early renewal warning: usage flat while their headcount grew · one power user carrying the whole account · exports rising again.

The credibility that makes growth possible comes from not selling during escalations. Hand signals to CSM with context and let them run the commercial conversation. A TAM who becomes a second account executive stops being told the truth about what is broken, and the truth about what is broken is the entire asset.

7 — Relationship & the technical reference role

  • Keep a people map. Who lags, who is sceptical, who has been burned by a previous system. Adoption problems are almost never distributed evenly, and the weak link is a person, not a feature.
  • Bring one risk you found before they did, every time. This single habit is what separates a technical reference from a well-liked contact, and it is the whole basis of being believed during an incident.
  • Disagree well. Lead with what is right in their design. Argue consequence, not correctness. Give the symptom they will experience, not the principle they are violating. You are not winning the argument — you are getting the change made by someone who still calls you next time.
  • "I'll find out" is a commitment, not a deflection. Track open unknowns with owners and dates, and age them. Saying it and returning within the promised time builds more trust than being right on the spot.

The colleague questions page is the working list for the internal half of this: each answer you close makes you faster, and several of them block KPIs in section 9 outright.

8 — Instrumentation: where the numbers come from

Every KPI in the next section has to come from somewhere real. Where it does not, that is a finding, not a gap to paper over.

SignalSourceCaveat
Load success rate and volumeLoaderProcess — status, failureAreaRetention not documented
Workflow failure trendProcess List / Workflows Dashboard90 days only
Workflow detail on a failuretaskOutputs, Workflow Artifacts90 days; deleted with the process
Publish cadenceAssortmentPublishChangeBaseline links expire in 24h
Integration lagConsumer-side acknowledgement — the customer must instrument itNot visible from inside VibeIQ
Adoption breadth by teamChange history by userManual to assemble
Escalations, updates, recurrenceYour own log plus ticketingDepends entirely on your own discipline
Expansion signalCRM, shared with CSMQuality-gate it or it becomes noise

Not observable today — each blocks a metric

Customer-facing alerting on load, publish and workflow failure · numeric rate limits and SLAs · workflow concurrency utilisation against the 25-per-workflow and 60-per-org ceilings · whether rule sets are enforced on API and Loader writes. Each of these is already a colleague question; attach the KPI it blocks to the question so it is a work item with a consequence, not a curiosity. And note the structural constraint: with a 90-day evidence lifetime, no trend can look further back than a quarter unless you snapshot monthly. Start snapshotting in week one — the history you do not capture is simply gone.

9 — KPIs and indicators

Four tiers, because they answer four different questions. Outcome KPIs are what the business is buying. Practice KPIs say whether the job is being run well. Leading indicators warn you early. Anti-metrics are the ones to argue against being measured on — a newly defined role gets measured on whatever is easiest to count, so naming the wrong metrics is part of the proposal.

9a · Outcome KPIs — quarterly, shareable

KPIDefinitionSourceStarting targetHow it can lie
Account technical health scoreWeighted composite, 0–5 (see 9e)Health check≥ 3.5, improving 0.5 per quarterSelf-scored — publish the criteria or it is an opinion
Escalation rateP1 + P2 raised per quarterTicketingDown quarter on quarterAlso falls when customers give up reporting
P1 recurrence rate% of P1s sharing a root cause with an earlier oneEscalation log0%Requires honest root-cause naming, including your own
Renewal technical riskRed / amber / green with named reasonsTAM judgement + health scoreGreen, with no unnamed ambersOptimism — force a written reason per colour
Expansion sourcedQualified technical signals passed to CSM that became pipelineCRM≥ 2 per quarterRewards volume; gate on quality, not count
Reference-abilityWould they take a prospect call?Ask them directlyYes by quarter 3Measures one relationship, not the account

9b · Practice KPIs — monthly

KPIDefinitionTarget
Time to localiseEscalation opened → checkpoint (CP1–6) named in writing< 1 business day
Time to first meaningful updateNot an acknowledgement — known / suspected / unknown stated≤ 4 hours
Update promises kept% of committed update times actually met100% — the one number that must never slip
Post-incident coverage% of P1/P2 closed with a written mechanism note100%
Improvement cycle timeIntake → classified and routed, measured by class≤ 5 working days to routed
Health-check currency% of accounts with a check less than 90 days old100%
Unknowns closedColleague questions answered and written down≥ 3 per month in the first quarter
Self-sufficiency% of customer technical questions answered without escalating internally≥ 70% by month 3

Time to localise is the distinctive one. It measures the thing the whole Week 9 method exists to produce, it is entirely within your control, and it improves fast with practice — which makes it a good number to be judged on while everything else is still being baselined.

9c · Leading indicators — weekly, ten minutes

Watch the direction, not the value. None of these is a target; each is a prompt to go and look.

IndicatorWatch forWhat it means
Workflow failure count, 90-dayAny upward slopeDegradation, days or weeks before a ticket
Load success rate and volumeA drop, or a step change in volumeA broken feed — or a new season starting
Publish cadenceA gap against their seasonal calendarA team disengaged, or blocked on something
Integration lagWideningApproaching a capacity ceiling
Adoption breadth by teamOne team flat while others growThe weak link that eventually costs the renewal
Export-to-Excel behaviourRising againThey have stopped trusting the system
Open-unknown ageAnything older than 30 daysYour own follow-through failing
Sponsor engagementDeclining attendancePolitical risk — the earliest signal of all

9d · Anti-metrics — argue against these

MetricThe behaviour it produces
Tickets closedVolume over prevention. A genuinely good quarter looks idle
Response time aloneFast acknowledgements with no content in them
Usage minutes / loginsConfuses presence with decisions being made — the Week 1 trap
Hours logged per accountOptimises for busyness rather than risk reduction
CSAT on individual ticketsLargely measures politeness during an outage

9e · The health score, defined

Score each dimension 0–5 against stated criteria, take the weighted mean, report to one decimal.

DimensionWeightA 5 looks likeA 1 looks like
Integration reliability30%No missed publishes, deletes handled, consumer instrumentedSilent losses, no reconciliation
Workflow health25%Flat or zero failures, grouping correct, an owner namedRising failures and nobody sees FAILED
Data-model hygiene20%Stable federatedId, correct property levels, no duplicatesDuplicates and copy-down clobber
Adoption breadth15%Every intended team deciding in-appOne team; exports everywhere
Open risk10%No unaddressed blockersKnown irreversible risks with no owner

Present it as version one

Say out loud that the weighting is a first attempt to be recalibrated after a quarter of real data. A score whose criteria are published and whose limits are admitted survives scrutiny; one presented as objective does not, and losing the score means losing the only quantitative story the practice has.

10 — The first 90 days

Days 1–30 — inherit and stabilise

  1. contrail types getAll — ground truth about the configuration, rather than anyone's memory of it.
  2. Data-model health check: item layers, property levels, federatedId sources.
  3. Workflow inventory plus the 90-day failure chart.
  4. Integration review — and take control of the live escalation: localise it, then set the update rhythm before attempting any fix.
  5. Platform and operations: org topology, credentials, custom apps and where they run, concurrency headroom.
  6. The people map.
  7. Write it up, ranked, and share it. That document is your credibility, and it is worth more than any fix delivered in the same period.

Days 31–60 — define and baseline

Publish the charter internally and ask for the boundary table to be confirmed. Deliver the first ranked health report to the customer. Baseline every KPI — an unbaselined KPI is a slogan, and the baseline is only capturable while the 90-day evidence still exists. Start the weekly indicator review and the monthly snapshot.

Days 61–90 — prove and adjust

Full cadence running. First QBR delivered, containing one risk the customer did not know about. The inherited escalation closed with a mechanism and a written post-incident note. Then revise the charter against what the quarter actually demanded and present the revision — a role defined once and never revisited was defined wrong, and revising it publicly is how the practice earns the right to keep defining itself.

11 — From one account to a practice

What becomes a template. The health check, the review checklists, the escalation communication template, the intake form, the advocacy packet, the KPI pack. The rule is simple: anything done twice gets templated the second time, while the second time is still fresh.

A tiering proposal. Tier 1 — strategic accounts, custom integrations, a named TAM, the full cadence. Tier 2 — standard integrations, pooled coverage, quarterly health check and reactive escalation. Tier 3 — self-serve, no named TAM, playbooks and enablement only. Write entry and exit criteria and review them quarterly, so tiering stays a decision rather than becoming a habit.

When a second TAM is justified

Argue from the KPIs, never from feeling busy: health-check currency below 100% · time to first meaningful update slipping · open unknowns aging past 30 days · Tier-1 account count above the point where the weekly cadence stops fitting in a week. Set that threshold before it is crossed — a threshold agreed in advance is a plan; the same argument made after the fact is a complaint.

12 — What the practice needs

Ranked, because a flat list of twelve asks gets ignored and three blockers get resolved.

  1. Confirmation of the boundary in section 1. Without it, every escalation reopens the question of whose problem it is, in the worst possible moment to be asking.
  2. Visibility: customer-facing failure alerting, and concurrency utilisation against the published ceilings. Two of the KPIs above cannot be measured until these exist.
  3. Decision rights: which configuration changes a TAM may make directly in a customer org, and which require PS or the customer's own change control.

Then, in order: documented rate limits and SLAs · the real internal escalation routing and tooling · what is GA versus roadmap in the AI layer · whether rule sets are enforced on server-side writes. Each is on the colleague questions page; each should carry an owner, a date, and the KPI it unblocks.

Practice page — built on Weeks 1–10, not part of the curriculum. The boundary table and the routing it implies are a proposal pending confirmation against VibeIQ's real internal process. Revise this page at every quarterly charter review.

Reference

Key findings

The non-obvious, high-consequence things pulled out of every module and collected in one place. These are the findings that change a diagnosis, cost money if missed, or fail without producing an error. Use this as revision before a customer conversation, and as a lookup during one.

On this page

  1. 📚1 · Symptom → likely cause
  2. 📚1b · Symptom shape → class of cause
  3. 📚2 · Failures that produce no error
  4. 📚3 · Decisions you cannot undo
  5. 📚4 · Claims to verify before asserting
  6. 📚5 · Numbers worth memorising
  7. 📚6 · The reframes that change how you think

1 · Symptom → likely cause

What the customer actually says, and where to look first. This table is the fastest route from a vague report to a hypothesis.

They reportLook first atWk
"Our data reverted overnight"A Family-level property reloaded and copying down over option-level edits2
"We have two of everything"UI-created records with no federatedId, or a federatedId that changed between loads2 · 4
"The system won't let us do this"Property modelled as Family where it needed Override; or a rule set with editable: false2 · 7
"PLM has styles we already cut"A missed publish — deletes do not accumulate and never replay3
"It works in VibeIQ but not in PLM"Separate the internal Plan→Assortment commit from the downstream sync; they fail independently3
"The load completed but values are blank"Unmatched column names (silently ignored), or the step 4/5/6 transformation interaction4
"The same file loads differently for different people"CLI can't use loaderConfigurationId; compare the config recorded on each LoaderProcess4
"The load emailed 3,000 people at 2am"The load emitted create events; workflows bound to those triggers fired once per row4 · 6
"We load every night but PLM has had nothing for days"shouldSkipAssortmentPublish on the loader config — no publish event is emitted at all3 · 4 · 5
"The assortment is nearly empty after a small update"partialAssortmentUpdate defaults to false — the load replaced the assortment4
"The load just stopped, nothing was applied"Validation is fail-fast. Read failureArea: VALIDATING_DATA means it halted before transforming4
"Some rows never loaded, no error anywhere"assortmentSplit: rows whose fieldToSplitOn value matches no entry are skipped, silently4
"This dropdown is empty for some products"An off-hierarchy parent value — the parent holds something the Option Set Hierarchy doesn't know7
"The workflow ran but only the first thing happened"Tasks are strictly serial: if one fails, none of the subsequent tasks run6
"We can't investigate — the workflow logs are gone"Deleting a workflow destroys its process list, task list and logs immediately, not after 90 days6
"We're missing maybe one record in a few hundred, can't reproduce it"The polling window: exclusive boundaries, in-flight writes, or a locally computed now5
"The event never arrived"A workflow investigation — the delivery mechanism is an Event Workflow. Start at the Process List5 · 6
"Only some of the automation runs"First-match-wins: a broad conditional high in the path list made everything below it unreachable6
"Unrelated automation got slow on Tuesday"The shared per-org concurrency ceiling of 60 — often a bulk load on Tuesday4 · 6
"Wrong values, different ones each re-run"A concurrency race: high parallelWorkerCount with no message grouping6
"The workflow runs but nothing happens"A missing or read-only accessGrant, or config pointing somewhere harmless6
"This value was right last season"A formula derived from reference data — reference updates do not re-run formulas7
"The UI wouldn't let us enter that, so how is it here?"An integration write. Rule sets evaluate client-side (see §4)7
"VibeIQ is down" (but only one panel)A customer-hosted extension iframe, not VibeIQ7
"It tested fine in staging and broke in production"Environment drift — an environment is just another org, and config and apps promote separately7 · 8
"Nobody noticed the nightly load failed for a week"There is no documented proactive alerting; every monitoring surface must be looked at8
"The app stopped working after we did nothing"A runtime deprecation — check nodeRuntime in the manifest8

1b · Symptom shape → class of cause

Before looking at content, look at distribution. How a problem spreads across records and time narrows the cause faster than any detail about the records themselves. Wk 9

ShapeClass of causeGo straight to
Intermittent, load-correlated, different records each timeConcurrency raceparallelWorkerCount and message grouping
Consistent, silent, every record of a kindConfigurationProperty level, formula slug, rule set, mapping
Part of a batch but not the restCreate vs. update divergenceConditionals using oldData; which rows were new
Started on a date, "nothing changed"A deploy, a config promotion, or an expiryApp version, types loadAll, runtime deprecation
Sporadic missing records, unreproducibleIntegration seamPolling window construction; missed events
Correct at write, wrong laterRecalculation or copy-downFormula timing; a Family reload
Only some users see itPermission or client-side rulesType / Type Property Policies; rule set criteria
Everything slow, nothing brokenShared capacityThe 60-process org ceiling; what else ran

The highest-yield question in troubleshooting

"If you retry, does the same record fail?" Same record → logic or configuration. Different record → timing or concurrency. Two words of answer eliminate half the possibility space, and customers never volunteer it. Wk 9

2 · Failures that produce no error

The dangerous class. Nothing is logged, every status says success, and the damage surfaces weeks later — usually in front of someone senior.

Data model & loading

  • Unmatched CSV columns are ignored. "If the column does not match a key or label on the specified entity type, the row column will be ignored." No warning. Wk 4
  • Step 4 transforms silently. Type transformations run twice — step 4 without warnings so conditional columns can work on clean values, step 6 with them. A value altered in step 4 and overwritten in step 5 never warns. Wk 4
  • No setting nominates the ID column. "There is no configuration to say 'this column is my Federated ID'." Only Custom Entity excepted. Values must arrive in the expected column, or be mapped there. Wk 4
  • Changing federatedIds creates duplicates. "It is critical to ensure that federatedId values in your load files do not change between loads." Nothing errors; you simply get two. Wk 2 · 4
  • Copy-down overwrites hand edits. A Family property reload persists onto every Option. Wk 2
  • shouldSkipAssortmentPublish makes a load invisible downstream. It bypasses the publish workflow, skipping change history, composite assortment updates and the assortment|publish event. The load succeeds, VibeIQ is right, and every consumer stays stale with nothing reporting a problem. Usually set for a legitimate backfill and then left in a reusable config forever. Wk 4
  • partialAssortmentUpdate defaults to false, which means replace. A file containing only the rows that changed leaves the assortment containing only those rows. Reports success. Wk 4
  • A computed federatedId inherits someone else\'s formatting. When a conditionalColumn derives the ID (e.g. "{familyNumber}.split(\'-\')[0]"), a change to that source field\'s format silently changes the upsert key for every row. Wk 4

Publish & integrations

  • Deletes never replay. "Delete data does NOT accumulate across publishes." A consumer down for one publish has permanently missed those deletes. Only re-baselining recovers. Wk 3
  • Polling loses records at the seam. Temporal filtering is not a cursor. Exclusive boundaries drop same-instant writes; in-flight writes commit after the query but timestamp before the cutoff; clock skew skips windows. Wk 5
  • A 404 is not proof of absence. It may mean access denied or wrong entity type. Wk 5

Workflows & configuration

  • Unreachable paths. "Only the first path whose conditional evaluates to true is executed." A broad conditional high in the list makes everything below it dead code. The process still reports success. Wk 6
  • oldData is absent on creates. So {newData.x} !== {oldData.x} behaves differently for new vs. existing rows in the same load. Wk 6
  • Misspelled formula slugs return undefined. "Verify every slug against the live Type before saving a formula." Wk 7
  • Reference data changes don't re-run formulas. "Updates to Reference Data does not cause Formulas to re-run." Correct when written, stale forever after. Wk 7
  • A failed task silently skips the rest. "Only one task runs at a time in a process, and tasks always run in order. If one task fails, none of the subsequent tasks are run." Later actions are never run rather than failed — so never infer they executed from the process existing. Wk 6
  • Deleting a workflow destroys its evidence instantly. "Once deleted, the process list, task list, and logs for a workflow is lost." Disable instead. Wk 6
  • Unmatched assortmentSplit rows are skipped. A new category value or a trailing space in the split column, and those rows quietly never load while the job succeeds. Wk 4
  • An off-hierarchy parent value empties a child dropdown. "If the parent holds a value that isn't in the hierarchy, the child dropdown will appear empty" — usually data loaded past a hierarchy that was never told about it. Wk 4 · 7
  • A slug-based Option Set Hierarchy config "silently fails to load." It is keyed on internal TypePropertyIds, not slugs. Hand-edited readable JSON produces no error and no hierarchy. Wk 7

Platform & operations

  • Environments drift with nothing to tell you. An environment is just another org, and configuration (types loadAll) and apps (publish/install) promote by different mechanisms. Nothing reconciles them. Wk 7 · 8
  • Monitoring is not alerting. Every surface — the workflow dashboard, the process list, LoaderProcess — is something a human must choose to look at. A failed nightly load is discovered by whoever notices missing data. Wk 8
  • Concurrency contention is invisible. The 60-process org budget is shared across every workflow, integration and load, with no documented way for a customer to see their own utilisation. Wk 6 · 8
  • Workflow artifacts vanish with the log. "The artifact will be automatically deleted when the process expires from the log (90 days)." Evidence and attachments die together. Wk 8

3 · Decisions you cannot undo

Raise these before they are made. Nobody thanks you at the time; everyone would have thanked you later.

DecisionWhy it's permanentAsk
App ownership"The org that first creates an app is the owner… App ownership can not be modified." An SI's app can never become the customer's."Whose org is this being created in, and is that where it should live in three years?"
App identifierReserved globally, cannot be altered. A rebuild can't reuse the name."Is this the name we want permanently?"
Property levelChanging Family↔Override↔All rewrites how existing values propagate."Which properties are Family, which Override, which All?" — on day one of any account.
federatedId sourceChange it and you get duplicates and orphans, silently."What generates this value, and what could ever cause it to change?"
Workflow identifierThe stable key enabling cross-org migration. Treat as immutable.Set it deliberately at authoring time.
Deleting a workflowTakes its process list, task list and logs with it, immediately. Disabling preserves the record and the trigger."Does anyone need to explain something this workflow did in the last 90 days?"
Who holds cached credentialsCLI auth caches to ~/.vibeiq/configs/ on every laptop that ran it. Access outlives the engagement unless revoked."Who has run the CLI against this org, and what is our revocation step at offboarding?"

4 · Claims to verify before asserting

Say "let me confirm" — not yes, and not no

Three things where the documentation is suggestive but not conclusive. Getting these wrong in front of a customer costs credibility that is expensive to rebuild.

  • Rule set enforcement. Rule sets "evaluate client-side, affecting editor experience for all users." That implies the API and Loader are not bound by them — the working assumption, since assuming enforcement is the more dangerous error. But confirm the exact boundary internally; it decides how validation must be designed for every integrated field. Wk 7
  • Inbound webhook verification. X-Verify-Signature is documented as optional and shown as a shared secret in a header, not an HMAC over the body, while the webhook ID in the URL acts as a bearer credential. Outbound does support HMAC. Keep the directions apart and confirm what is enforced server-side. Wk 5 · 6
  • AI capabilities. Heavily marketed, thinly documented. Named native agents are a public claim, not a verified capability set. Confirm GA vs. beta vs. roadmap before repeating any of it. Wk 1 · 3
  • Customer-facing alerting. Whether customers get proactive alerts on load, publish or workflow failure is undocumented. If the answer is no, recommend they build monitoring on the process APIs at implementation time, not after an incident. Wk 8
  • End-user roles. Type Policies and Type Property Policies exist, but how they map to named roles or groups inside Boards / Plan / Showcase is not public. Never improvise an answer to "can we restrict merchandisers to their own division?" — the shape of that answer determines their rollout design. Wk 8

A correction to make, not repeat

"VibeIQ has no real end-user permission model" is a common first impression — this program's own Week 1 open questions made it — and it is wrong. Type Policies ("access control statements defining READ, CREATE, UPDATE, DELETE permissions") and Type Property Policies ("property-level access restrictions") exist, and are first-class enough to be among the eleven categories Configuration Copy exports. What is missing is documentation, not capability. Say "there is a policy-based model with type- and property-level permissions; I'll get you the specifics." Wk 8

And one figure never to cite

The "15 requests/hour" rate limit circulating online belongs to vibe.co — a different company. VibeIQ's real limits are undisclosed: "rate-limited to ensure system stability," with higher limits available by contacting support. You cannot promise a throughput number, so insist consumers implement exponential backoff instead. Wk 1 · 5

5 · Numbers worth memorising

ValueWhat it governsConsequence
24 hoursS3 download link expiry on a publish changePersist the change ID, never the URL. Re-fetching refreshes the links.
90 daysWorkflow log retention — "permanently deleted"A hard evidence horizon. Check it before promising root cause on an old problem.
1–30 (default 5)parallelWorkerCountRaising it without message grouping converts slowness into corruption.
25Concurrent processes per event workflowAdjustable via the CSM.
60Concurrent processes per organizationShared. One chatty workflow starves every other integration in the org. No documented utilisation view.
90 daysEvent Workflows Dashboard history, and workflow artifact lifetimeThe failure-trend chart is the closest thing to a health metric. Put it in every QBR.

6 · The reframes that change how you think

Ten sentences worth carrying

  1. VibeIQ displaces Miro, Excel and PowerPoint — not PLM. The strongest answer to "isn't this overlap?", because it names what the customer is actually doing today. Wk 1
  2. Publish is not "send to PLM." It is first an internal commit from Plan to Assortment. A publish can succeed while the downstream sync fails — two investigations, two owners. Wk 3
  3. A load is an event storm — unless publish is skipped. Phase 5 upserts, so every bound workflow fires once per row; but shouldSkipAssortmentPublish suppresses assortment|publish specifically, so entity workflows still fire while the outbound integration stays silent. Wk 4
  4. Loads are all-or-nothing. Validation is fail-fast and no partial loads occur — so a rejected file needs fixing and rerunning, not reconciling, and a "completed" load really did apply everything it accepted. Wk 4
  5. The integration is a workflow. Publish events are delivered by an Event Workflow, so integration debugging and workflow debugging are one skill. Wk 5 · 6
  6. The data model is in the payload. The baseline exposes .item, .item.itemFamily and .projectItem — so downstream mapping is a data-model decision, not plumbing. Wk 2 · 5
  7. The valuable answer isn't yes or no — it's "that's possible, and here's what it costs to own." A rule set is a Tuesday afternoon. An extension is a hosted service with an on-call rota. Wk 7
  8. Localise before theorising, and bisect rather than walk. Ask "which segment is this in?" before "why is this happening?" — then check the entity itself first, because it splits the pipeline in half. Wk 9
  9. Never answer "who's wrong?" Answer "here's what happened, and here's what we change." Adjudicating blame between a customer's teams costs the neutrality that makes you useful to both. Wk 3 · 9
  10. "I don't know, I'll find out by Thursday" is a strong answer — when it is specific and it is kept. Bluffing a compliance answer is unrecoverable: security teams verify, and being wrong once means everything else you say gets checked. Wk 8

Collected across Modules 1–9. Updated as later modules are written. Every claim here is sourced from official Contrail documentation or explicitly marked as unverified in §4.

Priority matrix

The 20% that delivers 80% is everything marked Critical. Master those and you'll be effective fast.

On this page

  1. 📚Critical must know
  2. 📚Important
  3. 📚Useful
  4. 📚Nice to know

Critical must know

  • Core data model: Item / ProjectItem / AssortmentItem and property levels
  • Project (season) / Assortment structure
  • Plan → publish → AssortmentPublishChange → downstream lifecycle
  • "Sits alongside, doesn't replace PLM/ERP" positioning
  • Integration patterns: event-driven vs polling, and when to use each
  • Config-vs-customization boundary
  • Escalation routing (TAM / Support / Engineering / Product / PS)
  • Technical discovery methodology

Important

  • The Loader pipeline and its failure modes
  • AssortmentPublishChange internals (baseline, deletes, detail, 24h S3 expiry)
  • Event Workflows (triggers/conditionals/actions), internal vs external events
  • Auth, rate limits, error codes, webhook security
  • Formulas, validation functions, rule sets, blueprints
  • End-user apps (Boards, Plan, Showcase, Admin Console)
  • Troubleshooting methodology anchored to the pipeline
  • AI capabilities: shipped vs roadmap

Useful

  • SDK/CLI mechanics; app manifest, marketplace, lifecycle
  • Extensions (Doc Automation / Contextual Action / Admin Utility)
  • Custom entities, option set hierarchy, size range templates
  • Configuration copy, uniqueness; transformers in depth

Nice to know

  • Deep SDK internals & app-framework decorators
  • messageGroupId / parallelWorkerCount concurrency tuning
  • Node runtime management; Java SDK specifics; CLI CI/CD automation

Reference

Questions for VibeIQ colleagues

The public docs are strong on the developer layer but thin on operations. Treat this as your onboarding interview guide — confirm answers as you learn them.

Environments & tenancy

Do customers get separate dev/staging/prod orgs? How is config promoted between them?

Permissions & RBAC

What is the end-user role/permission model inside Boards/Plan/Showcase? (Docs cover app-level access grants + API-key auth only.)

SLAs, rate limits & scale

What are the actual numeric API rate limits and documented SLAs? Note: the "15 requests/hour" figure online belongs to vibe.co — a different company — not VibeIQ. Don't cite it.

Monitoring (customer-facing)

What monitoring/alerting do customers get for load, publish, and workflow failures? What's the TAM's visibility into a customer's operational health?

The AI layer (biggest gap)

What's actually GA vs beta vs roadmap? How are agents configured, governed, monitored? Nail this down before making AI claims.

Rule set enforcement boundary (raised in Week 7)

Rule sets are documented as evaluating client-side. Are they enforced on the API and Loader write paths, or are they purely an editor guardrail? This changes how validation must be designed for any integrated field — and whether "the UI wouldn't allow this" is ever evidence about how a value got there.

Webhook verification (raised in Week 6)

Workflow webhooks document X-Verify-Signature as optional, and the example compares a shared secret rather than an HMAC over the request body — while the webhook ID in the trigger URL acts as a bearer credential. What verification is actually enforced server-side? Is request signing available? Needed before any customer security review.

Workflow concurrency ceilings

Defaults are 25 concurrent processes per workflow and 60 per org, "adjustable via Customer Success Manager." What is the real process for raising them, what are the hard maximums, and can a customer see their own utilisation before they hit it?

Support model & escalation

Real internal escalation path & tooling? Where's the TAM/Support/PS/Engineering/Product line in practice? Who owns integration builds — PS or customer?

Data residency & compliance

File uploads route through AWS S3 (us-east-1 in examples). Residency options? Compliance certs (SOC 2, etc.) you can represent to a security team?
